### 1. ★★★☆☆ Deeply divided Israel braces for pivotal election

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T22:11:13+00:00
- **发生了什么**: After three years of regional war, this is a battle Israel always knew was coming, writes Lucy Williamson.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Figure caption, Watch: Why Israel&#x27;s election is one of the most important in its history
By Lucy Williamson Reporting from Tel Aviv, Jerusalem, Ramle and Sderot
After three years of regional war, this is a battle Israel always knew was coming.
The general election on Tuesday hangs over a country that&#x27;s deeply divided and ready for a fight.
Polls published on Friday put the governing coalition and the main opposition bloc almost neck-and-neck, with half a million first-time voters due to cast their ballot and some 70,000 Israelis reportedly flying in from abroad specifically to vote.
On Saturday night, vast crowds of opposition voters gathered in central Tel Aviv to demand a change of government.
"We&#x27;re optimistic," 21-year-old Omri told me. "I want peace, I want security, and I want to end corruption and get the religious fanatics out."
This campaign has been energized by new tactics - including AI videos designed to grab the headlines - and also by a new face.
Gadi Eisenkot is, perhaps, not the man you might expect to take on the flashy, aggressive, media-savvy brand of Israel&#x27;s longest-serving prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu.
How do Israel&#x27;s elections work and who are the main contenders?
Israel&#x27;s far right find fertile ground among first-time voters
The bitter row over military service that could shape Israel&#x27;s future
Relatively new to politics, with a working-class Moroccan background, Eisenkot is a former military chief of staff who is renowned for his calm manner and low-drama approach. He&#x27;s been dubbed by some as a "non-politician".
But he and his centrist Yashar party have regularly pipped Netanyahu and his Likud party to the top spot in recent polls, the last of which was published on Friday, in line with Israeli election law.
Israeli politics is the art of coalition-building, rather than the task of any one party winning power, but with the predicted result too close to call, Eisenkot&#x27;s rising profile has sharpened a race many Israelis already see as existential for the future of their country.
I watched him slip past the cameras at a community centre for Ethiopian Jews in the city of Ramle last week, through a gaggle of security personnel and advisors.
One of those who had come to hear him speak was David, a smartly dressed man in a freshly-pressed shirt and navy jacket, who said he had met Eisenkot before.
"He looks you in the eye, not like a leader looking down at someone," David told me. "And he listens. He&#x27;s very sympathetic."
Image caption, Eisenkot&#x27;s image as a "non-politician" has proved popular
Eisenkot&#x27;s appeal is often put down to his personal manner and profile. A softly-spoken military man whose son was killed during the Gaza War, he has presented himself as coming "from the periphery", not from Israel&#x27;s wealthy urban elite.
His Moroccan background became election headlines earlier this month when a pro-Netanyahu speaker used a racist slur to refer to Eisenkot during a TV debate, referring to him as a Moroccan "monkey".
"These elections are about many issues," Eisenkot told the crowd gathered at the Ramle community centre. "They are about security, economy, education...but primarily, they are about the identity of the State of Israel, and what kind of Israel we want to see. A Jewish state, a democratic state, a liberal, tolerant, egalitarian state."
This is music to opposition ears, who say Benjamin Netanyahu&#x27;s government is a threat to Israel&#x27;s democracy, pointing to his efforts to curb the powers of the judicial system, and his refusal to establish a state commission of inquiry into the 7 October 2023 attacks on the grounds that the country&#x27;s Supreme Court would be biased against him.
Eisenkot, along with other opposition leaders, has attempted to draw voters away from Netanyahu&#x27;s right-wing Likud party by promising such a state inquiry, which a recent survey suggests is supported by more than 70% of Jewish Israelis.
Image caption, Protesters gathered in Tel Aviv on Saturday to demand a state commission of enquiry into the October 7 attacks
He has also vowed to enforce court rulings on drafting ultra-Orthodox Jews into national military service - another measure that is popular with right-wing voters and opposition alike.
Benjamin Netanyahu has hit back with a campaign focused on Israel&#x27;s security, and a core campaign slogan of "No Surrender".
He has repeatedly cast his rivals as soft on security, playing on the fears of some right-wing voters that Jewish opposition parties might ally with Israel&#x27;s Arab parties to secure a majority, and suggesting this could compromise national security.
Around one-fifth of Israel&#x27;s population is Arab - many of whom describe themselves as Palestinian citizens of Israel.
These are the first elections since the 7 October attacks, in which Hamas-led gunmen stormed through Gaza&#x27;s border fence, killing almost 1,200 people and taking 251 hostage.
It was Israel&#x27;s worst security failure, but three years on its prime minister is again running for re-election as the only man who can keep Israel safe.
"If the army had woken him in time, it wouldn&#x27;t have happened," Shlomi Basher told me, one of several die-hard Netanyahu fans drinking in a basement cafÃ© in Jerusalem&#x27;s Mahane Yehuda market. "He does bear a certain responsibility, but after the 7 October, he changed the entire Middle East."
Netanyahu has been hammering home the message that Israel&#x27;s wars in Gaza, Lebanon and Iran, in the years after the 7 October attacks, have reduced existential threats to the country.
"We need him to be re-elected so that he can finish this work," said Shlomi. "In my opinion, he is one of the best leaders in the world. Most of the world loves him and respects him, because he is a strong man and his ideas are correct."
Image caption, Shlomi Basher is a long-time Netanyahu supporter
The prime minister has traditionally relied on a stable base of right-wing voters, but is projected to see a thinning of support in this election. Some voters may be slipping away to opposition parties like Eisenkot&#x27;s. Others, including some die-hard Likud voters we spoke to, said the 7 October attacks had pushed them towards parties further on the right.
Lt Col (Res) Eyal Hajbi, head of the regional civil defence unit in the Netanyahu stronghold of Sderot, right on the Gaza border, lost several friends and colleagues in the attacks. He lights candles for them every day in his office, and told me he would be thinking about them when he cast his ballot on Tuesday.
Standing on a ridge overlooking the ruins of northern Gaza, he gestured to the wasteland where Israel&#x27;s army has completely flattened the neighbourhoods of Beit Lahia and Beit Hanoun.
"As far as I&#x27;m concerned, after 7 October, we should wipe them all out," he said. "I&#x27;ve changed my understanding of Gaza. Before, I wouldn&#x27;t have said we should destroy it."
He told me he and his family had always voted Likud, but that now he was voting for the far-right party of Itamar Ben Gvir, Jewish Power.
"My family has become more extreme than it ever was before," he said. "If once we deliberated over it, today we don&#x27;t think twice; we vote."
Polls suggest that less than 10% of Israelis are undecided and that very few of those are considering switching between government parties and opposition ones.
Ben Gvir, National Security minister in Netanyahu&#x27;s coalition, has been reaching out to younger voters through headline-grabbing social media campaigns.
A poll for one of Israel&#x27;s leading newspapers, Yedioth Ahronoth, published on 9 October, suggested that more than 16% of first-time voters in Israel would choose far-right parties like his.
In Sderot, a group of 18 year olds from a nearby Jewish religious college told me their political views had been affected by the 7 October attacks far more than their parents. All four teenagers were planning to vote for another far-right party, Religious Zionism, led by Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich.
"Today, the whole younger generation understands that peaceful paths will not work," Aviad Elmahach, one of the teenagers, told me. "Older adults, who are set in a fixed mindset, find [it] harder to change, but the youth understand that we need to be here with strength and an iron fist, which is the only way to win."
The lesson of 7 October, he said, was that Israel needed to be as forceful as possible in its relations with the Palestinians, more than 74,000 of whom have been killed by Israel in Gaza since then, according to the Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.
"The youth have a fresh new perspective that has not yet become entrenched in politics, and they understand very well why we&#x27;re voting for Smotrich - it means holding the land with strength and giving [the Palestinians] nothing through peaceful gestures."
Image caption, Israel&#x27;s military campaign in Gaza has left much of the territory in ruins
A little further south along the Gaza border are the kibbutzim that were overrun by gunmen during the Hamas attacks. They were renowned for decades as bastions of the Israeli left.
Among them is Nir Oz, where 47 people were killed and 76 taken hostage that day.
Three years on, residents are moving back. Twenty-six-year-old Gal Pauker has opened a Friday wine cafÃ© at the edge of his small vineyard next to the Gaza border - a few wooden tables set out under sheets of beige canvas to shade customers from the strong desert sun. His grandfather Gideon was killed during the 7 October attacks.
I asked Gal, and his mother Galia, whether the attacks had shifted their political views.
Image caption, Nir Oz was one of the worst hit kibbutzim on 7 October
"We need a change - we can&#x27;t live with this government anymore," Galia said. "And I&#x27;m not talking about security, I&#x27;m talking first about humanity."
Having voted for centrist parties in the past, Galia says she&#x27;s decided to vote for Eisenkot this time around, because "he&#x27;s an ordinary guy and I hope he&#x27;ll continue the way he&#x27;s talking".
Her son Gal said he was deciding between Eisenkot and the left-leaning Democrats party of Yair Golan, another former army general.
"I&#x27;m not naÃ¯ve. I know there are a lot of more militant voices from young generations," he said. "I don&#x27;t know if Eisenkot will be better than Golan in terms of security, but I think we have bigger problems: economics, social problems, education, health. We didn&#x27;t get new neighbours on October 7 - it was a major event, but didn&#x27;t change how we see ourselves."
Threats to security versus threats to democracy - these are old political tramlines for Israeli voters, deepened by the 7 October attacks, and by four years of the most right-wing government in Israel&#x27;s history.
But on some of the biggest international concerns, the differences between government and opposition are not as wide as it might seem.
Gadi Eisenkot has pledged to end Hamas rule of Gaza within 18 months, either through a US-led peace plan, or through "military and operational capabilities".
All the main opposition leaders have voiced support for some form of Israeli settlement in the occupied West Bank, which has expanded massively under the current government and is illegal under international law. None have openly committed to the idea of full Palestinian statehood, something Benjamin Netanyahu has vowed to block. Eisenkot has spoken about creating the conditions for "autonomy or state-minus" under ultimate Israeli security control.
Tuesday&#x27;s vote could well end in deadlock, with neither government nor opposition able to form a majority without the support of Arab parties, which polls project could win enough seats to be kingmakers. All but one of Israel&#x27;s Jewish opposition parties have so far rejected working with either of the two Arab party blocs.
With Benjamin Netanyahu ramping up warnings of fresh threats from Iran, opposition voices warning of potential disruption to the ballot, and Israeli rights activists warning of a further spike in violence by settlers in the occupied West Bank, political instability at home could leave Israelis facing both a fresh test of their democracy, and fresh security threats.

How Arab parties could hold critical balance of power in Israeli election
The bitter row over military service that could shape Israel&#x27;s future

### 2. ★★★☆☆ Russia to host first Olympic federation world event since Ukraine invasion

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T14:34:33+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The International Weightlifting Federation becomes the first Olympic federation to choose Russia to host a world championships since its invasion of Ukraine.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Weightlifting was first included as an Olympic sport in the 1896 Games in Athens
Moscow will host the 2030 World Weightlifting Championships - the first time an Olympic federation has chosen to stage a global event in Russia since the country&#x27;s full-scale invasion of Ukraine began in 2022.
The International Weightlifting Federation (IWF) announced its decision on Sunday, four months after confirming Russian athletes would again be eligible to compete in its events.
In July, the International Olympic Committee (IOC) provisionally lifted a suspension on the Russian Olympic Committee.
That suspension had been imposed following the country&#x27;s invasion of Ukraine.
The IOC&#x27;s subsequent decision potentially opened the way for Russian athletes to compete for their country at the 2028 Olympic Games in Los Angeles.
The IWF announced the host cities of four world championships at junior, youth and senior levels, awarding the 2029 senior event to Ahmedabad in India.
"All the bids we received were excellent and we are sure that the championships in your respective countries will be a huge success," said IWF general secretary Jose Quinones.
No world championships in an Olympic sport, summer or winter, have been held in Russia since the start of the war in Ukraine.
The 2025 World Aquatics Championships, initially awarded to the Russian city of Kazan, were instead moved to Singapore.
Moscow has not held the World Weightlifting Championships since 1983.
The Russian Weightlifting Federation said: "[We are] the first Olympic sports federation to succeed in bringing a top-level official competition back to the country in many years."
Federation president Aleksandr Vinokurov thanked the IWF in a post on Telegram "for their trust", with the championships also serving as a qualifying event for the 2032 Olympic Games in Brisbane, Australia.
"Russia has all the necessary experience and capabilities to host competitions of the highest level," he said.
"Moscow has repeatedly staged major international sporting events and has modern infrastructure."
In announcing its decision in July to provisionally lift Russia&#x27;s suspension, the IOC said that no decision had been taken on whether Russia could display their flag, colours and anthem at the Olympic âGames.
The IOC said it would continue to "not organise IOC events in Russia or invite Russian government or state officials to its events".
Some Russian athletes competed at the 2024 Paris Olympic Games and this year&#x27;s Winter Olympics in Milan as neutrals.
Ukraine filed an appeal to the Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) requesting that the IOC decision to lift its suspension of the Russian Olympic Committee be annulled.
The IOC&#x27;s move decision came after the International Paralympic Committee had already ended Russia&#x27;s exile from global sports events, with the country&#x27;s athletes allowed to compete under their national flag at the Winter Paralympics.
Five Ukrainian athletes are listed to compete in the 2026 IWF World Championships in Ningbo, China, which are scheduled to take place from 27 October to 8 November.

### 3. ★★★☆☆ Rapper Nicki Minaj joins Trump onstage at Michigan rally

- **分类**: 🇺🇸 美国
- **来源**: BBC World / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T23:33:19+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The Super Bass singer is the latest big name to campaign in Michigan, where a tight Senate race is playing out ahead of the 3 November midterms.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Figure caption, Moment Nicki Minaj joins Trump on stage at Michigan rally
Rapper Nicki Minaj said US President Donald Trump has "changed the energy of the country" as she joined him at a Michigan rally to stump for Republicans ahead of the nationwide midterm elections.
"He&#x27;s given us a future to look forward to," she said, after Trump praised her figure and called her on stage to speak.
Minaj has called herself Trump&#x27;s "number one fan" despite criticising some of his policies in his first term.
In Michigan, Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed is locked in a tight race with Republican Mike Rogers with just over a week to go before the midterms. Former President Barack Obama held a rally - one of the first in this cycle - with the Democrat on Saturday.
Democrats are also aggressively hitting the campaign trail, with close elections taking place on 3 November which will decide which party controls Congress for the rest of Trump&#x27;s presidency.
In his remarks on Sunday, Trump focused on El-Sayed&#x27;s Muslim faith and background, alleging that he will "invite jihadists" into America if he wins.
El-Sayed has criticised similar remarks by Trump as Islamophobic, saying in an ad to voters last week that the US president "thinks he can use my name and my faith to distract us from the suffering his policies are inflicting on us at the pump, at the grocery store, and at the doctor&#x27;s office".
In Port Huron on Sunday, Minaj, wearing a gold-coloured Gucci-branded suit, led the crowd in chanting Trump&#x27;s name, and the names of Republican candidates in Michigan.
"We really need all these great people to be elected, because this is a major turning point for our country," she said.
"She never looked more beautiful," Trump said as she left the stage. "She gets it. She&#x27;s smart," he added.
Earlier in his speech, Trump praised the rear end of one male Republican candidate before turning the crowd&#x27;s attention to Minaj.
"By the way, I looked at a beautiful ass before," he segued. "Nicki Minaj. That&#x27;s hard to miss."
He then asked her to "stand up" as the crowd cheered, and added that he hoped she was not "insulted" by his remark.
The Super Bass singer had accompanied Trump to the state aboard Air Force One. She filmed a video with an aide - which was posted to X -on the president&#x27;s plane, pretending to be interviewing for the White House press secretary role.
The position is currently open after Katie Zacharia left five days after a shaky public debut.
The Trinidad and Tobago-born rapper has thrown her star power behind Trump a few times before, including on his allegations that Christians face persecution in Nigeria and his "gold card" visa, which offers applicants residency and a path to US citizenship.

### 4. ★★★☆☆ Do Palestinians in Gaza expect anything to change after Israel’s election?

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T08:43:39+00:00
- **发生了什么**: After years of war, displacement and restrictions, Gazans ask whether Israel’s election will affect their future.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Do Palestinians in Gaza expect anything to change after Israel’s election? on social media
Palestinians in Gaza are watching Israel’s election amid uncertainty over continued displacement, restrictions and the prospects for rebuilding their lives [Abdelhakim Abu Riash/Al Jazeera]
Deir el-Balah, Gaza Strip — After three years of genocidal war, Palestinians in Gaza are watching the upcoming elections in Israel with a mixture of fear and uncertainty.
Although the territory has been under a “ceasefire” since October 2025, Palestinians in Gaza continue to endure almost daily Israeli air strikes, dire living conditions, and a punishing siege that has caused shortages of basic goods.
list of 2 items list 1 of 2 Israeli elections: What are the possible outcomes?
list 2 of 2 Gaza content creator tells Al Jazeera how much her award win means
Israelis will head to the polls on Tuesday to vote for their next government, in an election that has been shaped by the war on Gaza. Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu has been strongly criticised for his handling of security before the October 7, 2023, Hamas-led attacks on Israel. Members of his far-right government have called for a tougher line on Gaza, including the expulsion of its population.
The people of Gaza know this election will shape their future and many have been closely watching the campaign.
Mohammed Eliyan, 36, was displaced from Jabalia refugee camp in northern Gaza and now lives in Deir el-Balah with his five children. He hopes a change in leadership in Israel could bring relief to Palestinians who have endured repeated displacement and war.
“We follow the Israeli election to see who will win in the next phase. We hope there might be some easing for the Palestinian cause and for us, who have been displaced again,” he told Al Jazeera.
Mohammed Eliyan, displaced from Jabalia refugee camp in northern Gaza, hopes Israel’s election will bring change and relief to Palestinians [Abdelhakim Abu Riash/Al Jazeera] Eliyan hopes that Gadi Eisenkot, the former Israeli military commander and Netanyahu’s main rival, will defeat the prime minister. He sees Netanyahu as more hardline and fears that if his Likud party and far-right allies win another term, it would mean more war and further displacement for Gaza’s people.
“We hope there will be changes after the election, and that someone other than Benjamin Netanyahu will win. What Netanyahu has done to the Palestinian people, before and after October 7, has not been a small thing,” he said.
But regardless of who wins, Eliyan does not expect a major shift in Israeli policy.
“They are still our enemy, and I don’t expect things to improve dramatically,” he said. “But we hope someone else might be less harsh than Netanyahu.”
He called on Israel’s next leader to end the war, prevent displacement and starvation, and ease restrictions at Gaza’s crossings.
“We want whoever leads Israel not to be hardline. End the war and let us live in peace,” he said.
Islam Salem, displaced from Beit Lahiya in northern Gaza, is uncertain whether Israel’s election will bring meaningful change for Palestinians [Abdelhakim Abu Riash/Al Jazeera] Islam Salem, 41, is displaced from Beit Lahiya in northern Gaza and has three daughters. He is following Israel’s election closely and believes the result could affect Gaza’s future and the lives of his children, likely for the worse.
“Politics is our livelihood. It is connected to everything in our lives, our living conditions and even the future of our children,” he said.
But he also believes that elections primarily serve Israeli interests, so even if there are new faces in the next government, the Gaza strategy will likely remain the same.
“Israeli policy will not change in any way. Whatever they take, they consider it theirs and a political gain for them, the gains of a war they have pursued for a long time,” Islam told Al Jazeera.
He pointed to Israel’s continued control of about 65 percent of Gaza’s territory inside the “Yellow Line”. He also doubts whether reconstruction will begin soon in Gaza, where more than 80 percent of buildings have been damaged, and about two-thirds of those have been completely destroyed.
“I think reconstruction will be difficult. They do not want to give others any rights. They want their rights in full, and even more,” he said.
Years of war have left Salem struggling to imagine a future beyond his harsh daily reality.
“There is no future left for us to be afraid of. We live day by day. We have no idea what will happen next, and everything we feared has already happened,” he said.
Asked what he would say to Israel’s next prime minister, he spoke of how many in Gaza are feeling.
“If only a simple person could come and talk to him and say, ‘Leave us alone. Give us some breathing space and let us breathe.’ But he would not be convinced. What can I say to him?” he said.
Hanan Abu al-Maaza, faces an uncertain future in Gaza as Palestinians consider what Israel’s election could mean for their lives [Abdelhakim Abu Riash/Al Jazeera] Hanan Abu al-Maaza, 57, has been displaced more than 12 times during the war on Gaza. She fears Israel’s next government could bring more suffering to its people in the besieged enclave.
“We follow the election because of the fear and anxiety we are living with. We have been displaced more than 12 times, and we are afraid they will do even worse to us than before,” she said.
“We go to sleep without feeling safe, wondering what they might do to us next. The fear is still in our heads and hearts.”
Abu al-Maaza says if Netanyahu is defeated, there will be more chance that she can return to her home in Beit Lahiya.
“I hope someone else wins, so we can have peace of mind and feel safe, and return to our homes. That is what we wish for,” she said.
After years of destruction in Gaza, she hopes Israel’s next leader will show Palestinians more mercy.
“Maybe he will say that there is nothing left in Gaza, that they have done enough and should let people live. There is hardly anything left in Gaza for anyone to think about doing anything more,” she said.
Abu al-Maaza urged Israel’s next leader to end the war and stop the suffering in the territory.
“We have had enough of war and destruction. There is nothing left to destroy, yet the suffering continues,” she said.
Manar Owaida, displaced from northern Gaza, follows Israel’s election closely amid uncertainty over what its outcome could mean for Palestinians [Abdelhakim Abu Riash/Al Jazeera] Manar Owaida, 23, is originally from northern Gaza and is now displaced in the Tal al-Hawa neighbourhood of Gaza City. She is also keeping an eye on the Israeli election and considering its potential impact.
“I follow the election to know what they are saying and planning, and to understand what is happening around us and how the next Israeli government might affect the situation in Gaza,” she said.
Owaida hopes Gadi Eisenkot will replace Netanyahu, and sees the current prime minister as more extreme than his challenger. But she does not believe a change in Israel’s leadership would transform Palestinian lives, arguing that government policies have remained consistent for decades.
“Israel’s policy is one of crime, killing and destruction. Since 1948, it has been the same policy, the same killing and the same blockade,” she said.
Still, she hopes a new government could ease restrictions and killings and withdraw from areas beyond the “Yellow Line”, allowing life to gradually return to Gaza.
“There might be a change in Israeli policy if someone comes in place of Netanyahu and eases the blockade and the killing we are living through,” she said.
“There could be a withdrawal from the Yellow Line and life could gradually return to Gaza. But we are waiting with fear and uncertainty, alongside a little hope.”
Her greatest concern is that Netanyahu could launch a wider regional war to avoid domestic political pressure around the time of the vote.
Owaida said she wants Israel’s next prime minister to allow Palestinians to live without fear of further violence.
“We want to live in freedom and peace, away from killing and wars,” she said.
Mousa Ali, a father of four, now lives in Gaza City after being displaced from Beit Lahiya in northern Gaza [Abdelhakim Abu Riash/Al Jazeera] Mousa Ali, 33, was displaced from Beit Lahiya in northern Gaza and is now in Gaza City with his four children.
He believes that the outcome of the election could determine whether displaced Palestinians can return to areas beyond the “Yellow Line”.
“For Palestinians, this election is critical, especially because of the Yellow Line. We hope the opposition will be better when it comes to allowing displaced people to return to their areas,” he said.
Ali mentioned Gadi Eisenkot as a possible alternative and hopes Arab parties will perform strongly so they have a role in the next government.
But he does not expect a new leader to bring major changes for the people of Gaza and fears that Israeli attacks will continue. He says a Netanyahu victory would prolong displacement and control over areas around the “Yellow Line”.
“Every day in Gaza, there is blood, there are people being killed, and there is bombing everywhere,” he said. “We hope this policy will end and that a new prime minister will stop this ongoing bloodshed.”

Burnham said: ‘Looking back at Brexit … I don’t think it has played out as people said it would at the time.’ Photograph: House of Commons/PA
Brexit deal is not good enough for British public, says Andy Burnham
EU exit has damaged economy and failed to bring promised benefits, says PM, but he rules out big pre-election changes
Brexit has not “played out” like it was promised to the public, has weakened the economy and the current deal is not good enough, Andy Burnham has said, while ruling out any major changes in the current parliament.
The prime minister said Britain had ended up with a “harder Brexit than was proposed in the referendum campaign” and it had not helped to control the small boats crisis. Backing the home secretary’s tougher immigration rules , he said Brexit had weakened the UK’s ability to control its borders as it could no longer send people back to Europe.
When asked directly on Monday whether he wanted Britain to rejoin the EU, Burnham told Good Morning Britain: “I’ve said I’m going to be honest and say, in my lifetime I would like to see that happen, but I am not going to move ahead of the public. I am not going to dictate in terms of my own opinions here, you have to make sure that the British public are the bosses.”
Explaining why he even brought up the possibility of “going all the way” in terms of closer relations with the EU, Burnham said: “Looking back at Brexit … I don’t think it has played out as people said it would at the time. We got a harder Brexit than was proposed in the referendum campaign.
“It’s weakened our economy. It’s also weakened our control of immigration, and that might surprise people because that was the big issue, but the small boats crisis has risen since and we have a different form of immigration now.”
He added: “I am not going to say at the moment that the deal we’ve got is good enough for Britain. I don’t think it is good enough.”
Burnham said he would not “move away from the red lines in Labour’s manifesto”, adding: “I’m not proposing rejoining [the EU] in this parliament. We’re way, way away from that at this particular point in time. What I am saying is we need to take some practical steps to make the arrangement work better.”
There are concerns among Labour MPs who represent leave-voting constituencies in the north of the England and the Midlands that reopening the Brexit debate could create opportunities for Reform UK.
Asked about the fact that 65% of his constituents in Makerfield voted to leave the EU, the prime minister said: “I said we wouldn’t rerun the referendum and that’s not what I’m doing. The British public, the people of Makerfield, will be the bosses here. They will decide what happens in relation to Brexit … What I can do though is make practical steps to make the arrangements work better.”
Emphasising the negative impact Brexit had had on Britain’s control of immigration, the Labour leader said: “I think we’ve got weaker control now. The reason the small boats crisis has arisen is because we can’t return people, and people know we can’t do that in the same way that we used to be able to.”
Burnham said he hoped the UK-EU summit on 20 November could yield a deal “which would be good for British food manufacturers, because they’ve been hit hard by Brexit”.
He said he backed “the principle” of the home secretary’s controversial plan to make foreign workers wait 15 years to get permanent residency.
Burnham said Shabana Mahmood was right to pursue the plans based on the principle that “settlement is a right to be earned not just something that is granted automatically”.
He also ruled out calling an early general election, despite polling suggesting Labour was still enjoying a “Burnham bounce” . “There is no election coming anytime soon. People may as well put that out of their mind.”
On Wednesday’s budget, Burnham said it would offer people and businesses “breathing space”, claiming it would give Britain “a little bit of light at the end of the tunnel”.

### 6. ★★★☆☆ How war, security and coalition math are shaping the Israeli election

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T09:46:59+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Israeli voters will head to the polls to cast their votes in a general election on Tuesday.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Israel election 2026: War, security and coalition math
Israel votes Tuesday in its first election since the Oct. 7, 2023, Hamas attacks and since the U.S.-Israeli war with Iran began in February.
Recent polls show neither Benjamin Netanyahu’s bloc nor the main opposition bloc reaching the 61 seats needed for a Knesset majority, leaving smaller and Arab parties potentially pivotal.
Security, the wars in Gaza and Iran, ultra-Orthodox military conscription and the economic cost of prolonged conflict are among the issues shaping the vote.
Israeli voters will head to the polls Tuesday in an election with implications beyond the country’s borders.
It is Israel’s first election since Hamas’ Oct. 7, 2023, attacks, which killed around 1,200 people and saw more than 250 abducted. The Palestinian Ministry of Health says Israel’s subsequent military campaign in Gaza has killed more than 74,500 people . It is also the first vote since the U.S. and Israel began their joint war against Iran in February.
Israelis vote for parties rather than directly electing a prime minister, with the Knesset’s 120 seats allocated through proportional representation. Parties must win at least 3.25% of the national vote to enter parliament.
Because outright majorities are rare, parties typically form coalitions. The president asks a Knesset member — usually the leader best placed to assemble a 61-seat majority — to form a government. If repeated attempts fail, another election can be called.
Israel&#x27;s Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu (C) waves to his supporters during a Likud Party meet-and-greet held as part of the campaign ahead of Israel&#x27;s legislative election, at King Solomon Hotel in Netanya on October 22, 2026. (Photo by JACK GUEZ / AFP via Getty Images)
Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu , who leads the right-wing Likud party, returned to power in late 2022 with a coalition including far-right and ultra-Orthodox parties. The alliance has since been strained by disputes over a Gaza ceasefire and the conscription of ultra-Orthodox men.
Recent polls put both Netanyahu&#x27;s bloc and the main opposition bloc, led by centrist former military chief Gadi Eisenkot, short of the 61 seats needed to govern, making smaller parties potentially decisive.
Arab parties are also in the running, but most of the main Jewish parties have ruled out alliances with them. More than 20% of Israel&#x27;s population is Arab, but Arab parties have rarely joined governing coalitions.
The impending vote is effectively a referendum on Netanyahu, with the Hamas attacks having "transformed Israeli society," according to László Csicsmann, a Middle East expert at Corvinus University of Budapest in Hungary.
"Israel has been at war since October 7, 2023, and the so-called &#x27;seven-front war&#x27; launched by Netanyahu has undoubtedly worn down Israeli society," he told CNBC. "The side that can better convince voters that it can protect the population is likely to win. Other campaign issues include mandatory military service, which is particularly sensitive for religious Jewish parties and Haredi communities."
Military service is compulsory for most Jewish, Druze and Circassian Israelis over 18, while most other Arab Israelis are exempt. The conscription of ultra-Orthodox men remains a major legal and political dispute.
The economic toll of the war is also a major issue, Csicsmann said.
"Due to the war and high military spending, economic issues are also dominating the political campaign," he told CNBC, noting that Israel currently spends around 8% of its gross domestic product on military expenditure. "In addition, nearly every family has a soldier or reservist who has been called up and has left their job for months."
A Netanyahu defeat would make Iran war harder for U.S., says analyst
Defense and security are also at the forefront of the election, said Daniel Schatz, a political scientist and former visiting scholar at Harvard, Stanford and Georgetown.
"For Israel, the stakes include restoring confidence in government and deciding how military power translates into lasting security," he told CNBC in an email. "For its neighbors and international partners, the question is whether the next government can turn battlefield achievements into workable political arrangements."
Schatz said that while Netanyahu&#x27;s supporters see the incumbent prime minister as "an experienced leader confronting formidable threats," his critics see failures of preparedness, accountability and strategic direction.
In September, book extracts published by Israeli newspaper Haaretz alleged that Netanyahu had been warned that Hamas was planning a major offensive around a week before the Oct. 7 attacks. Netanyahu called those claims an "absolute lie," and has reportedly filed a defamation lawsuit against the newspaper.
But Schatz told CNBC that any lingering dissatisfaction with Netanyahu should not be confused with support for the left.
"Some voters want different leadership precisely because they believe it would deliver security more effectively," he said.
All U.S. bombers leave UK air base on concerns of suspected terror plots; more tankers struck near Iran in the Middle East

Why the confusion around the Iran situation could get worse. How to profit anyway

Trump tells CNBC he expects U.S. to make &#x27;great deal&#x27; with Iran
He added that a change in prime minister and the governing coalition would also be unlikely to produce a dramatic shift in Israel&#x27;s stance on Palestinian statehood, Iran and other foreign policy issues.
Netanyahu&#x27;s right-wing Likud party is running on a manifesto promising "security without compromise," along with pledges to maintain a free economy and financial markets and a bid to strengthen "Israel as a regional and technological power."
Eisenkot&#x27;s Yashar party says it wants to make Israel a "democratic, powerful, progressive and secure" country, and it also has a focus on national security – including a proposed policy of integrating ultra-Orthodox and Arab communities into compulsory military service.
Farzan Sabet, senior analyst at the Eurasia Group think tank, told CNBC the main blocs competing for power are "quite hawkish on security" and would have comparable policies across key areas like confronting Iran and Hezbollah in Lebanon.
But he added that a new coalition government composed mainly of anti-Netanyahu bloc parties would "probably prefer to review the Israeli strategy towards Tehran" before pursuing any major escalation.
The U.S. and Israel have reportedly drawn up plans for possible renewed strikes on Iran after the midterm elections on Nov. 3, while Iran&#x27;s army chief has said it is ready for "asymmetric warfare."
Eurasia&#x27;s Sabet added that the anti-Netanyahu bloc might be "more conciliatory on some issues," such as military action in Gaza, especially if doing so would improve U.S. ties and Israel&#x27;s international standing while still meeting the country&#x27;s basic security needs.
He said a government composed mostly of the anti-Netanyahu bloc would likely take fewer actions in the West Bank, crack down on settler violence against Palestinians there, and avoid annexation of Palestinian land.
The West Bank is a Palestinian territory west of the Jordan River where Israeli settlements have been expanding for decades. In recent years, violence and forced displacement of Palestinians by settlers have drawn condemnation from lawmakers in the U.S. and beyond .
But Sabet told CNBC: "While the opposition to Netanyahu has the edge in the latest Israeli election polls, this will be a tight race, with several possible outcomes."

### 7. ★★★☆☆ Israel’s election: Five key things to know

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T05:16:38+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Vote comes as Israel is embroiled in multiple wars, faces international criticism and grapples with domestic divisions.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Israel’s election: Five key things to know on social media
People walk past an election billboard showing Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu with the Hebrew phrase 'We will not surrender' in Tel Aviv, Israel, October 6 [Abir Sultan/EPA]
Israelis will head to the polls on Tuesday for legislative elections.
The vote, the first since the Hamas-led attacks on Israel on October 7, 2023 and the subsequent genocidal war against Palestinians in the Gaza Strip, is being called one of the most important in its history .
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 US support for Israel continues three years into the genocide in Gaza
list 2 of 3 Israel’s deadline to close UK consulate in East Jerusalem nears
list 3 of 3 Protesters in Colombia rally against ties with Israel
It will determine the direction of Israel’s government as the country remains embroiled in multiple years-long conflicts, faces growing international criticism and grapples with deepening polarisation at home.
Here are five things to know about the upcoming election:
Which are the main parties and what do opinion polls suggest?
Any party clearing an electoral threshold of 3.25 percent enters parliament, whose term lasts four years.
There are two main blocs vying to lead the next government.
The first is the pro-Netanyahu coalition, led by Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party and joined by several far-right and ultra-Orthodox parties. These include United Torah Judaism (UTJ) and Shas – both conservative religious parties – and Otzma Yehudi and Religious Zionism-Zehut, led respectively by far-right ministers Itamar Ben-Gvir and Bezalel Smotrich.
The main opposition coalition comprises four Jewish-led opposition movements – the Yashar party, led by former Israeli military chief Gadi Eisenkot, the Democrats, led by former deputy military chief of staff Yair Golan, the Yisrael Beiteinu party, led by former Finance Minister Avigdor Lieberman and the Together alliance, led by former Prime Minister Naftali Bennett and joined by Knesset opposition leader Yair Lapid.
The latest opinion polls published before a ban on new surveys took effect on Friday showed that neither bloc was on course to secure a parliamentary majority of 61 seats.
A poll by public broadcaster KAN projected that the opposition alliance would win 52 of the Knesset’s 120 seats, compared with 50 for Netanyahu’s bloc, with the remaining seats going to unaffiliated parties, including the country’s two main Arab-majority political lists – Ra’am, also called the United Arab List, and the Joint List, an alliance of Hadash, Balad and Ta’al parties.
However, both the pro-Netanyahu and anti-Netanyahu blocs have effectively ruled out joining forces with the Arab-led parties for now.
The fractured landscape means Israeli parties could be in for tough post-election negotiations to cobble together a governing majority. If those efforts fail, Israel could end up with a minority government reliant on support from parties outside the governing coalition – or potentially head to another round of elections.
Israelis are grappling with a series of domestic and international crises this election cycle.
On the domestic front, there is growing demand for accountability for reported security failures, including those leading up to the October 2023 attacks, anger over the growing ultra-Orthodox community’s military exemption and concern over Netanyahu’s repeated efforts to push judicial reforms that critics say undermine Israel’s liberal democratic order.
“The secular and liberal opposition factions in Israel feel this election is the last chance to save Israeli democracy and restore the institutions that have eroded over Netanyahu’s successive terms,” Mairav Zonszein, an Israeli-American commentator who is a senior analyst at the International Crisis Group, told Al Jazeera.
A war-weary public is also weighing how Israel should proceed with its multiple military conflicts, with many voters seeking security and economic stability while remaining resistant to perceived concessions in Gaza, the occupied West Bank and Lebanon.
It is Israel’s first election since the October 2023 attacks and comes amid a surge in far-right attitudes and growing divisions over the direction of the country.
“Strains of Israeli society had always been given to violence and right-wing rhetoric, but the trends that had always been present had accelerated under the current government,” prominent Israeli sociologist Yehouda Shenhav-Shahrabani told Al Jazeera.
The election also comes as Israel’s international standing has deteriorated, with a host of Israel’s allies sanctioning its illegal settlements  in the occupied West Bank, and many other nations denouncing the country’s continued attacks in Gaza and Lebanon.
When Netanyahu addressed the United Nations General Assembly on September 24, dozens of envoys from around the world walked out , some jeering and booing.
The outcome of the upcoming vote could determine whether Israel’s next government maintains its approach to the international community or seeks to repair ties.
What does it mean for Netanyahu’s political future?
Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving prime minister who has led the country for more than 15 of the last 17 years, and a total of 19, faces growing scrutiny at home and abroad over alleged security failures leading up to the October 7 attack, as well as corruption charges he has been battling since 2019.
An electoral defeat would also leave Netanyahu more exposed to his ongoing corruption trial, which could ultimately result in a prison sentence if he is convicted.
A new government could also affect the prospects of a potential commission of inquiry into the October 2023 attacks and Netanyahu’s political standing as he faces an arrest warrant for war crimes issued by the International Criminal Court.
About 7.3 million Israeli citizens are eligible to vote in Tuesday’s election, including Palestinian citizens of Israel, who number about 1.2 million.
Meanwhile, roughly three million adult Palestinians in Gaza, the occupied West Bank and occupied East Jerusalem have no say in the government that controls their borders, movement and legal status.
Recent polling suggested that the turnout among the Palestinian citizens of Israel could reach 60 percent, with the majority of the votes expected to go the Arab lists, which are the principal representatives of Palestinian citizens of Israel. If turnout reaches 65 percent, these parties could potentially win as many as 15 seats in the Knesset. Campaigners are aiming for 70 percent turnout, which would be the highest in decades and raise the threshold, keeping smaller parties out of parliament.
Last month, Israel’s Central Elections Committee voted to bar the Joint List and Ra’am from the election.
Israel’s top court quickly reversed that decision, enabling the Arab parties to stay in the race. However, the court did force Balad leader Sami Abu Shehadeh to withdraw from the race over an article in which he described the October 7 attacks as “an important historical event”.
The role of Arab parties is going to be significant. Both major coalitions have ruled out joining forces with them for now, but without them, neither coalition might be able to form a majority.
Netanyahu has repeatedly sought to portray his opponents as potential partners of the Arab parties, claiming that an opposition-led government will seek their support and that only his Likud party can prevent this.
Joseph Mansour, a former aide to Israeli Knesset member Ayman Odeh, said politically alienating Arab parties is part of Netanyahu’s political strategy, which could allow him to remain in office as a caretaker prime minister if neither coalition forms a majority.
“If Palestinian-led party support becomes politically toxic and the opposition falls short of 61 seats, the deadlock itself becomes the latest win for Netanyahu,” wrote Mansour for the Arab Center Washington DC.

### 8. ★★★☆☆ Israelis have changed. Will they change their leadership?

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: NPR World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T16:00:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Israel is holding a general election this week after years of war. For many Israelis, shaken by the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks and years of war, it is the most consequential in decades.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Israel holds consequential election after years of war Israel is holding a general election this week after years of war. For many Israelis, shaken by the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks and years of war, it is the most consequential in decades.
Israelis have changed. Will they change their leadership?
Israelis have changed. Will they change their leader?
< iframe src="https://www.npr.org/player/embed/g-s1-149563/nx-s1-mx-6005257-1" width="100%" height="290" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="NPR embedded audio player">
A road sign stands in front of a large billboard featuring Israel's Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu (right) of the Likud party and his main center-right opponent, former military chief Gadi Eisenkot, in Ramat Gan, Israel. Israel heads to the polls on Oct. 27.
TEL AVIV, Israel — Israelis are convinced that elections scheduled for Tuesday are the most pivotal in their nation's history.
Will they vote out Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu — and his coalition of far-right, ultranationalist and religious political parties — because he failed to prevent the Hamas attacks on Oct. 7, 2023, the deadliest day for Jews since the Holocaust?
Or will they reelect him to continue his campaign of retaliation, waging wars that aim to reshape the Middle East? His main challenger, former military chief of staff Gadi Eisenkot, leads a center-right opposition promising more diplomacy and less extremism.
It is Israel's first vote after these years of battle and bloodshed. Israelis are so concerned about their country's future that tens of thousands of expats are expected to fly back to cast ballots, since Israel does not allow absentee voting.
"We almost could have been destroyed on Oct. 7," said Israeli lawyer Shoula Romano-Horing, who flew in from Kansas City, Mo., to vote. "This is why I'm voting for Netanyahu, to make Israel so secure and so safe."
In the opposite corner are those Israelis distressed by the global backlash to the wars Netanyahu's government has waged.
"The actions of this government cause Israelis in general and Jews in general to be tagged as, you know, mass killers," said Yochai Ben-Haim, who works in renewable energy in California and is flying home to Israel for 36 hours just to vote. "This government is led by extremists that do not represent who we are, and we need to change it."
In conversations with dozens of Israelis over the last few weeks, a portrait has emerged of who Israelis are today.
A man stands in a destroyed building in the Jabalia camp for Palestinian refugees in the northern Gaza Strip on Oct. 20, 2026.
They are a people defiant, but also weary after three years of incessant war. They are enraged at their enemies, but also at their leaders.
Many are impervious to the pain of their Palestinian neighbors, but traumatized from the war they have wrought. They are resistant to Palestinian independence, but also revolted by the Israeli settlers pushing Palestinians off their land.
They are fed up with the world's rebuke over the Gaza war, but also fearful of their growing global isolation. They are a people in a tug-of-war with themselves.
"Israelis want to be feared and to be loved," said Israeli author Micah Goodman. "The problem is that you have two reputations you need to protect, but you can't."
Meir and Kati Zohar stand together at the memorial marker dedicated to their daughter, Bar Zohar, at the Nova festival memorial site in southern Israel, on Sept. 24, 2026. Their daughter was among the victims killed on Oct. 7, 2023.
As elections near, Israelis are learning more about what led up to Oct. 7, 2023.
3 years on, Israelis grapple with reports Netanyahu was warned before Oct. 7 attacks
Throughout that year, as Israelis poured into the streets to protest Netanyahu's far-right government and its plan to weaken the judiciary, Netanyahu's security chiefs warned that regional enemies could take advantage of the turmoil to attack.
Behind the scenes, Israel received direct warnings that Hamas was planning something big. Israel and Hamas had been holding secret talks for more than a year, Palestinian mediator Sufian Abu Zaida said. When the talks stalled, Hamas' leader in Gaza had Abu Zaida relay a message threatening an "earthquake."
What kind of earthquake was unclear, said Abu Zaida, but he insists he relayed the warning to Israeli intelligence. The leader of the United Arab Emirates and Egypt's intelligence chief relayed similar warnings, according to reporting by NPR and other media outlets . "All what happened in 7 of October is needless," Abu Zaida said.
Burnt out vehicles in Ashkelon, southern Israel, are pictured following a rocket attack from the Gaza Strip on Oct. 7, 2023.
Nearly 1,200 people were killed when Hamas attacked army bases, residential communities and an outdoor music rave — and 251 people were taken hostage back to Gaza. Netanyahu denies he was warned and has never admitted any kind of responsibility for the security failure.
"Within days, his PR people were trying to shape the public narrative about events," said Amos Harel, a defense analyst for the Israeli newspaper Haaretz and author of 06:29 – Anatomy of a Failure , which details the Israeli failures to prevent the Oct. 7 attack. (The attack began that day at 6:29 a.m.) Netanyahu's strategy, he said, was "to shift the blame" to the security services and the military.
Amos Harel, military and defense analyst for the Israeli daily Haaretz, is pictured inside the newspaper's archival library in Tel Aviv. Harel has extensively reported on Israeli defense strategy, military operations, and Middle East security for decades.
For much of the Gaza war, Israeli protesters flooded the streets demanding that Netanyahu strike a deal with Hamas to release Israeli hostages and end the fighting. It would take two years, Israel's longest ever war, before a hostage deal was reached. Netanyahu said he was seeking the total defeat of Hamas, though former officials who worked with Netanyahu allege he prolonged the war for political reasons — a charge that Netanyahu has denied.
"I'm absolutely convinced that this is what happened," Harel said. "He felt that if this would move on too fast, then there will be more of a significant public demand for a national commission of inquiry."
There is a great demand among Israelis for an investigation into the attack, like the 9/11 Commission in the United States. Netanyahu has blocked one in Israel.
So Israelis grappling with Oct. 7 go to the spot of the day's deadliest attack, where hundreds of people were killed when Hamas attacked the Nova music festival. The memorial there is a dusty field of plaques and thin poles bearing the pictures of smiling young people who were partying when they came under attack.
Meir and Kati Zohar hold hands as they pose for a portrait beneath a tree at the Nova music festival memorial site in southern Israel on Sept. 24, 2026. Their daughter, Bar Zohar, was killed during the Hamas-led assault on the festival on Oct. 7, 2023.
Meir Zohar points to documentary photographs at the Nova festival memorial site. The center photograph shows his wounded daughter, Bar Zohar, receiving emergency treatment in the final minutes of her life on Oct. 7, 2023.
A memorial marker displaying the photo and biography of Bar Zohar stands among hundreds of tributes at the Nova music festival site on Sept. 24, 2026. Hundreds were killed at the site during the Hamas-led attack on Oct. 7, 2023.
Meir and Kati Zohar display matching memorial tattoos featuring the birth year and zodiac sign of their daughter, Bar Zohar.
Israeli soldiers visit a memorial commemorating security personnel who died defending those who came under attack at the Nova festival site.
Kati and Meir Zohar, whose 23-year-old daughter Bar was killed there, helped build the memorial with other families. The Zohars say they received no government funding for the effort and no phone call from Netanyahu expressing his condolences.
She believes Netanyahu bears the responsibility for what happened to her daughter on Oct. 7 and, like other Israelis, hopes Netanyahu faces a reckoning for it on election day.
"It's a hope that something will change," Zohar said. "I hope that someone will come and take his place and be the one who [is] trying to keep this country together."
The Oct. 7 attack left Israelis in a state of shock.
"I think the general line of thinking — not only among decision-makers, not only among ministers of the government, but also among commanders in the field — was that something biblical has happened to us and that something biblical needed to happen in return," Harel said.
What happened in return was an Israeli military onslaught that has killed more than 74,000 Palestinians and left most of Gaza destroyed. Today, Israel is fighting back against charges of genocide. Netanyahu faces an international arrest warrant for alleged war crimes. Israeli soldiers who fought in the war risk arrest in some countries.
"I'm proud of what I did and I know what I did," said Lior, 34, who was one of those soldiers. "I don't mind to be in front of a judge."
Lior, 34, an Israeli reservist soldier who commanded a sniper unit during wars in Gaza and Lebanon, poses for a portrait at a farm in Udim, Israel where he plans to launch a horse therapy program for veterans with post traumatic stress disorder.
Lior spoke on the condition that his last name be withheld to protect his family overseas from harassment because of the nature of his role: he commanded an Israeli sniper unit in Gaza and Lebanon, serving over 500 days in uniform.
" When you're inside Gaza, your life is under danger 24/7. There is shooting, there is booming… sometimes you hear about Hezbollah, Hamas, what they are doing. So your body, your mind is under survival tension 24/7," Lior said.
Photos: What a year of ceasefire in Gaza looks like
He is among the nearly 19,000 Israeli soldiers who have been newly diagnosed with post-traumatic stress disorder or related illnesses. It touches every part of his life: He imagines snipers on the streets. He sometimes breaks into tears riding the train. He struggles in his sex life. He and his fiancé broke up.
Lior said he often returns to one memory from Gaza: his order to shoot and kill a man he described as a Hamas terrorist, standing next to his child in a place where he shouldn't have been.
"Unfortunately, yeah, the kid saw his father dying in front of him," Lior said.
He does not regret any of his decisions to open fire.
"To be honest, in my opinion, I don't have any mercy of people in Gaza. I know what's going on there. I know of the poverty, I know about the lack of water, electricity. I don't have any mercy on them," Lior said. "When you see things with your own eyes and you remember what happened on the 7th of October, I'm not forgiving for that."
"You know, my Jewish identity (is) saying that I'm sure there is, but…I don't see it that way. That's what I told you about the kid," Lior said. "If he have the chance, he will kill me."
He is among the nearly 300,000 young Israeli military veterans ordered to leave their civilian lives and serve as reservist soldiers after Oct. 7.
Anat Saragusti, an Israeli journalist who reported from Gaza in the 1990s and early 2000s for Israeli Channel 12 TV, poses for a portrait in her home in Tel Aviv.
More than 67 percent of Israelis in a July 2025 poll said they were not very troubled about the suffering of people in Gaza. More than 60 percent of Israelis in an August 2025 poll said they believed there were no innocent people in Gaza. That view has been reinforced by mainstream Israeli media.
"There are no uninvolved civilians in Gaza," said Israeli reporter Almog Boker early last year on leading news network Channel 12.
Skeletons in their clothing: Recovering bodies from the rubble in Gaza
This was not always the way Israeli media covered Palestinians in Gaza. Anat Saragusti, a veteran journalist, used to report from Gaza in the 1990s and early 2000s for Channel 12.
"I tried to cover Gaza as if I was living in a foreign country, to show the complexity of life, to show different stories," Saragusti said from her Tel Aviv apartment. "To show the Israeli audience that Gaza is not what they thought and think now, you know, a black hole of terrorists."
Twenty years ago, after Hamas took an Israeli soldier captive in Gaza, Israel practically sealed Gaza shut to Israeli journalists and to all Israelis. Then, Hamas took control of Gaza, Israel imposed a blockade, there were years of Hamas rocket fire into Israel and Israeli retaliation.
Tents sheltering people displaced by war stand between the rubble of destroyed buildings in the Jabalia camp for Palestinian refugees in the northern Gaza Strip on Oct. 20, 2026.
Since Oct. 7, most Israeli media chose not to show what Palestinian journalists were reporting day after day from Gaza: the tens of thousands of men, women and children killed; the forced displacement; the starvation; the misery of trying to survive.
"The majority of the Israeli journalists didn't do one story, you know, not even one story was done on Gaza… to see whether what these Palestinian journalists are claiming is true," said Saragusti. "They were afraid of the pushback by the audience if they dare say something about Gaza."
Amos Harel, defense analyst for the Israeli daily Haaretz, stands inside the newspaper's archival library in Tel Aviv.
Harel, who writes a regular column for the Haaretz newspaper, says the nation's continuous war trauma also afflicted Israeli media.
" We were so busy with our pain and our suffering and worrying about the fate of the hostages — hundreds of hostages were held at that time by Hamas — that we didn't focus on what we did to the other side," he said.
Rubble and the remains of demolished Palestinian homes and agricultural structures lie scattered across a hillside in Masafer Yatta, in the Israeli-occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026.
There is one place where even veterans of Israel's military and security establishment say what Palestinians are enduring has gone too far: the Israeli-occupied West Bank.
Since Oct. 7, Israeli settler violence toward Palestinians has skyrocketed . Settlers have seized more and more land there, with the support of the military and Netanyahu's pro-settler government, which has approved more than 100 new Jewish settlements in the West Bank, a record number.
Retired Israeli defense and government officials toured Palestinian communities affected by Israeli settler violence in the occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026. "This is evil," said Ari Shuali (second from left), a former senior Israeli intelligence official.
A delegation of retired Israeli military and government officials meet with Eid Salem (right), a local community leader, in the Palestinian village of Umm al-Kheir in the Israeli-occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026.
Rubble and concrete debris from the demolished home of Yaser Abu Sabha lie scattered beside a water tank trailer in Masafer Yatta, in the Israeli-occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026.
Our reporter witnesses the largest detention of West Bank activists in a decade
"We are making a historic revolution," cabinet minister Bezalel Smotrich said this July as he announced the 104th new settlement approved by Netanyahu's current government. He said it "would prevent the establishment of a terror state in the heart of Israel."
A retired Israeli military general, Yaakov "Mendy" Or, leads groups of former Israeli military and security officials to Palestinian communities in the West Bank to show them the radical changes that have taken place over the last three years.
Maj. Gen. (Ret.) Yaakov "Mendy" Or poses for a portrait during a visit to Masafer Yatta in the occupied West Bank on Sept. 24, 2026. He leads independent groups of retired Israeli military, defense, and government officials visiting Palestinian areas affected by Israeli settler violence.
A remote Palestinian pastoral hamlet with stone dwellings and shelters nestled into the arid hills of Masafer Yatta, in the occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026.
On a recent tour, he brought them to Umm al-Kheir, a Palestinian village surrounded by an expanse of desert hills, where Israeli settlers have set up trailer homes right outside a Palestinian family's property.
Palestinian activist Eid Hatlin said barbed wire in the yard was placed by settlers. The settlers have blocked the family's outdoor bathroom and the Israeli army has blocked the path to their sheep, he said.
An Israeli settler drives an all-terrain vehicle along Route 317 in the South Hebron Hills, near Masafer Yatta in the occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026.
Retired Israeli military and government officials tour the Masafer Yatta region of the Israeli-occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026.
An Israeli flag erected by settlers is seen through the window of the home of Ikhlas al-Hathaleen in the Palestinian village of Umm al-Kheir, in the Masafer Yatta region of the Israeli-occupied West Bank, on Sept. 24, 2026. Israeli military closure orders have restricted the Palestinian family from accessing their courtyard toilet and sheep pen.
"This is nasty. This is evil," said Ari Shuali, a former senior Israeli intelligence official who was part of the visiting group. "I don't believe that we as a people became such kind of unhuman behavior. I'm an Israeli. I'm a proud Israeli and I'm ashamed to be an Israeli [at] the same time."
It's one reason why some Israelis — like him — want to vote Netanyahu out.
Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu stands amid security personnel as he meets with voters at a mall in Ramle, Israel on Oct. 19, 2026.
Netanyahu has downplayed Israeli settler violence in the West Bank and his allies are campaigning on a pledge to expel Palestinians — what they call encouraging emigration.
Netanyahu is campaigning on what he's done to fight back against external threats — with the wars in Gaza, Lebanon and Iran as examples of how he's making Israel safer since Oct. 7.
"On that darkest of days, our enemies expected us to collapse. Well, we didn't collapse. Instead, we rose and fought like lions!" Netanyahu said at the United Nations last month.
Over the last three years, Israel has killed the leaders of Hamas, Hezbollah and Iran. It has sent soldiers to capture new territory in the West Bank, Gaza, Lebanon and Syria. It has fought Houthis in Yemen and militias in Iraq. Netanyahu vows to destroy Iran's nuclear capabilities if he is reelected.
"We're entangled in endless wars and this was done on purpose," Harel said. "It's not a bug, it's a feature. The whole idea is for Israel to keep fighting, because endless wars serve Netanyahu to maintain… a constant state of emergency."
Netanyahu has called them wars of "no choice," alongside a "war for truth" waged in international media and social media.
Gadi Eisenkot, leader of the Yashar party and candidate for prime minister, speaks during an election campaign conference.
At a recent stump speech, Gadi Eisenkot took the stage. He is short with cropped white hair and he was wearing all black. He was speaking to a crowd in Kiryat Gat, a city near the Gaza border — historically a conservative Netanyahu stronghold.
Israel will hold elections this fall. Gadi Eisenkott could be the next prime minister
Eisenkot is the former military chief of staff, appointed by Netanyahu a decade ago, who came back into the public spotlight in the days after Oct. 7 when he joined Netanyahu's war cabinet. Soon after, Eisenkot's son was killed as a soldier in the Gaza war. The very next day, his nephew, too, was killed there.
Eisenkot told the crowd that he began to disagree with Netanyahu's prosecution of the war three months in. "I saw a deep dysfunction in the decision-making process," Eisenkot said. "I saw that political and personal considerations were put above everything else."
He has pledged, if he wins, to launch an official national investigation into the security failure of Oct. 7 — something Netanyahu has refused to do.
"He's straight," said Yair Amar, a longtime Netanyahu voter, who said he has switched allegiances to Eisenkot.
"Many Israelis wish for a saner future," said Harel, the Haaretz columnist. "For somebody who perhaps is not as charismatic and doesn't look the part of an American senator, but is one of us and is seen as somebody who sacrificed a lot."
Gadi Eisenkot, leader of the Yashar party, visits the Western Wall in the Old City of Jerusalem on Oct. 22, 2026. Eisenkot is a retired chief of staff of the Israeli military.
The race is tight. Most polls show Eisenkot's camp ahead of Netanyahu's, but those same polls show neither has a clear path to victory. To win, parties must form a coalition to control a majority in Israel's parliament. The election could tip on a small amount of votes or it could end in stalemate, forcing a repeat election next year. If that were to happen, Netanyahu would stay in power with full authority to make fateful decisions, like going to war. He strongly suggests he wants to strike Iran again.
If Netanyahu loses, Eisenkot vows a different approach. He has spoken of cracking down on settler expansion and violence against Palestinians in the West Bank and of reaching diplomatic agreements in the wars Netanyahu has kept open, but cautioned he would not withdraw troops from Lebanon and Gaza anytime soon.
"Truth be told," Eisenkot told the crowd, "it will take a lot of time."
On the biggest question of all — how to solve Israel's conflict with the Palestinians — Eisenkot boasted that he "never said the solution was two states for two peoples." Meaning the creation of a Palestinian state alongside Israel — the vision that the U.S. and the majority of the world have advocated for decades. Eisenkot thinks Israel should preserve possibilities for a solution in the future. After Oct. 7, Israelis do not want to discuss a Palestinian state.
"It's not something that the Israeli public can swallow at this time," Harel said. "More and more people suspect that the Arabs, the Palestinians, are out to kill us and that the world is out to get us. And there's less support for any kind of concessions because people keep bringing up Oct. 7."
This is not a race of conservatives versus liberals, right versus left. Eisenkot is leading a group of politicians who are largely right-wing, focused on matters of security and campaigning to enlist religious ultra-Orthodox Jews to the military, which Netanyahu has resisted in order to placate his ultra-Orthodox political allies.
"I'm trusting him on the military level, but is it enough?" said Israeli high school theater teacher Anat Ben-Ari, who came to hear Eisenkot speak. She was almost nervous voicing an unpopular opinion out loud: she wants him to forge peace agreements.
"The only way to end war — all the history says that — is by agreements, is by people talking to people, even enemies. Mostly enemies," Ben-Ari said. "I think we should also pay the price for peace in order for less people to die here and not always live on our swords."
"There will always be an enemy, so I think we will always be in a war. We [will] always live on our sword," Lior said. "It will always be."
A worker uses a loupe to examine ballots at a printing factory, ahead of the Israeli legislative elections in Kiryat Gat, southern Israel, on Oct. 8, 2026.
Israeli voters now consider two different visions for their country. Should Israel fight to regain the world's acceptance or to increase their neighbors' deterrence?
Israelis yearn for both says author Micah Goodman, who has written about the Israeli-Palestinian conflict and previously served as an unofficial advisor to Israeli prime ministers.
"In the Middle East, you wanna have a certain kind of reputation. You want to be seen as unpredictable and a little crazy. Like, don't mess with us… and in the West, we want to be seen as a Norwegian, nice, sweet country playing by the rules, predictable."
"After Oct. 7, the Middle East didn't fear us and you have to restore fear. But everything we did to restore fear eroded the love."
Additional reporting by NPR journalists Itay Stern, Abu Bakr Bashir and Michael Levitt.

Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Plenums are essentially closed door meetings of the Party&#x27;s central committee that happen at least once a year
A major political gathering is underway in the Chinese capital, with a closed-door meeting of senior figures that could shape the look of the ruling Communist Party in the years to come.
The Fifth Plenum brings together around 300 members of the Central Committee and state media said it would pave the way for a "tightening of discipline" within the Party&#x27;s ranks.
The four-day session that started on Monday will give Xi Jinping an opportunity to further consolidate his grip on power following recent purges.
The normally annual Plenums are highly secretive, but a summary of decisions and a more detailed document will be made public after.
They are essentially closed door meetings of the Party&#x27;s central committee that happen at least once a year.
There are usually seven plenums during each five-year Central Committee term and they are watched from afar because they are meant to signal the leadership&#x27;s priorities.
The timing for this year&#x27;s gathering is important because it is a year away from the next Party Congress, where a new set of priorities is set up and a new central committee appointed.
Officially, the Central Committee selects the Party&#x27;s top leadership but the real processes leading to the selection are kept secret within the Party elite.
The next Party Congress will almost certainly reappoint Xi as the country&#x27;s leader, following the abolition of term limits.
Past plenum sessions have been used to declare Xi as "the core" of the Party, abolish term limits which cleared the way for him to remain in power indefinitely, and elevate his personal philosophy â branded as "Xi Jinping Thought" â to the same level as Chairman Mao.
This year&#x27;s plenum is an opportunity for Xi to dictate strategy in the run up to the Congress, ensuring that views are aligned and delegates loyal.
Even the location of the meeting, which will wrap up on Thursday, has not been announced. Past Plenums have been held inside Beijing&#x27;s Jingxi Hotel.
According to a readout from the powerful 21-member Politburo this September, the plenum will adopt a decision on "several major issues persistently advancing the comprehensive strict governance of the Party", which is being read as a way to further ensure obedience and effectiveness from distant party officials.
This means it would also be a time of nervousness for delegates as they look around the room with its "empty chairs" following the scale of recent purges within the Party.
Allegations of corruption, even "moral corruption", have seen large swathes of senior figures culled.
"Moral corruption" has been seen by experts as a way of cracking down on perceived disloyalty to Xi because overall disloyalty to the Party can be interpreted as a breach of "morality".
The Asia Society Policy Institute has estimated that of 44 military members appointed to the Central Committee in 2022 only two have not been taken down.
The all-powerful seven-man Central Military Commission, which runs the armed forces in China, has also been whittled down to only two people, Xi Jinping and one other general.
This Plenum is an opportunity for him to appoint new officers to this body.
&#x27;Tigers and flies&#x27;: Millions of officials later, why is Xi&#x27;s corruption purge still going?

China has purged its highest-ranked military general. Why?
