<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Serbia wakes up to new reality after student-led movement ousts Vučić – Europe live
Hopes raised for a reset in relations with EU and other partners following the defeat of authoritarian president
Serbian leader ousted by student movement in seismic election
Mon 26 Oct 2026 06.25 EDT First published on Mon 26 Oct 2026 05.00 EDT
Reporters without Borders say &#x27;press freedom predator&#x27; Vučić&#x27;s defeat offers chance to &#x27;restore media freedom&#x27;
Kremlin hopes new Serbian government understands value of cooperation with Moscow
Hungary&#x27;s Magyar &#x27;feeling nostalgic&#x27; about pictures of celebrations in Serbia
Morning opening: Serbia wakes up to new reality after Vučić
Supporters of the student movement celebrate in the streets of central Belgrade on Sunday night Photograph: Marko Dimic/ZUMA Press Wire/Shutterstock
Supporters of the student movement celebrate in the streets of central Belgrade on Sunday night Photograph: Marko Dimic/ZUMA Press Wire/Shutterstock
Serbia wakes up to a new reality this morning after a grassroots opposition movement led by students ousted the country’s former authoritarian president Aleksandar Vučić , raising hopes for a new opening in relations with the EU and other partners.
Opposition supporters celebrate at the parliament building after Aleksandar Vučić, represented by the silhouette, conceded defeat in the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Đurica/Reuters With 96.9% polling stations processed , the Student List has 52.6% of votes, well ahead of Vučić’s bloc at 39.22%.
The latest CeSID projection suggested the students will have 140 seats in the new parliament, well clear of the 126 required for majority, with 103 for Vucic’s bloc. No other party crossed the 3% threshold, with national minority parties taking the remaining seven seats.
We are likely to get more reactions to the vote today, with international observers set to present their preliminary report on the vote in the early afternoon.
I will also pick up the latest on the first round of the presidential vote in Bulgaria , which saw a record-low turnout of just 22%. The second round will be held this Sunday.
Elsewhere, I will be looking for updates from France and Italy after another rocky weekend of protests. French PM Sébastien Lecornu is going to meet with the student unions in the afternoon for what could be pivotal talks on the future of the demonstrations.
And E5 defence ministers – that’s France, Germany, Italy, Poland, and the UK – are meeting in Paris for discussions on Ukraine . Oh, and Slovakia’s foreign minister Juraj Blanár is in Moscow for energy talks with Russia.
It’s Monday, 26 October 2026 , it’s Jakub Krupa here, and this is Europe Live .
Reporters without Borders say &#x27;press freedom predator&#x27; Vučić&#x27;s defeat offers chance to &#x27;restore media freedom&#x27;
Kremlin hopes new Serbian government understands value of cooperation with Moscow
Hungary&#x27;s Magyar &#x27;feeling nostalgic&#x27; about pictures of celebrations in Serbia
Morning opening: Serbia wakes up to new reality after Vučić
Press freedom organisation Reporters without Borders welcomed the defeat of “press freedom predator” Aleksandar Vučić and said it offered “a historic opportunity to restore media freedom.”
“The defeat of press freedom predator Aleksandar Vucic by the opposition movement Student List in the Sunday parliamentary election offers a historic opportunity to restore media freedom. RSF hails the courageous work of all journalists who resisted Vucic’s oppression for more than a decade.”
We are also getting the first reaction to last night’s vote from Moscow.
The Kremlin said “it hopes Serbia’s new rulers understand that cooperation with Moscow is in their own country’s interests,” and that it would want to “continue to develop ties with Serbia , but it takes two to tango,” Reuters reported.
In recent years, Aleksandar Vučić was a rare European voice calling for “dialogue” with Russia and not shying away from visiting Moscow, even during the full-scale invasion of Ukraine .
This put him close to the EU’s awkward squad with Hungary’s now-ousted Viktor Orbán and Slovakia’s Robert Fico (up for re-election next year, where he is likely to struggle.)
Russian president Vladimir Putin and Serbian president Aleksandar Vučić enter the hall during their meeting in Kremlin, Moscow in May last year. Photograph: Alexander Zemlianichenko/AP POOL/EPA The Student List’s policy on Russia is likely to look quite different in style – don’t expect their leader, whoever it may be, to attend Moscow’s Victory Parade, for instance – but will not necessarily see a major shift in substance, given Serbia’s close economic and energy ties with Moscow (including so many Russian émigrées finding their new home in Belgrade to escape sanctions).
Vučić used his concession speech last night to claim the incoming government would adopt sanctions against Moscow by spring, despite polling showing that a clear majority of over 60% of the public opposes such a move.
But putting his sour grapes rhetoric aside, that looks improbable. In fact, the Student List explicitly stated in its manifesto that it wants to retain “the traditional partnership” with Russia and will oppose any punitive measures against Moscow.
I explained a bit more on that on our election night blog last night.
Hungary’s prime minister Péter Magyar – who came into power after ousting Viktor Orbán earlier this year – is clearly emotionally invested in the events in Serbia .
After posting several times last night and drawing ( somewhat imperfect, but understandable ) parallels between the two victories, he is back at it this morning:
“ Surely I wasn’t the only one feeling nostalgic when I saw those pictures coming out of Serbia last night? It’s symbolic that the “Budapest Express” reached Belgrade in politics before it did on the tracks.”
Serbia wakes up to a new reality this morning after a grassroots opposition movement led by students ousted the country’s former authoritarian president Aleksandar Vučić , raising hopes for a new opening in relations with the EU and other partners.
Opposition supporters celebrate at the parliament building after Aleksandar Vučić, represented by the silhouette, conceded defeat in the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Đurica/Reuters With 96.9% polling stations processed , the Student List has 52.6% of votes, well ahead of Vučić’s bloc at 39.22%.
The latest CeSID projection suggested the students will have 140 seats in the new parliament, well clear of the 126 required for majority, with 103 for Vucic’s bloc. No other party crossed the 3% threshold, with national minority parties taking the remaining seven seats.
We are likely to get more reactions to the vote today, with international observers set to present their preliminary report on the vote in the early afternoon.
I will also pick up the latest on the first round of the presidential vote in Bulgaria , which saw a record-low turnout of just 22%. The second round will be held this Sunday.
Elsewhere, I will be looking for updates from France and Italy after another rocky weekend of protests. French PM Sébastien Lecornu is going to meet with the student unions in the afternoon for what could be pivotal talks on the future of the demonstrations.
And E5 defence ministers – that’s France, Germany, Italy, Poland, and the UK – are meeting in Paris for discussions on Ukraine . Oh, and Slovakia’s foreign minister Juraj Blanár is in Moscow for energy talks with Russia.
It’s Monday, 26 October 2026 , it’s Jakub Krupa here, and this is Europe Live .

Blog opening: Serbia&#x27;s strongman Vučić faces sternest test of his 12-year rule
And, on that note, we will be closing the blog for tonight as the clocks strike half past midnight in Belgrade .
The opposition grassroots movement Student List has won a landslide in the Serbian parliamentary election, ousting the country’s authoritarian leader Aleksandar Vučić.
The leaderless student movement, which started less than a year ago in protest against alleged widespread state corruption ( 17:42 ), is projected to have a clear majority in the new parliament ( 21:43 , 22:22 , 00:37 ).
The result appears to have been driven primarily by unprecedented mobilisation across the country, with the final turnout projected to be just below 70%, the highest since the first election in 1990.
“It is clear ⁠that we have lost these ​elections,” Vučić conceded ( 21:35 ), prompting wild scenes of celebrations in Belgrade and other Serbian cities ( 21:45 , 22:01 , 22:08 , 22:19 , 22:38 , 22:42 , 22:45 , 23:21 ).
Vučić rose to prominence in the 1990s as a radical nationalist, who supported the policies of Serbian expansion and ethnic cleansing led by Slobodan Milošević, and has been the country’s leading government figure for more than a decade ( 17:14 ).
A prominent Student List candidate Radomir Saičić hailed the result as “a victory of courage over fear, of hope over indifference,” as he stressed “the great responsibility” facing the movement. “Tonight, we celebrate … Tomorrow, the work begins,” he said ( 22:34 ).
The result was warmly welcomed by EU leaders, Ursula von der Leyen and António Costa, as they expressed their hopes for a new start in the bloc’s relationship with Serbia ( 23:39 , 23:54 ), despite the movement’s ambiguous positioning on the EU ( 16:48 ).
Hungary’s prime minister Péter Magyar was the first national EU leader to offer congratulations, drawing parallels with his victory over Viktor Orbán earlier this year ( 22:48 , 23:09 ).
In a likely further embarrassment to the ousted strongman Vučić, Serbia will have to hold presidential elections in the coming months to choose his successor as the head of state ( 23:34 ).
Thousands of diaspora voters joined in lengthy queues to cast their votes around the world ( 20:41 ), with up to three hours wait in London ( 18:58 , 19:09 ).
Serbian leader Aleksandar Vučić ousted by student movement in seismic election
For now, that’s all from me, Jakub Krupa , with contributions from Ana Milosavljevic on the ground in Belgrade. Thank you for joining us tonight.
Ilija Srdanović, first on the electoral list ‘Student List – Students Win’, celebrates following the first results of the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Đurica/Reuters The latest version of the Ipsos/CeSID poll, updated with the partial results, gives the Student List 141 out of 250 seats in the new parliament with 52.9% of the vote share.
That’s well clear of the 126 required for majority.
Vučić’s list will have 103 seats in the new parliament, with 38.8% of the vote share.
Four minority parties will take the remaining six seats in the new assembly.
EU enlargement commissioner Marta Kos is clearly hoping for more engagement from the new government in Belgrade.
“ Dear Serbia, you have made your voice heard! After nearly two years of protests for democracy, the rule of law and the fight against corruption, Serbians turned out in historic numbers. Congratulations to the Student List on your victory!
These are the very values at the heart of our European Union. A new chapter can begin for Serbia. The door to the EU is open, and we are ready to move forward together.”
Montenegrin prime minister Milojko Spajić also offered his congratulations.
“ Today in Serbia is a vivid reminder that the will of the citizens and their right to freely decide on their future are the foundation of every democratic change.
I congratulate the representatives of the Student List and firmly believe that a new chapter lies ahead for Montenegro and Serbia, based on respect and open dialogue, without unnecessary escalation of tensions.
The citizens of both countries deserve relations that will be measured by concrete results of cooperation, and not by the depth of diplomatic misunderstandings.”
Montenegrin president Jakov Milatovic also joined in:
“I congratulate the Student List on their electoral victory, and the citizens of the Republic of Serbia on their clearly expressed democratic will. I am convinced that this electoral result will open a new chapter in the relations between Montenegro and the Republic of Serbia, based on friendship, sincere dialogue, and commitment to a shared European future.”
The change of government in Serbia could indeed significantly shift the dynamic in the western Balkans, although again we should not necessarily expect an immediate move against Russia ( 17:54 ).
Council of Europe’s secretary general Alain Berset has also offered his congratulations.
“Congratulations to the Student List of Serbia on your electoral result.
Serbian citizens have made their democratic choice. We welcome former President Vučić’s acceptance of the result and commitment to a peaceful democratic transition.
Council of Europe stands ready to work with Serbia’s democratically elected leadership to strengthen human rights, democracy and the rule of law.
Your perseverance, energy and commitment to European principles and Serbia’s future have brought you this far. You can count on our support.”
European Commission chief Ursula von der Leyen and European Council president António Costa have congratulated the Student List on their victory in tonight’s election in Serbia.
“ Today, the people of Serbia turned out in record numbers to shape their country’s future. Congratulations to the Student List on its victory in the parliamentary elections.
We stand ready to support reforms on the rule of law, media freedom, the fight against corruption and strong democratic institutions, and to work together to advance Serbia’s future in the European Union.”
As explained earlier, it may not be quite that straightforward when it comes to the EU… ( 16:48 ).
By the way, Vučić’s decision to step down as president to contest this election now puts him in a very awkward position.
Because, erm, the Serbian people will now have to choose his successor as the head of state, and looking at the results tonight, he may not like the outcome of that process either.
After his resignation, a close Vučić ally – the outgoing speaker of the parliament and former prime minister Ana Brnabić – has temporarily stepped into the role as acting president.
But once the new parliament forms, the new speaker will have to decide the next steps for recruiting the full-time replacement.
The process is complicated and CRTA has a good explanation on all possible dates here , but essentially the vote needs to take place before the end of the year.
Brnabić and Vučić. Awkward. Photograph: Anadolu/Getty Images View image in fullscreen
People celebrate after Aleksandar Vučić, leader of the rightwing Serbian Progressive party (SNS) conceded defeat in the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Drobnjaković/AP View image in fullscreen
Supporters of the Student List wave a giant Serbian flag in Belgrade as they celebrate victory in the Serbian parliamentary elections. Photograph: Andrej Isaković/AFP/Getty Images View image in fullscreen
People celebrate after Aleksandar Vučić, leader of the rightwing Serbian Progressive party (SNS) conceded defeat in the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Drobnjaković/AP View image in fullscreen
People celebrate after Aleksandar Vučić, leader of the rightwing Serbian Progressive party (SNS) conceded defeat in the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Drobnjaković/AP View image in fullscreen
People celebrate after Aleksandar Vučić, leader of the rightwing Serbian Progressive party (SNS) conceded defeat in the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Drobnjaković/AP View image in fullscreen
A person lights a flare as supporters of the Student List celebrate following the first results of the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Zorana Jevtić/Reuters View image in fullscreen
Citywide euphoria breaks out in Belgrade as former president Aleksandar Vučić admits defeat. Photograph: Daniel Alfoldi/ZUMA Press Wire/Shutterstock When thinking about today’s opposition win in Serbia, there is an understandable temptation to compare it to Hungary and the fall of Viktor Orbán in April – and Magyar clearly plays on that in his reaction, too ( 22:48 ).
Péter Magyar celebrates Tisza’s victory at the election party in Budapest in April. Photograph: dts News Agency Germany/Shutterstock And there are some undeniable similarities in how the two countries visibly grew tired of their increasingly authoritarian leaders.
The opposition forces in both countries – Tisza in Hungary and the Student List in Serbia – have some things in common, for example their sort of catch-all character as they formulate their demands in the broadest possible terms to attract all sorts of voters, while relentlessly focusing on corruption and demands of accountability for political leaders.
On a technical level, they both focused on a door to door campaign, making a point of covering thousands of kilometres across the country, and reaching out to voters directly, instead of being a sort of talking shop with ideas. They also both adopted elements of national folks and symbolism for their campaigns.
The Serbian Student List does not have a single strong leader figure in sharp contrast to Péter Magyar’s strong position in Tisza . It is based on a grassroots group of people with little prior experience of frontline politics, and not on a former insider who switched sides, with all sorts of upsides and downsides that come with that. For one, we do not even know who could be their PM.
Members of the electoral list “Student List - Students Win” celebrate following the first results of the parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Marko Đurica/Reuters The Student List’s path to a parliamentary majority was also technically more complicated or, put it simply, required a larger win. That is largely down to the mechanics of the Serbian electoral system, which – unlike the setup Viktor Orbán had tailored in Hungary with the intention of helping his own party, not the opposition – does not offer the same disproportionate winner’s bonus.
Finally, the underlying issues demanding immediate attention are possibly much bigger and more complicated to resolve in Serbia than in Hungary, particularly without the option of getting some EU funds flowing quickly after the vote to buy you some good will and time. Hungary’s Tisza is much more openly pro-European than the Student List.
But Magyar clearly and, at least to an extent, rightly sees the two movements and their victories as linked : earlier this morning, he even posted a slightly cheesy post reminiscing the times he and his parents spent at an unnamed Serbian restaurant in Budapest, “which has since closed down.”
“Fortunately, you can still find great ćevapi in plenty of places, whether Sarajevo-style or Serbian-style,” he added, posting a picture of himself with the said ćevapi.
(There are some sensational Serbian places in London, too. Just saying.)
Hungary’s prime minister Péter Magyar – who came to power ousting Viktor Orbán after 16 years in power – is among the very first European leaders to offer his congratulations to the Student List.
“I have just spoken by phone with the leaders of the Students’ List @studentblokade, who won the elections in Serbia, to congratulate them on their victory. I assured them of Hungary’s support.
I was delighted to hear how much strength our victory in April had given them during this difficult struggle. ”
“In Serbia, @avucic has conceded defeat. The Students’ List has won an absolute majority in the elections. Celebrations have begun in the streets across Serbia. Sound familiar? ”
“I think this is a moment we will remember for the rest of our lives,” Sara, 24, says as she smiles with her friends.
“It’s simply the realisation of our dream. And it’s very strange that on the very streets where the police beat us , on the streets where we retreated countless times in the face of the gendarmerie, we are now standing here as the victors. ”
“I think this is a victory for everything we stood for, for all the values we promoted through the student movement. And I’m very glad that we’ve proven that Serbia only wins when the students win. ”
Sara, 24, centre, celebrates the opposition election win in Belgrade, Serbia, with flanked by her friends Photograph: Ana Milosavljevic/The Guardian Ana Milosavljevic
“This feels like New Year’s Eve to me, like three New Year’s Eves all at once, really. We can finally breathe again.”
She says she is hoping for swift accountability for key members of the ousted regime.
Ivana, 43, celebrates on the streets of Belgrade after opposition wins the 2026 Serbian election Photograph: Ana Milosavljevic/The Guardian Ana Milosavljevic
"After 14 years, we finally feel freedom again,” Nemanja , 29, says when we start chatting amid celebrations in Belgrade.
“We feel that justice will come and that this will become a beautiful and good country, where people can get ahead through their own knowledge and abilities.”
Nemanja, 29, smiles as he celebrates the result of the 2026 Serbian elections in Belgrade, Serbia Photograph: Ana Milosavljevic/The Guardian Speaking at the Student List’s election night party, chemist Radomir Saičić had one of the strongest speeches as he thanked all who cast their votes in today’s election.
Radomir Saičić, a chemistry professor and candidate on the student-backed electoral list, celebrates the Students' List victory in the Serbian parliamentary elections. Photograph: Andrej Isaković/AFP/Getty Images But he said “ tonight is about more than just the results: it is about the people who kept believing when things where at their hardest, who refused to give up, to believe that nothing could ever change.”
He calls it “ a victory of courage over fear; of hope over indifference; and of conviction that Serbia can, and must, be different.”
He says the victory “is not a personal trophy; does not belong to any of us,” and brings about “great responsibility” for all those in the new parliamentary majority.
“ Tonight we have shown that change is not only possible, but that there is a will for it. Now we must prove ourselves worthy of the trust we have been given. …
Tonight, we celebrate in the way Serbia knows to do best: with song, dance, embraces, and tears of joy. And tomorrow? Tomorrow, the work begins. ”
In pretty moving scenes, some people offer handshakes, high fives and even bear hugs to police officers as they stand aside from blocking the streets, despite earlier concerns about a potential mobilisation of security forces.
A man celebrating the election results hugs a police officer as the result of the 2026 Serbian election prompts street celebrations in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Ana Milosavljevic/The Guardian View image in fullscreen
A person celebrating the election results chats with a police officer as the result of the 2026 Serbian election prompts street celebrations in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Ana Milosavljevic/The Guardian The crowd is now moving towards Slavija square, where they held their final pre-election on Thursday.
Crowds celebrate the result of the 2026 Serbian parliamentary election in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Ana Milosavljevic/The Guardian Crta’s projection is very similar to that of Ipsos/CeSIR ( 21:43 ): it puts the Student List at 51.2% with 134 seats in the new parliament, well clear of the 126 required for the majority.
Vučić’s list is in the second place with 40.4% of the vote and 106 seats.
10 seats are expected to go to minority groups, including three seats each for Hungarians and Albanians.
If you’re planning a trip to Belgrade in the coming days, I hope you like enthusiastic street dancing, red flares and brass bands.

The Siemens turbine blade factory in Hull, northern England. Photograph: Christopher Thomond/The Guardian
The Siemens turbine blade factory in Hull, northern England. Photograph: Christopher Thomond/The Guardian
Drive reindustrialisation from No 10 North or risk failure, MPs tell Burnham
Ninety-plus Labour MPs warn civil service lacks skills to drive growth where it is needed and plan should be steered by Louis Haigh from Manchester
Andy Burnham’s pledge to reindustrialise Britain is doomed to fail unless it is driven decisively from his new No 10 North outpost in Manchester, a group of more than 90 backbench Labour MPs is warning.
The PM has suggested that rebuilding the UK’s industrial capacity will be central to meeting his pledge to bring “good growth in every postcode” .
John Healey, who will deliver his first budget on Wednesday, used his speech at Labour’s conference last month to promise a “new age of industrialisation”.
France drops opposition to UK being included in ‘Made in Europe’ legislation
However, in a new briefing paper, seen by the Guardian, the backbench group warns that decades of deindustrialisation and deregulation mean the government no longer has the detailed understanding of industry needed to make such a plan work.
“The state has lost capacity and has acquired a worrying set of behaviours which will inhibit reindustrialisation. The state has become less knowledgable about the state of the economy and our businesses, unable to comprehensively plan and slow to make decisions,” it says.
They are calling on Burnham to pass a new Reindustrialisation Act, to set clear targets for creating jobs by rebuilding the country’s manufacturing capacity, and ensure ministers are held to account against them. “If we do not plan for reindustrialisation, we cannot achieve it,” the group argues.
The briefing paper urges the PM to give the job of overseeing reindustrialisation to first secretary, Louise Haigh, and base it at No 10 North, the Manchester outpost he established in July that has already taken on some responsibility for growth policy .
And the paper argues that the National Wealth Fund – the arm’s-length body that invests in promising businesses – should be transformed into a Reindustrialisation Development Corporation, whose primary job is to provide capital for rebuilding UK industry.
Andy MacNae, the MP for Rossendale and Darwen, who chairs the group, says: “I’ve got absolutely no doubt about Andy or John’s commitment to this, but there is a kind of inertia in government, within the civil service, within economic orthodoxy, that still says that growth policy is about cities – it’s about the places that are already doing well.”
Instead, he said reindustrialisation must be about “delivering jobs to the places that need them most”, adding: “It’s about making things again, and it’s about making the things that give us national resilience in a turbulent world.”
He cited the government’s recent decision to nationalise British Steel , adding that reindustrialisation involves policies across government, from procurement to education.
Steve Yemm, MP for the former mining constituency of Mansfield, said: “Countries around the world are reindustrialising, yet too many in Whitehall still treat industrial policy as dangerous nostalgia. We need the prime minister’s full backing to challenge those outdated assumptions.”
A third member of the group, Lorraine Beavers, MP for Blackpool North and Fleetwood, said she was enthusiastic about the promise of reindustrialisation for constituencies like hers; but warned: “If I have learned anything since becoming an MP two years ago, it’s that Whitehall doesn’t fix a problem unless it is told to.”
In its industrial strategy , published while Keir Starmer was prime minister, Labour identified eight sectors ripe for growth, including advanced manufacturing, clean energy and defence.
In his conference speech, to which Healey had invited a young apprentice who had worked on building a new Mersey ferry, he said Labour would “get Britain growing. With hope for our young people. With good work, good jobs and a new age of industrialisation.”

- **多源确认**: 1

- **发生了什么**: Private equity growth investments in defense reached $4 billion in the first nine months of 2026, up from $500 million in 2024.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Defense tech investment boom sparks bubble warning as private equity pours in
Traditional buyout investors are increasingly backing younger, high-growth defense tech businesses.
As defense tech companies mature, private equity, debt, and other forms of financing are expected to become more important in bridging the gap between innovation and industrial-scale production.
"It is clear to me that PE is moving into territory VC used to own," PitchBook analyst Jim Corridore told CNBC.
Private equity firms are pouring billions into defense technology as rising military spending and geopolitical tensions fuel an investment boom.
Traditional buyout investors are increasingly backing younger, high-growth firms in the sector, according to PitchBook data, with private equity growth investments in defense reaching $4 billion in the first nine months of 2026, up from $500 million in 2024.
As defense tech companies mature, analysts say private equity, debt and other forms of financing are expected to become more important in bridging the gap between innovation and industrial-scale production, as venture capital alone may not be enough to finance companies&#x27; expansion.
It comes as geopolitical tensions, wars, and countries&#x27; efforts to strengthen military capabilities are creating opportunities for companies developing everything from autonomous drones to missile defense systems and satellite technology.
A survey of 250 senior decision-makers at U.K. defense companies by law firm White & Case found that 97% expected their businesses to seek significant external funding over the next 12 months.
"Private capital is now playing a critical role in the financing of defence technology companies," Daniel Turgel, a partner at White & Case, told CNBC.
Private equity and venture capital firms, sovereign wealth funds and family offices were all looking to invest more in the sector, marking a significant change from just a few years ago, Turgel said, adding that many investors were previously reluctant or restricted from doing so.
The growth of private equity allocations to defense technology also comes amid a surge in venture capital investment in defense technology. Startups across NATO and allied countries raised $27.1 billion through September, nearly double the total for all of 2025, according to an October report from Dealroom and Resilience Media.
While venture capital traditionally backs young, high-growth startups, and private equity focuses on more established businesses, the distinction is increasingly blurring as some PE firms move into growth investments.
"Private equity firms are putting more money into defense, and they are changing how they do it," Jim Corridore, lead industrials analyst at PitchBook, told CNBC.
The number of defense PE growth deals increased from 15 in 2024 to 31 in 2025 and 48 through September 2026, according to PitchBook.
Those investments accounted for 41% of defense PE deal value in 2026, up from just 3% in 2024.
"It is clear to me that PE is moving into territory VC used to own," Corridore said.
Private equity firm Advent International announced a commitment of up to $1 billion to next-generation defense technology in March, including investments in autonomous systems developers Shield AI and Saronic.
Shonnel Malani, Advent&#x27;s managing partner and global head of aerospace and defense, told CNBC the firm has already deployed roughly three-quarters of that commitment.
Malani said Advent&#x27;s expansion into younger defense technology companies was a natural evolution of its existing investments in more mature defense businesses.
Advent is backing businesses it knows well and prioritizing proven technologies that address specific military needs and have been validated by customers, rather than focusing primarily on financial metrics, Malani said.
While the influx of private capital is helping accelerate innovation in defense technology, it is also fueling concerns about inflated valuations and speculative investments.
Malani said defense technology companies are increasingly valued on their technological promise rather than revenue, profit, or order books, leaving considerable room for interpretation over what they are worth.
He added that a correction may be inevitable as some companies struggle to turn technological promise into commercial success.
"I think there is a very big risk of that," he added when asked whether an investment bubble could emerge.
There is "a lot of froth in the system," Malani said, adding that Advent had walked away from several potential investments because valuations had become "silly."
‘Project Firepower’: Inside Rheinmetall’s gunpowder expansion as Europe races to replenish its ammunition
Europe’s defense boom faces a new test: Can it actually deliver weapons?
Ukraine’s drone playbook is wreaking havoc in Russia — and upending where NATO wants to invest
Tank maker KNDS postpones IPO amid market struggles for defense
Defense stocks plummet on reports Germany is scrapping warships; Rheinmetall stock down 18%
PitchBook&#x27;s Corridore pushed back against the notion that the sector is overheated, but he expects investment interest to keep growing.
"Budgets are growing, the military has used up a lot of ammunition, and new drone and counter-drone technology is arriving," he said. "All of that gives investors the steady revenue PE needs to make long-term bets."
Other private equity investors are sticking more closely to traditional buyout strategies.
Lionel Mestre, partner at French private equity firm Weinberg Capital Partners, told CNBC his firm still focuses on profitable defense businesses with established products, customers and positive cash flow, rather than early-stage startups.
He said valuations for the smaller, established defense suppliers Weinberg targets had not increased significantly, despite growing investor interest in the sector.
Mestre sees opportunities to finance manufacturing expansion and consolidate Europe&#x27;s fragmented defense supply chain, and considers the rapidly evolving drone market too immature for Weinberg&#x27;s traditional buyout strategy.
The smaller defense businesses Weinberg is targeting need investment in production capacity, employees, working capital and commercial operations to meet rising demand.
"This sector will have to consolidate anyway," Mestre said, arguing that smaller suppliers need to become larger and more competitive.
He also said European institutional investors&#x27; attitudes toward defense had shifted significantly, particularly since President Donald Trump&#x27;s return to the White House and renewed pressure on European countries to increase military spending.
Malani and Mestre both see opportunities for PE firms to help defense companies scale production, providing not only capital but also industry connections and access to government customers.
Malani said some startups were rushing toward initial public offerings before developing the operational maturity needed to become sustainable public companies.
And, despite the increase in investment activity, exits remain a potential challenge.
According to PitchBook, there have been 504 defense PE deals but just 101 exits since 2021, although the comparison includes add-on acquisitions and therefore does not directly measure the proportion of investments awaiting an exit.
IPOs accounted for 70% of defense PE exit value through September 2026, leaving investors exposed to changing stock market conditions.

NHS consultants to go on two-day strike in England in November, says BMA
Industrial action by senior doctors to begin on 25 November, with only emergency cover provided, union says
Senior doctors in England plan to go on strike for two days in November, the British Medical Association has said.
The industrial action by consultants is to begin at 6.59am on 25 November, with only emergency cover to be provided.
More than three-quarters of consultants who took part in the BMA ballot voted in favour of strike action.
The doctors were given a 3.5% pay increase this year.

Saudi Arabia intercepted drones and missiles headed for several cities, including the capital Riyadh, fired by Iran -aligned Houthis on Sunday, the Saudi-led coalition in Yemen said.
The ongoing fighting between Saudi Arabia and the Yemen-based Houthis, combined with remarks by President Donald Trump last week suggesting that the U.S. and Israel are planning fresh strikes on Iran after the Nov. 3 midterm elections, drove oil prices to a six-month high Friday.

Smoke and flames rise from King Khalid Airport, after Yemen’s Iran-backed Houthis said they attacked the airport, in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia, October 8, 2026, in this screengrab obtained from a social media video.
King Khalid International Airport said in a notice to travelers that it was resuming operations Sunday, using its Terminal 1 for domestic flights and Terminal 2 for overseas routes.
The airport suspended operations on Oct. 10 following a strike that killed at least 13 people and injured around 300 others, making it the deadliest attack on Saudi Arabia in more than a decade.
Saudi Aramco did not immediately respond to CNBC&#x27;s request for comment.

Trump said Wednesday that the U.S. was not negotiating with Iran and something would happen "fairly soon."
The president&#x27;s cryptic remarks came as U.S. officials told The New York Times that the U.S. and Israel have plans for fresh strikes on Iran after the midterm elections.

"We keep getting asked by clients: &#x27;Is it going to be before the midterms? Is it after the midterms?&#x27; So it&#x27;s not a question of &#x27;if.&#x27; People are asking if it&#x27;s a question of &#x27;when.&#x27; Our view remains that it&#x27;s more likely after the midterms than before."
Rising tensions, worries about supply disruptions and concerns about possible fresh U.S.-Israeli strikes on Iran helped push Brent crude to a six-month high Friday.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Oil fell as investors weighed the threat of further attacks on Saudi Arabian energy infrastructure against concerns that elevated prices could eventually erode demand.
The pullback followed a sharp rally on Friday after attacks by Yemen&#x27;s Houthi militants on Saudi Arabia reignited fears of disruptions to global energy supplies.
A pumpjack operates after sunrise in Kern County, which has a long history of oil production, in California’s 22nd Congressional District on Oct. 16, 2026 near Bakersfield, California.
U.S. crude oil fell over 2% on Monday as investors weighed the threat of further attacks on Saudi Arabian energy infrastructure against concerns that elevated prices could eventually erode demand.
U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures slid 2.19% to $101 a barrel, while Brent crude futures declined 1.89% to $114.51 a barrel.
The pullback followed a sharp rally on Friday after attacks by Yemen&#x27;s Houthi militants on Saudi Arabia reignited fears of disruptions to global energy supplies.
Tensions remained elevated over the weekend as the Houthis said they had targeted Saudi Arabia&#x27;s main airport in Riyadh and oil facilities on Saturday. Saudi authorities said Sunday that they had intercepted additional missiles and drones.
The kingdom partially resumed operations at its main airport on Sunday despite damage from earlier attacks, even as the Houthis threatened further strikes.
The continued hostilities have heightened concerns about the vulnerability of Saudi Arabia&#x27;s energy infrastructure, with traders increasingly focused on the risk of a major disruption to production, refining or export facilities.
Speaking to CNBC, Michael Alfaro, chief investment officer at Gallo Partners, estimated that geopolitical uncertainty had already added roughly $20 a barrel to oil prices.
I think the theme we&#x27;re in right now is that uncertainty is off the charts, and if you think about the risk premium that&#x27;s baked into oil right now, it&#x27;s about $20 a barrel.
A substantial strike on Saudi Arabia&#x27;s East-West pipeline or additional refining infrastructure could send prices sharply higher, he added, but further gains could eventually undermine consumption.
"What we would likely see if oil pushed above 120, 130, 140 dollars a barrel for Brent, that is, you&#x27;re going to see more demand destruction," Alfaro said.
Beyond the immediate threat to crude supplies, damage to refining infrastructure could pose a more persistent challenge to global energy markets.
"The real challenge is refining capacity," he said, warning that the disruptions could develop into a longer-term structural problem extending into 2027 and beyond.
Refining constraints could keep fuel prices high: CIO
Meanwhile, Saudi Arabia plans to begin storing crude oil in Singapore from the second half of 2027, according to energy ministers from both countries, in a move aimed at strengthening the resilience of its Asian supply chain.
In the U.S., the Energy Department on Friday authorized the release of up to 400,000 barrels from the Strategic Petroleum Reserve to address a supply disruption at a Louisiana refinery.
Still, the prospect of additional supply from American producers remains uncertain, with companies reluctant to commit to major new investments amid volatile prices and geopolitical risks.
Alfaro added that U.S. producers were adding some drilling rigs, but remained cautious about substantially increasing capital expenditure.
"We&#x27;re not really seeing major capex additions to bring new rigs on or bring new production online," he said.

Figure caption, Watch: Supporters fill streets of Caracas to welcome MarÃ­a Corina Machado
By Will Grant ,Â  BBC&#x27;s Mexico, Central America and Cuba correspondent Â  and Â  Grace Eliza Goodwin
Venezuelan opposition leader MarÃ­a Corina Machado has returned home to a fraught political landscape after more than 10 months in exile.
"I am here. With you," Machado wrote on X on Sunday. Her return comes after repeated prior efforts to re-enter the country came to no avail.
In January, a month after Machado left Venezuela on a speedboat under cover of darkness to accept the Nobel Peace Prize in Norway, the US seized her opponent, President NicolÃ¡s Maduro.
The Trump administration then backed his deputy, Delcy RodrÃ­guez, as leader and she was sworn in as interim president, later signing an oil deal which granted very favourable terms to the US.
Machado was photographed on Sunday attending Mass at a church in the Venezuelan state of Lara, surrounded by supporters hugging her and clasping her hands.
She then began travelling to the capital Caracas in a caravan of cheering supporters. More supporters have lined the route of her journey to the capital.
She was photographed smiling as she leaned out of the sunroof of a car as she entered Caracas late on Sunday night.
"Night of reunion in our Caracas. For the reunion of each family, for the Freedom of each political prisoner, for the Freedom of Venezuela," she wrote on X.
Her ally, former presidential candidate Edmundo GonzÃ¡lez, confirmed her arrival, writing in a statement: "I am thrilled to announce to you that our leader, my dear MarÃ­a Corina, is on Venezuelan soil."
"Today is a very important day for Venezuelans. Our sovereign voice returns with her to lead and guide this process!" GonzÃ¡lez said.
He added that he will soon join Machado in Venezuela to advance the country "toward freedom".
An advisor to Machado said in September that over the previous few weeks Machado had attempted seven times to return to Venezuela, but was denied each time. There have been reports that the US was involved in delaying her return, which it has denied.
It&#x27;s unclear what Machado plans to do next now that she&#x27;s back, but she has previously said she would run for president again if an election were held . Her ability to return to her country was always contingent on approval from the Trump administration, which it appears has now been granted.
A staunch supporter of democracy, Machado won an independent presidential primary election - held outside the Maduro government&#x27;s official election authority - in 2023, earning more than 90% of the vote.
Despite winning the primary, Maduro&#x27;s government barred Machado from running for office, accusing her of fraud and tax violations. Machado denied the allegations, calling them politically motivated. She spent her final days in the country hiding in safehouses before her fleeing to Norway to accept the Nobel Prize.
In the 2024 election, Machado&#x27;s ally GonzÃ¡lez ran in her place against Maduro.
The electoral authority, which is closely aligned with the government, declared Maduro the winner without making detailed voting tallies public.
The opposition argued that GonzÃ¡lez won the election, not Maduro, uploading copies of the voting tallies as evidence.
Maduro was removed from the country by US forces in January, and is currently jailed in New York where he faces drugs, weapons and torture charges. He has pleaded not guilty.
A spokesman for Machado called her return "a major step toward a democratic transition and free, fair, and verifiable presidential elections in the near future".
"We thank President Donald Trump, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, the US Government, Congress, and allied governments for their continued leadership and support," her spokesperson, David Smolansky, added.
Speaking to reporters on Sunday, Trump said, "I don&#x27;t know anything about" her return, and added that he hoped she was "safe".
"I think she&#x27;s a very nice woman. She gave me her Nobel Prize, so how can I say anything else? And I hope she&#x27;s safe," he said.
In January, Machado visited the White House and gifted her Nobel prize award to Trump . The committee that issues the prize had said "it cannot be revoked, shared, or transferred to others".
US Secretary of State Marco Rubio praised his country&#x27;s role in securing her return through political reconciliation talks.
"The conditions allowing for the safe return of Ms Machado to Venezuela is the direct result of the US supported reconciliation talks which has already seen hundreds of political prisoners freed and hundreds of political opposition figures return from exile," he posted on X.
He added: "We expect all political actors to support these talks which represent the best chance Venezuela has had in thirty years to peacefully achieve not just a free and fair election but an enduring democracy."

### 18. ★★☆☆☆ Australian murderer loses bid to eat Vegemite in prison

- **分类**: 🇨🇳 中国
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T04:05:28+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Andre McKechnie, who murdered a property developer, argued it was his right to enjoy Australian culture.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Andre McKechnie argues the Vegemite ban in Victoria&#x27;s jails denies him the right to "enjoy his culture"
A convicted murderer serving a life sentence in Australia has lost his bid to overturn a ban on prisoners consuming Vegemite - the salty yeast-based spread that has become a national symbol.
Andre McKechnie argued that the ban, in place since 2006, denied him the right to "enjoy his culture as an Australian".
Authorities in the state of Victoria say inmates may use the strong-smelling paste to disguise contraband substances or to brew alcohol behind bars.
And on Monday, a Supreme Court judge rejected McKechnie&#x27;s appeal, saying the prisoner&#x27;s "cultural rights are limited primarily by the fact he has been sentenced to a term of imprisonment".
In his lawsuit against Victoria&#x27;s Department of Justice and Corrections, the agency which manages the state&#x27;s prisons, McKechnie, 54, said the ban on the spread breached law requiring prisons to provide food that allowed inmates to maintain their health and well-being but the judge said that had not been proven.
McKechnie was handed a life sentence for stabbing a property developer to death in Queensland in the 1990s. He served part of his sentence in Queensland before being transferred to Victoria.
During the hearing, the court heard evidence from Corrections Victoria that the smell of vegemite can distract sniffer dogs trying to detect drugs being brought into prison.
The product is also banned in jails in Queensland, South Australia and Tasmania.
A much-loved breakfast spread for some, Vegemite has acquired controversial accolades since it first hit shop shelves in Australia in 1923.
The thick, brown spread was invented in the Victorian capital of Melbourne more than a century ago as an alternative to the British spread Marmite.
It sparked an international dispute in April last year when an Australian cafe owner in Canada was told to remove jars of the condiment from his shelves as it did not comply with local health regulations.
Australia&#x27;s Prime Minister Anthony Albanese intervened in the row, saying it was "rather odd" that Canada allowed the sale of Vegemite&#x27;s "rubbish" rival product Marmite. The cafe owner was later allowed to continue selling the spread.
Australian murderer sues for right to eat Vegemite behind bars

</details>

A man suspected of a deadly gun attack near Paris has left custody and been placed under psychiatric observation, French anti-terrorism officials have said.
One person was killed and five others injured on Friday when an attacker opened fire from his car - first in Trappes, and then in nearby La VerriÃ¨re - appearing to target people of migrant origin.
The suspect, named in French media as 39-year-old Kevin T, will be observed to evaluate whether his "psychiatric health is compatible with pursuing his interrogation", the National Anti-Terrorist Prosecutor&#x27;s Office (PNAT) said.
President Emmanuel Macron called it a "racist terrorist attack", adding that people had been targeted over their skin colour or religion.
It marked a "turning point in the life of our Republic and our nation", the French president said.
Figure caption, Watch: Emergency services at the scene of the shooting on Friday
PNAT said the move to place the suspect under psychiatric evaluation followed guidance from a psychiatric expert.
The evaluation will determine whether the suspect should remain in police custody and is "likely to last 48 hours", PNAT added.
PNAT said the suspect was also found to have cannabis and cocaine in his bloodstream.
French newspaper Le Monde , external and news agency AFP report that a Bible, a Quran and a copy of Adolf Hitler&#x27;s Mein Kampf were found at the suspect&#x27;s home, citing a source close to the investigation.
The lawyer for Pengo Bossio Wangi, the one person killed during the attack, told French media the move to place the suspect under observation was "very rare".
He alleged that it was being used to portray the suspect as criminally irresponsible rather than having acted deliberately.
Wangi, a 26-year-old French-Congolese martial artist, was named as the one fatality on Saturday.
The mixed martial arts club where he trained paid tribute to him, saying: "On the mats as off them, he was one of those who lead by example: present, respectful, always ready to lift others up.
"His smile, his energy and his generosity will remain etched in our walls and in our hearts."
Three of those injured in the attack were taken to hospital in a serious condition - including a 12-year-old girl who remains in a critical condition.
The mayor of La VerriÃ¨re, Nicolas Dainville, told French radio network France Info on Sunday that he had spoken to the girl&#x27;s mother over the phone and confirmed she was "doing better".
Another injured resident was recovering while a third remained in a critical condition, he said.
The suspect had been known to police prior to the attack but not to France&#x27;s intelligence services, local media report. PNAT previously said he had four previous convictions that had been "automatically expunged".
He is thought to have used an old semi-automatic weapon during the attack, firing in bursts from his car window.

### 20. ★★☆☆☆ Gabon says access to social media restored after eight-month ban

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治

- **多源确认**: 1

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Gabon says it has lifted a ban on social media imposed eight months ago - a move that triggered widespread criticism at the time.
The High Communication Authority (HAC) announced the U-turn on Saturday in a televised statement. The BBC has approached the country&#x27;s media regulator for comment.
The ban had been introduced on 17 February, with the HAC saying that online content was fuelling conflict and deepening divisions in the country.
The move came as a shock to the central African nation of about 2.5 million people, where social media platforms such as Facebook, TikTok, YouTube and Instagram have been particularly popular with younger people who use it for business as well as pleasure.
Many turned to VPNs in order to continue accessing social media sites following the ban.
The ban had been imposed at a time when the Gabonese President Brice Oligui Nguema was facing a wave of social unrest less than a year after his election.
Teachers and civil servants had staged strikes over what they said were low pay and poor working conditions.
The opposition said the suspension was an abuse of power.
Nguema won last year&#x27;s elections with more than 90% of the vote, two years after his coup had ended more than five decades of rule by the Bongo family.
At the time he pledged to reform Gabon, a small, oil- and timber-rich country, where digital blackouts were used by the previous governments to control information.
Social media suspended in Gabon for &#x27;spreading of false information&#x27;

### 21. ★★☆☆☆ El Salvador president says bodies found in Haiti are not those of hostages

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T10:55:33+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Nayib Bukele says bodies delivered to soldiers are "not our men" amid search for Salvadorans abducted last week.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Nayib Bukele said the bodies handed to Salvadoran forces in Haiti were "not our men"
Four bodies handed over to special forces on a mission to Haiti to recover Salvadoran hostages are "not our men", El Salvador&#x27;s president has said.
Nayib Bukele said a nongovernmental organisation delivered the human remains to one of Haiti&#x27;s airports claiming they were four El Salvadoran air technicians, but that one of the corpses was a child&#x27;s and another was wearing a different country&#x27;s army uniform.
He had dispatched a military contingent, reportedly of hundreds of soldiers, on Thursday after the four men vanished after being ambushed by "heavily armed criminal groups".
The hostages had been part of the UN-backed Gang Suppression Force (GSF), which is tasked with tackling worsening gang violence in Haiti.
The men had been travelling back to their camp in an armoured vehicle when they were attacked in an area of the Caribbean island nation controlled by armed groups called Croix-des-Bouquets. No gang has so far claimed responsibility.
Bukele previously said the Salvadoran military operation in Haiti had the sole purpose of returning the four men alive. Approximately 450 soldiers had so far been deployed, according to Haitian newspaper Le Nouvelliste.
Bukele said the bodies the mission had received included one of a man of "considerably greater height than that of our air technicians", a person "who was wearing part of a uniform from an army that is not ours", and that among the remains were identification cards and uniforms from other countries.
He posted pictures showing the ID card of a GSF member and a corresponding national ID card from Chad in Africa.
Bukele&#x27;s comments poured skepticism on an earlier report in the New York Times, citing people close to the Salvadoran mission, that the bodies found on the outskirts of the Haitian capital, Port-au-Prince, on Saturday were thought to belong to the missing men.
The newspaper reported that the bodies delivered to the military contingent had been badly burned, meaning a medical examination would be required to identify them.
It cited one unnamed source briefed on the situation as saying the ID posted by Bukele in fact belonged to a soldier who had lost it when he had been injured.
Haiti has been ravaged by gang violence for years, but the situation has worsened in the political vacuum that followed the assassination of its president in 2021.
The multinational force dispatched to quell the violence and push back the armed gangs has struggled to penetrate areas where they hold sway.
More than 5,700 people have been killed or injured by gangs or during operations against them between 1 January and 11 September this year, the UN said at the start of October.
Bukele said the four GSF members had primarily been there to "carry out medical evacuations and save lives, not to wage war".
When they were abducted, helicopters were deployed and multiple attempts were made to reach the location, but these efforts were hampered by fighting in the area, the Salvadoran armed forces said.
El Salvador&#x27;s president threatens Haitian gangs over military hostages
More than 5,000 people killed or injured in Haiti due to gang violence this year, UN says
At least 16 killed and thousands displaced by gang attack in rural Haiti

### 22. ★★☆☆☆ Big, noisy and coming to a town near you - why the data centre backlash is growing

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T23:44:34+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Data centres are the lifeblood of our digital world, but there are growing concerns about their environmental impact. The BBC explores their rise in charts.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

By Daniel Thomas Â  and Â  the Visual Journalism Team
Until a few years ago many of us may have been blissfully unaware of what a data centre was.
These giant, warehouse-like buildings power virtually all our internet activity, from our web searches and emails to movie downloads and online banking.
They are at the heart of the AI boom, housing the advanced computer chips and servers needed to run services like ChatGPT and Claude.
But the speed at which they&#x27;re growing, the enormous amount of electricity they consume and concerns about their environmental impact are fuelling a public backlash around the world.
In the US, they&#x27;ve become a huge issue ahead of the midterm elections on 3 November, with both Republicans and opposition Democrats coming out against their development.
It&#x27;s putting opponents of data centre expansion on a collision course with US President Donald Trump, who has dismissed the pushback as a "sick conspiracy", even as public anger grows.
Here&#x27;s what you need to know about the data centre boom, and why opposition is growing.
There are about 12,250 data centres around the world, according to Data Center Map , external , a company that tracks both operational and pre-operational sites.
That&#x27;s up sharply from 2021, when there were around 8,000 , external operational sites.
As the chart below shows, data centres are most densely clustered in the US which has 4,767 sites. It is followed by Europe, where the UK and Germany lead.
Data centres have typically been located near to cities where it&#x27;s easier to access high voltage power lines or water resources for cooling. But as they get bigger, more are being built in rural areas.
But when it comes to actual capacity, the US is ahead by a much larger margin, with China in second place and Europe lagging far behind.
This gap is set to widen in the coming years, leading some to warn Europe risks missing out on the potential economic benefits of the AI boom , external .
Data centres vary wildly in size, ranging from ones that fit inside a garden shed to others covering vast expanses of land.
The world&#x27;s largest operational data centre is widely reported to be one run by China Telecom in Hohhot, Inner Mongolia, covering an area of approximately one million square metres (10.7 million sq ft). That is equivalent to about 140 Fifa football pitches.
However, much larger sites are under construction as the graphic below shows.
All these sites are being built exclusively to provide the immense computing power needed to run AI services.
For instance, Colossus 2, which is part of the wider Colossus campus, will house hundreds of thousands of advanced AI chips made by tech giant Nvidia.
It will also be used to train xAI&#x27;s chatbot Grok. Elon Musk&#x27;s company will use 41 methane gas turbines to help power the site, which activists say could "significantly increase" local air pollution , external . Local regulators say the turbines will meet air quality standards.
The pressure data centres put on local resources has sparked increasing concern. These facilities can use significant amounts of water to cool their systems, which could lead to water shortages , some experts say.
Data centres worldwide are also thought to consume more electricity than the whole of France, as the chart below shows.
This growing hunger for power is driving up carbon emissions, analysis shows , while putting pressure on local grids, which some fear could push up energy prices .
With others worried about the noise data centres make and the space they take up, a public backlash is gathering steam.
Some 16 US states have proposed laws to ban or temporarily block new facilities, and it could be a key issue in November&#x27;s midterm elections .
Outside the US, opposition to data centres has emerged in countries like Mexico , France, Indonesia and the UK .
But supporters say data centres are critical to national security , and the development of AI.
They also say they are good for the economy , external , attracting investment, generating tax revenue and creating jobs in areas like construction.
A recent report by the Brookings Center , external suggests data centres do create local jobs, although fewer than industry advocates claim.
The type of data centre matters too. So-called hyperscale facilities - massive sites owned and run by tech giants like Amazon and Microsoft - have a bigger positive effect on jobs, it found.
But "colocation" facilities, which lease space to remote tenants without a local presence, brought fewer benefits.
As the debate over their merits intensifies, the data centre rollout continues.
This year, the tech industry is expected to spend a combined $765bn , external ($544bn) on AI infrastructure in 2026, with this topping $1tn next year.
But as concerns about the environmental impact grow, the tech industry is exploring alternatives.
One idea is to put data centres in space , external or on the Moon , where they could harness abundant solar energy.
Others predict that more AI processing could eventually be carried out by the hardware already inside our computers and smartphones , reducing the need for vast data centres altogether.
Additional reporting and design by Erwan Rivault, Matt Faraci and Dominic Bailey.

</details>

### 23. ★★☆☆☆ India's cartoonists built an audience online - now their posts are being restricted

- **分类**: 🇯🇵 亚洲
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T01:09:41+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Indian cartoonists say online restrictions are making satire harder, but they aren't giving up.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Satish Acharya is among Indian cartoonists whose work has been restricted by Meta recently
For the second time in three weeks, Indian cartoonist Satish Acharya found himself effectively invisible on Instagram in his own country.
The 55-year-old discovered on Tuesday that his account had once again been briefly blocked in India, allegedly with no explanation from the platform.
Just a few weeks ago, another cartoonist, Manjul, had found that Instagram had restricted a post criticising India&#x27;s election commission over its controversial revision of voter rolls. His profile remained accessible.
The cases have raised concerns about the use of legal orders to restrict political satire in India, with cartoonists saying they are often given little explanation for why their work has been blocked.
Acharaya&#x27;s account was first restricted from 28 September to 6 October, when users in India could neither find his profile through search nor access it through a web link.
Anyone trying to access his profile saw a message saying it was unavailable under India&#x27;s Information Technology Rules.
The rules allow authorities and courts to order social media platforms to remove or restrict content.
The government says they help tackle unlawful material, but artists and free-speech advocates warn they can stifle India&#x27;s rich tradition of political satire.
"Meta platforms have been restricting the reach of my cartoons for some years," Acharya told the BBC. "But when my entire profile was restricted in India, I was a little sad."
He wrote to Meta, Instagram&#x27;s parent company, but received no reply. He says he was preparing to go to court when his account became accessible again in early October, only to be blocked again earlier this week for three days.
In a statement, Meta told the BBC that it may temporarily restrict accounts to comply with India&#x27;s IT Rules but did not explain the specific reasons for restricting Acharya&#x27;s account or Manjul&#x27;s post.
Image caption, The message on Satish Acharya&#x27;s profile when it was last blocked between 20-22 October
Critics say the restrictions come as humour and satire play an increasingly visible role in political dissent in India, particularly among younger people.
Memes and Gen-Z humour helped fuel protests over education and jobs in July, which led to the resignation of India&#x27;s education minister. Online debate has also intensified over a controversial revision of voter rolls that removed 130 million names.
The political significance of social media has not been lost on the government. Prime Minister Narendra Modi&#x27;s Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) has urged minister s to strengthen their online presence and connect with younger audiences.
At the same time, technology policy expert Prateek Waghre says the government is increasingly using legal orders to regulate online content.
The Indian Express reported , external that government agencies sent nearly 195,000 blocking and content-restriction orders to platforms including Instagram, Facebook and YouTube between March and July through the federal government&#x27;s Sahyog portal.
The figures, obtained through a right-to-information request, do not show how many posts were ultimately restricted or removed. The BBC has not seen the original response.
Waghre says that some platforms, particularly Meta, "seem more willing to comply", he says, while X appears more willing to push back. But limited public data makes comparisons difficult. Meta declined to comment on his assessment.
The government has also reportedly sought greater scrutiny of how social media platforms recommend content.
Earlier this week, several media outlets reported , external that authorities had asked Meta to ensure its systems did not steer users towards "a single or limited set of narratives" and strengthen checks on content that could "cause public disorder or incite violence".
The BBC has sought comment from India&#x27;s IT ministry on the restrictions and reported instructions to Meta but has not received a response.
Image caption, RK Laxman is among the most recognisable political cartoonists in India
Of course, political satire has a long history in India - as do attempts to curb it.
Cartoons have been a fixture of Indian journalism since independence, with RK Laxman &#x27;s Common Man becoming one of its most recognisable characters.
But cartoonists have also faced censorship. During the Emergency in the 1970s, when civil liberties were suspended and the press was tightly controlled, Abu Abraham continued drawing, even as dozens of his cartoons were stamped "not passed by censors".
In 2012, cartoonist Aseem Trived i was arrested on sedition charges over cartoons allegedly mocking the Indian constitution. The charges were later dropped.
Today, concerns about restrictions on political expression extend beyond cartoonists. India ranks 157th in Reporters Without Borders&#x27; World Press Freedom Index, while critics accuse the government of putting growing pressure on independent media. The government rejects the criticism, saying India&#x27;s media remains free and vibrant.
Comedians have also faced controversy over political satire. Kunal Kamra , known for his jokes about Prime Minister Narendra Modi and his party, has faced threats, cancelled shows and legal action.
For cartoonists, however, challenging those in power is central to their work.
"We are among the default opposition," says Sandeep Adhwaryu, a cartoonist at a leading Indian newspaper.
"The nature of the craft is to oppose," he says, adding that cartoonists, like journalists, must question whoever holds power.
Acharya says he has followed that principle throughout his career. Before 2014, many of his cartoons targeted the Congress-led government and were shared by BJP leaders.
"I never faced official restrictions on my cartoons during the previous governments, though there would be some trolling occasionally," he says.
Now, with the BJP in power, much of his satire is directed at Modi&#x27;s government.
Image caption, One of Manjul&#x27;s cartoons which criticised the controversial voter roll revision that has resulted in the removal of 130 million names from the electoral rolls
Social media has allowed independent cartoonists like Acharya to reach audiences without relying on newspapers or other traditional outlets.
"We are not associated with newspapers or any mainstream media. That&#x27;s why we can draw," says Manjul.
But that freedom comes with a new vulnerability - dependence on platforms that can restrict their work or cut off access to their audiences.
No one knows this better than Rachita Taneja, the creator of the hugely popular Instagram comic Sanitary Panels.
"My career would not have existed without the internet," she told the BBC. Yet she describes social media as a double-edged sword.
"At the end of the day it&#x27;s a for-profit endeavour that answers to advertisers," she says.
Taneja says that Meta&#x27;s compliance with Indian government requests reflects the company&#x27;s commercial priorities rather than concern for users&#x27; rights. Meta declined to comment.
Even cartoonists working in traditional newsrooms face pressure. Adhwaryu says artists may soften captions, avoid certain subjects or think twice before submitting a cartoon. Over time, he says, such self-censorship can become normalised, even without explicit instructions.
Still, cartoonists have found ways to support one another.
When Acharya&#x27;s Instagram account was first restricted, Taneja drew a stick-figure cartoon in solidarity. "Seeing people on the front lines creating communities, protesting, running campaigns, tirelessly fighting for their own rights and the rights of others is what keeps me going," she says.
Adhwaryu believes satire will continue to adapt. He compares cartoons and humour to a "shape-shifting virus".
"It keeps mutating in response to its environment," he says. "It will find its way."

</details>

### 24. ★★☆☆☆ Xi Jinping’s corruption crackdown in the spotlight as China prepares for meeting of top leaders

- **分类**: 🇨🇳 中国
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T00:58:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The fifth plenum comes against a backdrop of years of purges that have had some analysts wondering if the issue is corruption or a lack of loyaltyWhen Xi Jinping came to power in 2012, he pledged to stamp out corruption all through the ranks of the Communist party. More than a decade later, the pace of investigations has not slowed. Last year, China’s disciplinary authorities opened investigations into more than 1 million people, including more than 100 officials at or above ministeria

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

People say an oath of loyalty in front of a flag of the Communist party of China on the 105th anniversary of its founding in Beijing in July. Photograph: Kevin Frayer/Getty Images
People say an oath of loyalty in front of a flag of the Communist party of China on the 105th anniversary of its founding in Beijing in July. Photograph: Kevin Frayer/Getty Images
Xi Jinping’s corruption crackdown in the spotlight as China prepares for meeting of top leaders
The fifth plenum comes against a backdrop of years of purges that have had some analysts wondering if the issue is corruption or a lack of loyalty
When Xi Jinping came to power in 2012, he pledged to stamp out corruption all through the ranks of the Communist party. More than a decade later, the pace of investigations has not slowed. Last year, China’s disciplinary authorities opened investigations into more than 1 million people, including more than 100 officials at or above ministerial or provincial level.
As Chinese Communist party (CCP) officials kick off an important meeting this week, discipline – and party loyalty – is high on the agenda. For those who try to read the tea leaves of elite Chinese politics, concern about the apparent lack of discipline is dominating CCP discussions.
The “fifth plenum” began on Monday and is set to focus on “major issues on advancing the party’s full and rigorous self-governance”, according to a Chinese state media. In party-speak, “governance” is a byword for discipline, loyalty and anti-corruption .
Taiwan charges five men over plot to run over comedian for his Xi Jinping jokes
As Xi continues to crack down on corruption, the fact that investigations nearly always result in a conviction begs the question over whether targets are truly guilty of corruption, or are they just political rivals to the people in charge.
Kerry Brown, director of the Lau China Institute at King’s College London, explained that in Xi’s worldview, which is obsessed with discipline and loyalty, there may not be much difference between the two. “If you’re corrupt, you’re not politically loyal. If you’re politically loyal, you’re not corrupt,” Brown said.
He added that in the CCP system, “corruption is not just material things”, but also “spiritual corruption”, which includes political disloyalty.
Some analysts argue that the reason that the anti-graft drive is so enduring is because corruption is a feature rather than a bug of an authoritarian, top-down political system with a sprawling bureaucracy that governs 1.4 billion people.
Neil Thomas, a senior fellow at the Asia Society thinktank, said: “Xi has made the party less corrupt and strengthened his control, but he knows that tougher punishment cannot fully resolve the problem of officials putting their own interests ahead of the party’s.
Xi Jinping at a gathering at the Great Hall of the People in Beijing last week. Photograph: Xinhua/Shutterstock “Xi’s answer is to keep tightening discipline, making anti-corruption a permanent method of governing with no obvious point at which he can declare the job done,” Thomas said.
The job may be never ending, but the plenum is an opportunity to refocus the party’s energy on discipline. The fifth plenum is the fifth of seven gatherings that normally happen in every five-year cycle between CCP congresses. The plenums bring together more than 300 members of the CCP’s Central Committee, the most senior officials in the party hierarchy.
The meeting is particularly significant because it is likely to be the last substantive meeting before next year’s 21st Party Congress, where a new Central Committee will be chosen, and where Xi is expected to be anointed for a fourth term as the head of the CCP.
Eliminating anyone who poses a risk to his authority or to the party’s credibility is a key priority for Xi. And potential threats are everywhere. Nearly 15 years after Xi took office, according to CCP proclamations and a host of recent investigations, the problem is still not stamped out. “It is a deeply ingrained part of [Xi’s] worldview,” said Brown. “Everyone else is corrupt”.
In the past, Xi has warned that the party could “lose its ruling status and inevitably be eliminated by history” if it could not get its house in order. “This is by no means an exaggeration,” he said.
The current focus on party governance comes amid a host of high-profile investigations against senior party and military leaders.
In January, Zhang Youxia , China’s top general and a man second only to Xi in the military hierarchy, was placed under investigation; he was expelled from party last month, having already been removed from the military’s top ruling body, the Central Military Commission (CMC)
A host of other purges means that the CMC, which normally has seven members, has been reduced to just two: Xi and Zhang Shengmin, the CMC’s head of discipline.
Other recent scalps include Ma Xingrui, the former party secretary for Xinjiang, and Huang Qifan, the former mayor of Chongqing.
Huang had long been known as the ultimate survivor in Chinese politics because of his connections to the scandals in the early 2010s in which Xi’s political rival, Bo Xilai, the former Party boss for Chongqing, was embroiled in the death of the British businessman Neil Heywood. Bo was ultimately convicted of bribery and embezzlement, for which he is serving a life sentence, but Huang survived. No longer.
Some China analysts have asked whether the recent purges, which have reached the highest echelons of the Party and military ranks, indicate instability in the regime. But Xi likely sees it differently. Corruption investigations demonstrate who is in charge. Plus, Brown added, they are “the most easy and natural tool by which to enforce unity and discipline”.
