### 25. ★★☆☆☆ Macron accused of putting France’s right to protest at risk with ‘vandal’s law’

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T19:57:40+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Making innocent protesters liable for damage would be bad for rule of law and play into far-right hands, say opponentsEmmanuel Macron has been accused by trade unions of trying to curb the fundamental French right to protest as the government fast-tracks a bill to make protesters pay for damage caused by vandalism at street demonstrations even if they did not directly cause it themselves.The French president, who le

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

A protester throws a projectile towards police after a demonstration against rising living costs in Paris on Saturday. Photograph: Thibault Camus/AP
A protester throws a projectile towards police after a demonstration against rising living costs in Paris on Saturday. Photograph: Thibault Camus/AP
Macron accused of putting France’s right to protest at risk with ‘vandal’s law’
Making innocent protesters liable for damage would be bad for rule of law and play into far-right hands, say opponents
Emmanuel Macron has been accused by trade unions of trying to curb the fundamental French right to protest as the government fast-tracks a bill to make protesters pay for damage caused by vandalism at street demonstrations even if they did not directly cause it themselves.
The French president, who leaves office next spring, has faced more than a month of protests in which high-school students have blockaded schools demanding a response to teacher absences, repairs to decrepit buildings and changes to the university applications platform.
Trade unions and gilets jaunes (yellow vest) anti-government protesters are now joining sustained street demonstrations in a growing social crisis over schools, as well as the cost of living and public services. Authorities have increased security measures and placed restrictions on demonstrating after France saw one of its worst days of violence in years earlier this month when largely peaceful demonstrations were marred by looting, fires and vandalism of state buildings.
The senate will this week debate the government’s proposed “vandals pay” law. The legislation would mean that a person protesting could be held liable to pay up to €10,000 for damage and vandalism even if they did not directly cause it themselves, if they were continuing to demonstrate nearby or as part of the same group as perpetrators. The bill also provides for greater use of drones over protests.
Hundreds of thousands march across France in protests over schools and policing
The prime minister, Sébastien Lecornu, said the bill, which was originally prepared in the wake of violent clashes after Paris Saint-Germain’s victory over Arsenal in the football Champions League in May, was “absolutely essential” after looting and burning during recent demonstrations across France.
The bill comes as the government struggles to contain the current crisis. Marine Le Pen , of the far-right, anti-immigration National Rally (RN), has seen her frontrunner status in next spring’s presidential election boosted as she seeks to reinforce her law-and-order credentials and back police.
Le Pen, who has lodged an appeal to France’s highest court over her conviction for embezzlement of European parliament funds , launched her presidential campaign on Sunday at a rally in Orléans, promising to “bring back order to France”.
The RN has claimed the “vandals pay” bill was originally its idea and Le Pen said she would ensure damage to property was paid for by rioters. Le Pen told a cheering crowd of more than 8,000 people at the rally: “Vandals will pay and if it takes their whole life to pay, then they will spend their whole life paying.”
She said: “We are going to restore the rule of law. Who can claim that there is rule of law when hordes come to ransack and burn our buildings?”
Éric Coquerel, from Jean-Luc Mélenchon ’s radical left La France Insoumise, said on Sunday that the “vandals pay” law “would mean the end of street demonstrations” in France because people would not want to take the risk of being held responsible and made to pay up to €10,000 for vandalism they had not committed. The Communist senator Fabien Gay told the parliamentary TV channel Public Sénat: “No demonstration could take place again in our country.”
In an open letter to Le Monde , leftwing trade unions and France’s Human Rights League wrote: “This unfair law, which is a serious attack on the freedom to demonstrate, should be abandoned, or risk a major step back for the rule of law.”
At this weekend’s protests there was widespread anger at Macron for banning non-union demonstrations that his government deemed “illegitimate” and for keeping all protesters away from city centres.
Although Macron has not put in place a state of emergency, the government and local authorities have introduced limits on demonstrating, including bans on gatherings in front of schools and demonstrating in city centres, as well as curfews in some places and extended periods of police custody for the record number of people arrested.
NFL shutters Paris fan experience as protests overshadow first France game
At demonstrations this weekend, many said they had come out to defend the French right to protest in the street. Street protests in France, which are seen as a fundamental way for people to make themselves heard by a centralised state, have regularly been used to express criticism of changes to the law, from pension changes to same-sex marriage .
Serge Slama, a professor of public law at Université Grenoble Alpes, told Telerama magazine: “Although a state of emergency has been ruled out [by Emmanuel Macron] at this point, we have seen a number of decrees bringing a wide restriction on public liberties.”
He said this was “unprecedented” and would benefit the far right if they won elections next year, allowing them to put in place further crackdowns without changing the law.
Lecornu will meet representatives of protesting high-schoolers on Monday as the government comes under pressure to respond to their demands with funding and school repairs in order to avoid further school blockades.

At their co

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

I would like to rejoin EU, says Burnham, but ‘British public are the bosses’ – UK politics live
PM says talking about changing the relationship with the bloc would not be divisive but ‘can start to bring people together’
Mon 26 Oct 2026 06.34 EDT First published on Mon 26 Oct 2026 04.50 EDT
Drive reindustrialisation from No 10 North or risk failure, MPs tell Burnham
Burnham says budget will confirm &#x27;path&#x27; to higher defence spending
Burnham claims welfare cuts proposed by Tories and Reform UK will just lead to &#x27;more failure and crisis and spending&#x27;
Burnham says Shabana Mahmood &#x27;right&#x27; to want to make settlement for migrant workers &#x27;right to be earned&#x27;
Burnham insists there&#x27;s &#x27;no election coming soon&#x27;, as 2 polls giving Labour 8-point lead revive early election speculation
Burnham claims debate on revising Brexit could &#x27;bring people together&#x27;, and insists rejoining would be public&#x27;s choice, not his
Richard Madeley, left, and Susanna Reid interview Andy Burnham on Good Morning Britain on Monday Photograph: ITV
Richard Madeley, left, and Susanna Reid interview Andy Burnham on Good Morning Britain on Monday Photograph: ITV
Drive reindustrialisation from No 10 North or risk failure, MPs tell Burnham
Burnham says budget will confirm &#x27;path&#x27; to higher defence spending
Burnham claims welfare cuts proposed by Tories and Reform UK will just lead to &#x27;more failure and crisis and spending&#x27;
Burnham says Shabana Mahmood &#x27;right&#x27; to want to make settlement for migrant workers &#x27;right to be earned&#x27;
Burnham insists there&#x27;s &#x27;no election coming soon&#x27;, as 2 polls giving Labour 8-point lead revive early election speculation
Burnham claims debate on revising Brexit could &#x27;bring people together&#x27;, and insists rejoining would be public&#x27;s choice, not his
Andy Burnham’s pledge to reindustrialise Britain is doomed to fail unless it is driven decisively from his new No 10 North outpost in Manchester, a group of more than 90 backbench Labour MPs is warning. Heather Stewart has the story.
Drive reindustrialisation from No 10 North or risk failure, MPs tell Burnham
Andy Burnham has said the budget will set out a “path” to higher defence spending.
The government has signed up to a Nato commitment to raise defence spending to 3.5% of GDP by 2035. John Healey, the chancellor, has also in the past, when he was defence secretary, stressed the importance of the UK raising defence spending to 3% of GDP by 2030, but that is not a commitment he has firmly promised in his new job because he has not yet identified how that would be funded.
Asked in his ITV interview if Healey would set out the route to 3% by 2030 on Wednesday, Burnham at first just said he would set out “the path to meet our Nato commitments”. That appeared to be a reference just to the 3.5% target.
But when Burnham was asked if he was referring to both the 3% and the 3.5% targets, Burnham said he was. He said the budget would set out “a plan as to how we think is the right way to do that”.
Burnham also said his priority for this budget has been finding the £5bn a year needed to fund the defence investment plan announced by Keir Starmer before he resigned.
It was not clear from the interview whether Burnham’s reference to a “path” to higher defence spending meant a detailed fiscal plan, or just a statement of intent. Ministers have already expressed their wish to meet these targets; they just have not committed to the funding plans that would make them happen.
Giving evidence to the Commons defence committee earlier this month, Wes Streeting , the defence secretary, said he had always expected the detailed pathway to 3% to be set out in the spending review next year.
Andy Burnham has claimed that the welfare changes he is planning to get more young people into work could over time save a “significant amount of money”.
Ahead of the budget, the Conservatives and Reform UK are both calling for massive cuts in welfare spending, which they say could be used in part to fund more defence investment. At their conference the Tories identified welfare cuts worth £36bn a year, and today Reform UK is saying it would cut welfare spending by £40bn a year.
Burnham has repeatedly rejected what he calls “crude cuts”. But the government will soon publish recommendations from the Alan Milburn review into Neets (young people not in education, employment or training), and it is expected to recommend more spending on support getting young people into work, offset by some cuts to benefits. Milburn has said this would cost more in the short term, but would save money over time.
In his interview on ITV’s Good Morning Britain, Burnham said:
I think a big change is needed here where we move to a situation where the spending is not to sustain people on benefits, but we give people the right support when they need it as they are growing up, coming through their teenage years.
Asked how much this could save, he replied: “A significant amount of money, and that’s what the Milburn review is all about.”
Burnham also said the cuts proposed by the Tories and Reform UK would just lead to “more failure and crisis and spending”. He said:
The opposition parties are out there today saying just make crude cuts to the welfare budget.
I personally think that won’t work, and it isn’t right to do that anyway, because it just leads to more and more failure and crisis and spending that isn’t helpful for anybody.
Andy Burnham on ITV's Good Morning Britain. Photograph: ITV Andy Burnham has said that Shabana Mahmood is right to want to make migrant workers wait longer until they can claim indefinite leave to remain (ILR) in the UK.
Under plans announced last year , migrant workers would normally have to wait 10 years for ILR, instead of five years as they do now, and care workers would normally have to wait 15 years. Some cabinet ministers have made it clear they believe 15 years is too long.
Asked about the split, Burnham broadly defended the home secretary in his Good Morning Britain interview. He said:
Why has this problem arisen? It’s arisen because if you look back to the early 2020s, people call it the Boriswave, a huge number of people came in seeking permanent settlement on longer-term visas …
And the home secretary is right, in my view, to bring forward changes that are based on the principle that settlement is a right to be earned, not just something that is granted automatically.
Burnham said the final plans would be published soon.
But, when asked if he was sticking to the 15-year element of the plan, Burnham just said that he stood by “the principle” of what Mahmood was proposing. He also said the government would take into account the results of its consultation on the plans.
In fact, Burnham himself is one of the ministers who have signalled they are unhappy about care workers being singled out for particularly harsh treatment. And Mahmood has already indicated that some aspects of the plan announced last year will be revised in response to objections.
Burnham was not asked for detail on what a compromise might look like.
In his Good Morning Britain interview, Andy Burnham was twice asked about an early election.
At the start, Susanna Reid asked him if he was softening people up for an early election. Burnham replied:
Asked if that was absolutely the case, he said it was. He said he had made that clear before the Labour conference. He went on:
I just don’t think people want another backward-looking squabble. I think they want me to get on with the job.
And, when Reid raised the subject again at the end of the interview, Burnham said:
My mind is not on that [an early election]. I don’t know if that is coming over here, but it genuinely is not on that. There is no election coming anytime soon. People may as well just put that out of their mind.
Reid did not ask specifically about polling, but two polls came out over the weekend both giving Labour an eight-point lead.
📊Labour on 32% with a lead of +8 – the largest Labour lead Ipsos have recorded this parliament
📊Conservatives take second place the first time since June 2025
Polling. Photograph: Ipsos And this is from Opinium.
🚨 Labour continues to lead in the polls, 8 points ahead of Reform in second place.
Polling. Photograph: Opinium These polls have attracted a lot of attention in part because they both give Labour a bigger lead than other recent polls. The Politico poll of polls , which averages the results of all polls, has Labour just four points ahead of Reform UK in second place. But that has not been updated to take into account the most recent Ipsos and Opinium numbers.
In one pre-conference interview, Burnham said that he would not hold the next election until 2029. But mostly he just rules out an “early” general election, while leaving it unclear what “early” means.
At Westminster no one is expecting an election before the end of the year. But the weekend polls have revived speculation about Burnham being tempted to call one in May next year, not least because having the rightwing vote split almost 50/50 between the Conservatives and Reform UK is an ideal backdrop for Labour.
Good morning. Andy Burnham started budget week with an interview on ITV’s Good Morning Britain with Susanna Reid and Richard Madeley. There were plenty of decent lines in it, but some of his most intriguing comments were about Brexit. Yesterday opposition parties (and even in private some Labour MPs ) were criticising the PM for saying in a Sunday Times interview that he would be willing to consider going into the next election proposing accepting EU free movement rules. This was treated as if it was a novel outrage – even though at Labour conference Burnham announced that rejoining the EU could end up as the best option to emerge from his proposed renegotiation of the Brexit settlement, which of course would automatically involve accepting free movement.
Neil Kinnock said recently he thought Burnham was determined to take Britain back in the EU, but in his interview this morning Burnham insisted that he was not dogmatic in that way. When Madeley asked him if he saw himself as someone “destined” to be the PM that would take Britain back in, Burnham replied:
I see it as my job to be pragmatic about the whole situation. Can I make it work better? Can I add growth to the British economy?
And when Reid asked him bluntly to be honest and say if he wanted the UK to rejoin, Burnham replied:
I’ve said – I’m going to be honest – in my lifetime I would like to see that happen, but I am not going to move ahead of the public. I am not going to dictate in terms of my own opinions here. You have to make sure that the British public are the bosses here.
When it was put to him that he had broken a promise he made during the Makerfield byelection campaign not to reopen the Brexit referendum campaign, Burnham said that was not what he was doing.
He repeated the argument he made in his Labour conference speech – that it was worth revising the relationship with the EU because the current, post-Brexit settlement was not working.
But he also claimed that, rather than being divisive, this new debate could bring people together. Later in the interview, he returned to Brexit unprompted.
And [to] go back to Brexit, by just talking about it in a more practical way, by being honest about it … that can start to bring people together who voted in different ways.
The divides that we’ve lived through in the last decade really do need to end, and we need to pull people together …
I’m trying to give the country a direction and there is no way I can do that without coming back to the relationship with Europe and saying, ‘Look, here are some options as to how we got this right.’
Not for the first time, Burnham categorically ruled out trying to rejoin the EU before the election.
And, on the timing of the election, Burnham also insisted that it would be “no time soon”. He went on:
My mind is not on that [an early election]. I don’t know if that is coming over here, but it genuinely is not on that. There is no election coming anytime soon. People may as well just put that out of their mind.
Morning: Andy Burnham is on a visit in London ahead of attending a summit on rough sleeping in Downing Street.
2pm: Nigel Farage, the Reform UK leader, and Robert Jenrick, his Treasury spokesperson, hold a press conference to present what they call their “economic rescue plan”.
2.30pm: Shabana Mahmood , the home secretary, takes questions in the Commons.
I’m afraid we are no longer able to have comments open below the line every day on this blog. If you want to contact me, please email politics.live@theguardian.com. You can use this email to flag up corrections, comments for publication, questions or complaints.

Yohannes Lowe (now) with Adam Fulton and Marina Dunbar (earlier)
Mon 26 Oct 2026 06.15 EDT First published on Sun 25 Oct 2026 15.00 EDT

Supporters of Brazilian president Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva react in Rio de Janeiro after his win against Flávio Bolsonaro in Brazil’s presidential election runoff. Photograph: Raquel Cunha/Reuters
Supporters of Brazilian president Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva react in Rio de Janeiro after his win against Flávio Bolsonaro in Brazil’s presidential election runoff. Photograph: Raquel Cunha/Reuters
It has just gone past 06:15am in Brasília and 09:15am in London, where I am blogging from. Here is a quick recap of the main updates from one of the most significant and bruising elections in Brazilian history:
Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva , the 80-year-old Brazilian president, has staged a dramatic election comeback , defeating his far-right opponent, Flávio Bolsonaro , to claim a historic fourth term in power.
Lula secured 51.79% of the votes compared with Bolsonaro’s 48.21% , according to Brazil’s Superior Electoral Court – about 4.4m more votes
Flávio Bolsonaro, the eldest son of former President Jair Bolsonaro , refused to accept the election result, and has called for it to be annulled .
The 45-year-old senator called into question a late-Sunday decision by a Lula-appointed supreme court justice that extended voting by two hours over alleged voter transport irregularities.
His refusal to concede defeat has echoes of his father’s reaction after losing to Lula in 2022 . Jair Bolsonaro never accepted his loss and was later sentenced by the supreme court to 27-years in prison for plotting a coup to remain in power.
Lula, who highlighted a track record of falling poverty and inequality during his campaign, becomes the first president in Brazilian history to win a fourth term.
It is also the first time, since Brazil’s return to democracy in 1988, that a candidate who lost the first round of voting went on to overturn the result and win the presidency. Lula’s new term is set to begin on 5 January 2027 .
The leftist president’s victory is a blow for the US president, who had been hoping Bolsonaro would become the latest in a series of pro-Trump leaders to take power in South America.
Numerous world leaders have congratulated Lula for his dramatic election win, but so far Donald Trump has remained silent.
You can read more in this insightful recap from my colleagues, Tom Phillips and Tiago Rogero :
Lula wins historic fourth term as Brazilian president in defeat of far-right Flávio Bolsonaro

We will change the way we run this country, Lula says in victory speech

Could highly organised pop fans clinch victory for Brazil’s Lula?
Exclusive: Ex-Lula environmental official warns of Amazon destruction if Flávio Bolsonaro wins presidency
A scandal engulfing Brazil’s top court could return the Bolsonaros to power
Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva v Flávio Bolsonaro: Brazil presidential runoff to decide direction of nation
The Kremlin has welcomed Lula’s election victory, saying the leftist president had done “a lot” to develop ties with Russia.
“We welcome Mr Lula’s victory. He does a lot, not only for the development of bilateral relations but also for advancing processes within the framework of Brics,” Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov told reporters.
Brazil has maintained diplomatic and economic ties with Russia despite Moscow’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine four years ago.
Both countries were among the earliest members of Brics (alongside India, China and South Africa), a grouping, established in 2009, that was conceived as a counterweight to western dominance.
Even though Flávio Bolsonaro has lost this election, my colleague Libby Brooks has looked at why the far-right 45-year-old senator struck a chord with so many voters across Brazil:
Evangelical Christianity has expanded across Brazil in recent decades, with church leaders throwing their support behind the socially conservative policies favoured by Bolsanaro Sr and Jr.
Pro-gun Flavio Bolsonaro harnessed voter frustration at chronic levels of urban crime and violence. Despite being embedded in Brazilian politics for decades, he successfully portrayed himself as an anti-establishment outsider, using social media far more effectively than his rival.
And others believe he had significant help: a dozen watchdogs and research groups have published reports alleging that Meta may have favoured the senator’s campaign.
The controversy grew after Brazils’ electoral court ordered Meta not to give the candidates “unequal treatment”, while the company called the allegations “false and unfounded”.
Brazil keeps left: what Lula’s victory means for his country and beyond
Brazilians protested outside Meta’s headquarters in São Paulo last week, alleging the tech firm restricted Lula’s reach. Photograph: Nelson Almeida/AFP/Getty Images Here is some footage of Lula’s victory speech, in which he thanked the Brazilian youth for rallying support for his cause, and of Bolsonaro refusing to concede defeat as he vows to pursue legal action and have the election result annulled:
Lula re-elected for fourth term as Bolsonaro refuses to accept Brazil election result – video
It has just gone past 06:15am in Brasília and 09:15am in London, where I am blogging from. Here is a quick recap of the main updates from one of the most significant and bruising elections in Brazilian history:
Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva , the 80-year-old Brazilian president, has staged a dramatic election comeback , defeating his far-right opponent, Flávio Bolsonaro , to claim a historic fourth term in power.
Lula secured 51.79% of the votes compared with Bolsonaro’s 48.21% , according to Brazil’s Superior Electoral Court – about 4.4m more votes
Flávio Bolsonaro, the eldest son of former President Jair Bolsonaro , refused to accept the election result, and has called for it to be annulled .
The 45-year-old senator called into question a late-Sunday decision by a Lula-appointed supreme court justice that extended voting by two hours over alleged voter transport irregularities.
His refusal to concede defeat has echoes of his father’s reaction after losing to Lula in 2022 . Jair Bolsonaro never accepted his loss and was later sentenced by the supreme court to 27-years in prison for plotting a coup to remain in power.
Lula, who highlighted a track record of falling poverty and inequality during his campaign, becomes the first president in Brazilian history to win a fourth term.
It is also the first time, since Brazil’s return to democracy in 1988, that a candidate who lost the first round of voting went on to overturn the result and win the presidency. Lula’s new term is set to begin on 5 January 2027 .
The leftist president’s victory is a blow for the US president, who had been hoping Bolsonaro would become the latest in a series of pro-Trump leaders to take power in South America.
Numerous world leaders have congratulated Lula for his dramatic election win, but so far Donald Trump has remained silent.
You can read more in this insightful recap from my colleagues, Tom Phillips and Tiago Rogero :
Lula wins historic fourth term as Brazilian president in defeat of far-right Flávio Bolsonaro
Amid the cacophony of congratulatory messages, it is notable that we are yet to hear Donald Trump ’s reaction to the Brazilian election result.
Lula, a prominent Trump critic, had warned Washington not to interfere in the election and insisted Brazil would “never fit into anyone’s backyard”.
His victory cements Latin America’s most populous country and largest economy as a counterweight to the US, which has enormous influence across the region.
In today’s First Edition newsletter, my colleague Libby Brooks examines the role Trump played in the election. Here is a snippet:
Although Donald Trump did not openly endorse Flávio Bolsonaro, his influence loomed large. Last year, he denounced the supreme court trial of his ally Jair Bolsonaro as a “witch-hunt” , imposing extraordinary 50% tariffs on Brazilian imports as retribution for what he claimed was political persecution.
Lula has styled himself as the country’s defender against Trump’s “colonial” designs. His victory consolidates a multilateral foreign policy and pragmatic approach to relations with the US, China and Russia. He has rejected invitations to join the controversial coalition of Latin American countries conducting military operations against drug cartels alongside the Pentagon, which would have resulted in much greater US involvement on Brazilian soil.
Brazil keeps left: what Lula’s victory means for his country and beyond
Donald Trump holds a bilateral meeting with Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva at Kuala Lumpur Convention Centre in Malaysia on 26 October 2025. Photograph: Andrew Harnik/Getty Images Lula’s win is encouraging for leftwing politicians trying to fight the ascendant right, including in Europe where Spanish prime minster, Pedro Sánchez , is trailing in polls ahead of his November election.
“A great victory for Lula! What has happened in Brazil will happen in Spain. The global progressive comeback has just begun,” Sánchez, one of the few leftist leaders in Europe, posted on X .
Earlier this month, the socialist prime minister, who has been in office since 2018, staked his political future on a snap general election called after rightwing parties torpedoed emergency legislation to tackle the housing crisis.
French President Emmanuel Macron also congratulated his Brazilian counterpart on his re-election, calling for efforts to “strengthen our strategic partnership”.
“France and Brazil have much to build together,” Macron wrote in a post on X , saying their two countries should nurture their friendship “in the service of a fairer world”.
We are seeing more messages of support pour in for Lula after his historic election victory.
China’s foreign ministry spokesperson, Guo Jiakun , was among those to congratulate the president, telling reporters that Beijing was “willing to work with Brazil to continue advancing the building of a China-Brazil community with a shared future.”
Jiakun said that China, which is Brazil’s largest trading partner, “noted” Lula’s victory as was confirmed by Brazil’s Superior Electoral Court (unlike Bolsonaro who has refused to recognise the result).
As we have been reporting, Flávio Bolsonaro defied opinion polls by securing a narrow lead over the leftist president Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva in the first round of voting held on 4 October. But Lula managed to make a dramatic comeback after reigniting his campaign between the first and second rounds.
The Guardian’s South America correspondent, Tiago Rogero , has looked at how Lula’s campaign gained ground after a massive social media push and enthusiastic street protests rallied support around the incumbent leader. Here is an extract from the report :
Bolsonaro began the runoff campaign with a lead and the support of most other defeated candidates, but instead of growing, he stagnated in the polls while Lula gained support.
Many analysts saw this as the result of part of the population suddenly waking up to the “fear” of a Bolsonaro returning to power, as well as controversies including the fact that the senator is under investigation in two federal police inquiries over alleged corruption and money laundering.
Lula, by contrast, radically changed his digital strategy and benefited from student-led protests across the country, most of which did not even involve the president.
Protest in support Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva’s re-election bid, in Brasília, on 13 October 2026. Photograph: Adriano Machado/Reuters In case you’re just joining us, here is a rundown on what’s happened in Brazil’s presidential election runoff – it is 4am in São Paulo.
Leftwing president Luiz Inácio Lula da ⁠Silva pulled off a dramatic comeback to defeat far-right senator Flávio Bolsonaro in the second round of the election vote.
Lula won a historic fourth term on Sunday in a victory – declared by the country’s electoral court – that put the brakes on a wave of rightwing election wins across Latin America.
Lula defeated Bolsonaro , the son of jailed former president ⁠Jair Bolsonaro, by more than 4 million votes after narrowly losing the first round of voting.
Flávio Bolsonaro, 45, said it was “impossible to concede the election result” and that his legal team would seek to have the election annulled due to a supreme court ruling that extended voting for two hours.
Lula, who turns 81 on Tuesday, told supporters in São Paulo after his victory: “We are going to change the way that this country is governed … This election was a defeat for denialism … I want to thank God and to thank you, Brazilian men and women of this country … it was democracy that made this happen.” Vice-president Geraldo Alckmin told the crowd: “You saved Brazilian democracy for the second time.”
Luiz Inácio Lula da ⁠Silva addresses supporters in São Paulo after winning Brazil’s presidential runoff. Photograph: Isaac Fontana/EPA
Lula, of the Workers’ party (PT), received 51.79% of the ballots, or 64,256,232 votes. Bolsonaro, of the Liberal party (PL), had 48.21% or 59,826,041. Pollsters said a better-than-expected turnout helped power Lula’s win.
The victory marks another of his comebacks . Lula was jailed from 2018 to 2019 on corruption charges that were later overturned, paving the way for him to ‌beat Jair Bolsonaro in the 2022 election. Jair Bolsonaro was later given a 27-year sentence for plotting a coup to remain in power. He was hoping to be freed and given a role in government had his son won this election.
Lula’s win is also a blow for Donald Trump that deprives the US president of a major addition to what has been a growing bloc of pro-Trump leaders to take power in Latin America, including in Colombia, Ecuador, Chile and Argentina.
Mexican president Claudia Sheinbaum said of Lula’s win: “Long live democracy, the people of Brazil won.” Cuban president Miguel Díaz-Canel congratulated Lula on “this historic ​victory that ensures ​the continuity of the ​path of peace, sovereignty and social justice embarked upon ​by the ‌Brazilian people ​under your leadership”. EU chief Ursula von der Leyen said: “Democracy and multilateralism have won in Brazil.” Canadian prime minister Mark Carney said the Canada-Brazil “partnership” had flourished and “let’s seize this opportunity to make it even stronger, together”. Other leaders’ reactions can be seen in posts here and here .
Lula’s reelection win has set up ⁠a test for financial markets , with investors expecting recent bets on a change of government to unwind in a volatile week ahead. November futures for the Brazilian real were down 10% in Asian hours while a Tokyo-listed exchange-traded fund tracking Brazil’s Bovespa index fell nearly 15% in Asia trade.
Ursula von der Leyen has praised Lula for his election win and said Europe will “remain by your side for the years to come”.
The head of the European Union said in a message posted on X :
Congratulations to my friend @LulaOficial on his electoral victory. Democracy and multilateralism have won in Brazil. Europe’s partnership with Brazil is thriving.
We will remain by your side for the years to come. Together we will keep working closely for free and open trade. A rules-based global order. And strong ambitions for our climate.
There’s more from the crowds parading down São Paulo’s Paulista Avenue with a mixture of ecstasy and relief in celebration of Lula’s surprise election victory.
Cheers of “Let’s go, Lula!” rose from the crowd again and again. The leftwing president – who turns 81 on Tuesday – joined the parade, leaning out of the sunroof of a car with a megaphone in his hand and saying:
I just had to tell you that I love you. Tomorrow, the fight continues.
“I feel so happy!” 43-year-old Anna Zanatta said before bursting into tears of joy. “Lula’s victory represents many things for us: freedom, our democracy, our history,” she said, waving a flag bearing the message “Antifascist Action”.
Lula supporters make an ‘L’ sign as they watch him speak in São Paulo’s Paulista Avenue after his win. Photograph: Brian Snyder/Reuters Zanatta viewed a hypothetical Bolsonaro victory as a “threat” to Latin America’s largest democracy.
“I was born under a dictatorship, and I promised my granddaughter that we Brazilians would never go through that again,” she said, referring to the military regime that ruled from 1964 to 1985.
Agence France-Presse also quoted doorman Alfonso dos Reis , 64, who carried a sign reading “Lula again with the strength of the people” alongside a photo of the incumbent president.
“We’re going to build a much better country,” he beamed.
Lula supporters celebrate in São Paulo. Photograph: Isaac Fontana/EPA Lula supporters have poured into Brazilian streets after his election win and the defeat of rival far-right candidate Flávio Bolsonaro .
In São Paulo, the Guardian’s Tom Phillips reports that crowds are celebrating in a street party that is set to go until dawn to mark Lula’s historic victory.
Outside Bolsonaro’s mansion in Brasília, the Guardian’s Tiago Rogero reports that the senator has declared it “impossible to concede Lula’s victory” and vowed to seek the election’s annulment.
Lula supporters celebrate in São Paulo. Photograph: Brian Snyder/Reuters Lula ’s reelection win has set up ⁠a test for financial markets, with investors expecting recent bets on a change of government to unwind in a volatile week ahead.
Markets had moved sharply in anticipation of a rightwing shift ⁠since Flávio Bolsonaro unexpectedly finished first in the election’s opening round three weeks ago. Stocks closed on Friday almost 7% above their level going into the first round, while the real ⁠currency was nearly 5% stronger against the US dollar.
Interest-rate futures had indicated expectations that tighter public finances would allow borrowing costs to decline, Reuters reports.
“We expect a sharp market reaction on Monday, with aggressive repricing, a ⁠selloff in assets and interest rate futures likely to hit their upper limit shortly after the open,” said Luis Felipe Vital , head ​of macro strategy at Warren Investimentos.
Over the next four years, ‌Lula must slow the growth ‌of Brazil’s debt while protecting social programs and investment.
“We expect the rally that followed the first round to be largely priced out, and sentiment to stay weak until a clear fiscal plan emerges,” said Alejo Czerwonko , chief investment officer for emerging markets at UBS Global Wealth Management.

### 28. ★★☆☆☆ Ukraine disputes Elon Musk’s claim that its military violated Starlink terms during drone operations

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T18:18:49+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Ukraine challenged Musk’s claim that its military violated Starlink terms, arguing that commercial Starlink limits prevented medium- and long-range strike use.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Ukraine disputes Musk claim that military violated Starlink terms
A Ukrainian defense technology adviser disputed Elon Musk’s claim that the military violated Starlink&#x27;s terms, saying geographic and speed limits made the alleged strike physically impossible.
Musk said Ukraine used Starlink for offensive drone strikes in violation of the service’s terms and that SpaceX closed the alleged loophole last week.
Ukraine and European partners are exploring sovereign satellite communications options, including an Oct. 22 Nokia-ICEYE letter of intent with Ukraine’s Defense Ministry.
Soldiers from a separate task rifle battalion of the National Police of Ukraine set up a Starlink satellite communication terminal at a combat position in the Donetsk region, Ukraine, on Oct. 16, 2026.
A Ukrainian official disputed SpaceX CEO Elon Musk&#x27;s claim that Ukraine&#x27;s military violated Starlink&#x27;s terms of service, prompting the company to restrict Ukraine&#x27;s access to the high-speed satellite technology.
Serhii Beskrestnov, a defense technology advisor to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy , wrote an online post on Sunday contesting Musk&#x27;s reason for temporarily throttling Ukraine&#x27;s access to high-speed service from SpaceX&#x27;s Starlink satellite communication systems.
Ukrainian officials previously said the restrictions disrupted the country&#x27;s military drone services and left the country vulnerable to Russian attacks .
Musk on Saturday claimed in a post on X that the "Ukraine military violated Starlink terms of service and used it for offensive drone strikes to kill people."
Beskrestnov pushed back on Musk&#x27;s allegation, arguing that pre-existing restrictions on the systems would have made it "physically impossible" for Ukraine to use Starlink in the types of strikes the SpaceX CEO described.
"It is very strange that Elon Musk is unaware of the operational limitations of Starlink in Ukraine," Beskrestnov wrote in a post on Telegram , noting that Starlink&#x27;s civilian system in Ukraine "does not function over the territory of Russia" and that Ukrainian drones operating with Starlink have had a speed restriction of 70 to 90 km/h (roughly 43 to 56 miles per hour) for months.
"Consequently, it is PHYSICALLY IMPOSSIBLE for us to use commercial Starlink for deep-strike or medium-range strike operations," Beskrestnov wrote.
Ukraine officials acknowledged on Saturday that high-speed service for SpaceX&#x27;s Starlink and Starshield satellite communications systems had been restored. The country has blamed that restricted service for disrupting its drone air defense capabilities during frequent Russian air attacks.
Starshield is SpaceX&#x27;s military-focused satellite network for government and national security use, built on technology developed for Starlink, the company&#x27;s broader commercial satellite internet network.
Before Musk acknowledged that he had restricted the systems, confusion and speculation abounded over who ordered the high-speed service changes.
At one point, Musk wrote in a social media post that the U.S. government was behind the decision, though President Donald Trump claimed he had no knowledge of the decision . Ukrainian officials have also said Starlink or the U.S. had imposed the restrictions without warning, leaving the country blindsided.
Musk has not explained the reported lack of notification to Ukraine before his company implemented the restrictions.
Musk and SpaceX did not respond to CNBC&#x27;s requests for comment.
The restrictions on Starlink and Starshield in war-torn Ukraine have driven Ukraine and its allies to seek or build alternative, "sovereign" satellite communication systems.
Poland&#x27;s Deputy Minister of National Defense, Cezary Tomczyk, discussed his country&#x27;s plans on a podcast called Influence Zone (Strefa Influenceów) earlier this month. Ukraine also announced partnerships with Norway and Finland&#x27;s mobile giant Nokia , as well as satellite maker ICEYE, to develop alternatives.
Beyond Ukraine, several countries are also exploring sovereign systems. The European Union is building IRIS² , a constellation of hundreds of satellites, but it doesn&#x27;t expect its first launches until 2029.
SpaceX&#x27;s Starlink generated $5.25 billion in revenue in the third quarter of 2026, up from $3.1 billion a year earlier. Revenue from consumer Starlink subscribers totaled $3.12 billion, while the company generated $2.13 billion from government and enterprise customers during the quarter.
A wide array of customers, including non-government entities, the governments of Poland and France, and the Pentagon, have paid SpaceX to provide its Starlink and Starshield hardware and wireless internet services to Ukraine.

The extraordinary suit,

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Blanche defends DOJ lawsuit to block Republican loss
Attorney General Todd Blanche defended the Justice Department&#x27;s lawsuit seeking to block certification of Alaska&#x27;s Senate election if Republican incumbent Sen. Dan Sullivan narrowly loses.
Blanche said the DOJ is trying to ensure Alaska can determine the correct victor if litigation plays out after the election.
The extraordinary suit, which centers on two candidates named Dan Sullivan, has raised concerns that the Trump administration may be meddling in the midterm elections.
Acting U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche (C) leaves after a meeting with Sen. Bill Cassidy (R-LA) and Sen. Lisa Murkowski (R-AK) at the U.S. Capitol on Aug. 5, 2026 in Washington, DC.
Attorney General Todd Blanche defended the Justice Department&#x27;s  lawsuit  on Sunday seeking to block certification of Alaska&#x27;s Senate election if Republican incumbent Sen. Dan Sullivan narrowly loses.
The lawsuit, filed last week, cites what it argues is likely voter confusion over the name Daniel J. Sullivan, a retired teacher also on the ballot. The DOJ said confusion over the two Sullivans could help former Alaska Democratic Rep. Mary Peltola , who is narrowly ahead in polls just over a week from Election Day, win the election.
"What we&#x27;re asking the court to do is make sure that after the election, if it&#x27;s clear that there was confusion, if it&#x27;s clear, and that&#x27;s for a court to decide at a later date if there&#x27;s litigation ... and that confusion led to an unjust, improper result that is not the will of the Alaskan voter, we want to make sure that the Alaskan system and the Alaskan state courts and the Alaskan electoral process can understand that in a way that lets them do the right thing," Blanche said on CNN&#x27;s "State of the Union."
"Never before have opponents gone to such lengths to try and thwart and cheat in an election as they&#x27;re doing in this case," he said.
The DOJ&#x27;s lawsuit says that the potential for voter confusion exists and Alaska should not certify the election if "(1) Senator Sullivan does not win the election; and (2) had all votes cast for Daniel J. Sullivan been cast instead for Senator Sullivan and Alaska&#x27;s rank-choice process played out accordingly, Senator Sullivan would have won the election."
Alaska applies a top-four primary system to select candidates for the general election. The November general election uses ranked-choice voting.
Ranked-choice voting lets voters rank candidates in order of preference. If no candidate receives a majority of votes in the first round of counting, the candidate with the fewest votes is eliminated. Voters who ranked the eliminated candidate first then have their second choice counted and added to that candidate&#x27;s total. This continues until a majority winner — or the candidate with more than half of the vote — wins.
This combo shows U.S. Sen. Dan Sullivan, left, in Palmer, Alaska, on Aug. 17, 2026 and a candidate in Alaska&#x27;s U.S. Senate race, Dan J. Sullivan, right, in Petersburg, Alaska, on July 3, 2026.
Republicans have argued for months that Daniel J. Sullivan is a Democratic plant intended to confuse voters and have sought to remove him from the ballot. Democrats have maintained they have no connection to the candidate, and Sullivan himself has denied running to confuse voters.
The Alaska Division of Elections briefly removed Daniel J. Sullivan in June, before a state court ordered his name returned to the ballot. The state Supreme Court upheld that decision.
President Donald Trump , who has long made claims of voter fraud without evidence, said he ordered the DOJ&#x27;s lawsuit himself.
Democratic groups and voting rights advocates, including Peltola&#x27;s campaign, are seeking to join the lawsuit to stop the DOJ&#x27;s effort to block certification of the election if Sen. Sullivan loses. The groups argue the executive branch lacks the legal authority to intervene in state-run elections and is attempting an unprecedented override of state election administration, with early voting already underway.
"In an unprecedented pre-emptive strike on democratic elections, the Department of Justice—acting on the President&#x27;s personal orders—demands that this Court enjoin the certification of the ongoing U.S. Senate election, but only if the President&#x27;s preferred candidate loses," the motion to intervene reads. "But the federal government has no power to impose that Hobson&#x27;s choice on the people of this State."
Rep. Mary Peltola, Alaska, poses for a picture in her Cannon Building office on Thursday, February 9, 2023.
Alaska is seen as one of the best chances for a Democratic flip in the Nov. 3 elections, which will determine control of Congress for the remainder of Trump&#x27;s term. Trump has been barnstorming competitive states in recent weeks as the polls favor Democrats to win control of the House of Representatives and potentially flip the Senate.
Democrats and voting rights advocates have long feared that Trump — who denied his election loss to former President Joe Biden in 2020, leading to a violent riot at the U.S. Capitol on Jan. 6, 2021 — would attempt to meddle in the midterm contest to save narrow Republican majorities in Congress.
Blanche bristled on Sunday when asked whether the lawsuit aimed to sway the election in Alaska toward Republicans.
"That is crazy," Blanche said, pressed on CNN about whether the president is laying the groundwork for a "doomsday" scenario in which he tries to overturn election results.
"We&#x27;re not laying any groundwork for the president to do that ... the groundwork that we are laying is for the state of Alaska, if it becomes an issue after the election, will have the ability to determine whether there was confusion that violates the law in determining who the senator should be."

- **发生了什么**: With industrial action over job cuts heading into its 21st week, students at famed London college are unhappyStudents arriving at Goldsmiths, University of London this autumn found a campus embroiled in a bitter industrial dispute that has upended timetables and disrupted courses.“I was really excited to start my studies at Goldsmiths,” one international student told the Guardian at a gathering of unhappy students on Friday. “But when I first arrived, I was quite shocked because

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Goldsmiths students made their feelings known in Deptford, south-east London, on Friday. Photograph: Lily Renshaw/The Guardian
Goldsmiths students made their feelings known in Deptford, south-east London, on Friday. Photograph: Lily Renshaw/The Guardian
Goldsmiths fine art students call for compensation over record tutor strike
With industrial action over job cuts heading into its 21st week, students at famed London college are unhappy
Students arriving at Goldsmiths, University of London this autumn found a campus embroiled in a bitter industrial dispute that has upended timetables and disrupted courses.
“I was really excited to start my studies at Goldsmiths,” one international student told the Guardian at a gathering of unhappy students on Friday. “But when I first arrived, I was quite shocked because almost all the induction sessions seemed to turn into discussions about strikes.”
The international student was one of the unlucky ones, having enrolled on the master of fine art (MFA) course that has been among the worst affected, with 10 out of the 12 usual tutors on strike, and students now saying they want compensation from the university for their lost learning.
Edinburgh joins UK universities heading for strikes as cuts spread through entire sector
Florence Sweeney, a part-time student, said there had been “virtually no teaching” so far this term for many of the fine arts students, and that a group were seeking remuneration.
“Not only has it been a financial drain, but I can see my cohort are very distressed and anxious, and some are depressed by what’s going on,” Sweeney said.
“Some people have moved from the other side of the world, and for them it’s such a risk. To not even get welcomed or proper briefings, it’s really disappointing, especially for a place with such a good reputation for producing exceptional contemporary artists.”
Goldsmiths is famous for the stellar array of artists among its former students, including Steve McQueen , Sarah Lucas and Damien Hirst . But it has been financially squeezed in recent years by falling applications in its core humanities subjects and the erosion of domestic tuition fees, setting off what one staff member described as “non-stop” rounds of redundancies and restructuring.
Now entering its 21st week and with no end in sight, the current strike involving University and College Union (UCU) staff is the longest in living memory in British higher education, originating from a staff marking boycott in April over proposed job cuts.
Goldsmiths’ management responded firmly to the boycott by deducting 100% of pay from those involved. The union argued that amounted to a lockout and a formal ballot followed, so that since early June a group of UCU members at the south-east London university have been on indefinite strike.
Joe Newman, a Goldsmiths UCU branch co-president, said: “We’ve resisted as much as we can over the last two rounds of restructuring and redundancies. But right now, the scale of the action that we are taking is in response to the unprecedented scale and speed of the cuts management are pursuing.”
The Goldsmiths crisis: how cuts and culture wars sent universities into a death spiral
Newman said students were largely supportive because they “have more in common with staff on precarious contracts than with senior managers on six-figure salaries”, who he said wanted more savings despite earlier cuts.
“When we talk to the students about why we are on strike, of course there are a range of opinions, but overwhelmingly they are just aghast,” Newman said. “The students who have been here longer have seen the impact of previous cuts and seen how things have only got worse. Students who are new can’t believe that management is about to launch an incredibly messy redundancy process.”
The university said only “a small portion of our staff – around 8.5%” were on strike, leaving most students unaffected, and that it had offered significant compromises to the union, including a pause on compulsory redundancies. But it was not prepared to scrap the 100% pay deductions for the marking boycott, saying it had spent significant amounts to ease the impact on students.
“Like many UK higher education institutions, Goldsmiths faces significant financial challenges, which need to be addressed to ensure its future sustainability,” a spokesperson for the university said.
“We recognise colleagues’ right to take industrial action, but continued disruption risks further impacting students’ education and the university’s hard-earned reputation as a world-renowned academic institution in the long-term.
“We are doing everything we can to monitor and mitigate disruption, and are supporting students on courses affected by this strike action, including offering students’ regular updates, meetings with their schools and other appropriate support as needed.”
UK universities face ‘financial crisis’ amid collapse in international students
MFA students said they had been disappointed by the impact on the two-year course, which charges nearly £15,000 a year for full-time UK students and £33,000 for international students.
John Carver, a part-time student in his second year, said he had decided to suspend his studies after attending a “dismal” departmental meeting at the start of term.
“I’m a mature student, time matters to me, and I would be going into a chaotic situation where tutors have been on strike for so long. There wasn’t any light at the end of the tunnel,” Carver said.
“The tutors at Goldsmiths are fantastic, and the way they interact in tutorials, when you do get them, it’s as good as it gets, you build relationships with all the tutors. But what’s going on now is that [replacement] tutors are being brought in who don’t know the students and it’s not as impressive, to put it politely.”
James Evans, an international student from the US, said visa and funding restrictions meant he had to stick with the MFA course despite the disruption.
“Towards the end of last year, in the first days of the strike, I never received marks back, the end-of-term show wasn’t graded. The marks aren’t the important part, but the dialogue is, and there were very important fixtures for the year that went without any discussion. It was a bit frustrating. But this year it’s been worse,” Evans said.
“Our fees went up this year, [and] as an international student they were exorbitant to begin with. There’s still no feedback on projects from last year, but beyond that there’s no structure, barely any communication. We’ve been left to our own devices. It’s been difficult to navigate.”

### 31. ★★☆☆☆ Healey's Budget needs to sustain the stronger economy through gathering clouds

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 1
- **假设性事件**: 是（已降权）
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T00:26:49+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The economic is positive now but come January things could become trickier for the Labour government

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The chancellor&#x27;s aides are using intelligence-style security measures to guard its secrets: the Budget has been split into "compartments" with very few given access to all of them. There are codenames. The IT has been locked down and ministers viewing the plans have to sign a digital promise not to brief media.
Such security is a response to the feeling that speculation around the last Budget, sometimes informed by insiders, was economically destabilising.
As a recent former defence secretary, Chancellor John Healey has embraced such strict procedures, and has approached most of his public engagements and interviews with a sturdy refusal to break the Budget omertÃ , akin to his fellow Yorkshireman Geoffrey Boycott&#x27;s at times ultra-defensive stance on a cricket pitch.
The economic news has been good - growth has been better than expected and the International Monetary Fund has endorsed the UK for "standing out" among the G7 countries for at least planning to cut borrowing.
Image caption, John Healey has embraced strict security procedures to keep this year&#x27;s budget secret
The bond market&#x27;s reverse beauty contest, in which investors single out the countries they distrust most, has moved on from Britain. Traders I occasionally hear from who were once focused on UK borrowing costs are now more preoccupied with "le spread" - the widening gap between French and German borrowing costs (the former being higher than the latter) - and with America&#x27;s long-term debt outlook.
And yet there are clouds on the horizon. The inflation rate rose more than forecast to 3.5% in September, and borrowing numbers were also higher than forecast. This means an imminent Bank of England rate rise is very much on the cards, with the resultant rise in mortgage repayments for some.
Credibility with investors requires not just that Budget maths add up on Wednesday, but also belief that growth plans are realistic, and the perception that Downing Street can actually convince MPs to pass its Budget.
The question for John Healey is whether he can deliver a Budget that can sustain the revival in growth and offer the help with the cost of living that his backbenchers demand. At the same time he needs to reassure the markets that his budget will keep a check on borrowing.
"Is it resilient, or is it actually strong?" has been the tone of the debate among economists about the curious case of the UK economy ahead what is a vital Budget.
The UK is the fastest growing G7 country so far this year, at 0.6% in Q1 and 0.5% in Q2. The widespread assumption of stagnation in the second half is being defied with Q3 heading for 0.6 to 0.8%.
Each quarter has seen growth twice as fast as predicted by the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) in March and independent forecasters. This is the fastest growth in the first eight months of a year since 2017, ignoring the post-lockdown re-opening.
It&#x27;s more resilient than it was in 2022. Back then, the Ukraine war, massive energy bailouts, and Liz Truss&#x27; mini budget all culminated in the UK facing the highest borrowing costs in the G7, in what was derided in financial markets as a "moron premium".
Then last year, with constant policy u-turns, UK borrowing costs rose the most of the G7.
This year the gap has narrowed, as uncertainty about the US and France takes centre stage, but all advanced economies are feeling the heat from the Iran war energy shock.
Inflation has risen, but not just in Britain. The link between gas and electricity prices has weakened, meaning spikes in gas prices have less impact on electricity bills than they used to and helping to keep inflation lower than it otherwise might have been.
The fact that up until now the Iran war has been more of an oil shock than a gas shock (where the UK is more exposed) has been another saving grace. Meanwhile, the significant amount of AI and tech investment in the UK is another boost to the economic numbers.
The net result is that doom-mongering predictions of recession, of being hit worst by the Iran war, and, in some quarters, of imminent national bankruptcy, have been defied.
That economic defiance of the early part of 2026 has persisted in the first three months of the Burnham-Healey tenure in Downing Street, buoyed by a lull in the Iran war and the energy price shock.
Image caption, Post Liz Truss&#x27; mini-budget the UK faced the highest borrowing costs in the G7
Burnham was advised upon entry to Downing Street that if consumers could at least feel more confident, they might save less and spend more and therefore help boost growth.
So far Burnham and Healey appear to be relying on a series of relatively inexpensive "feel-better" policies - such as capped bus fares and a temporary VAT cut on household energy bills - that have improved confidence without significantly increasing spending.
But there isn&#x27;t a lot of spare money, which limits just how much can be offered for "temporary and targeted" measures. And come January, the clouds on the horizon could be overhead, with typical energy bills predicted to rise by 16%, interest rates potentially up and an enduring fear that the AI bubble could burst.
At a gleaming hi-tech robotics research facility in Coventry last month, John Healey sought to set out the broad contours of his approach to the economy. On a walkabout at the Manufacturing Technology Centre, I asked him if there had been too much doom-mongery.
"I know the global conditions mean that it is hardâ¦ we have huge latent potential in this country. Great talent, and deep capital markets.
"The challenge now is to make sure that we balance the books, we manage and control public spending, and the prime minister and I are in lockstep in our determination to meet the fiscal rules with a buffer," he said.
The giant orange robot arms whizzing around were being trained by AI to use lasers to carve the most intricate metal shapes for prototype parts for the jet engines of the future. The Budget manoeuvres required by Healey may require even more precision.
The essential question is whether he has chosen to impress the markets and explain himself to his MPs, or impress his MPs and explain himself to the markets. Judging by my three interviews with him in recent weeks, it is more the former than the latter.
Image caption, At Labour conference Healey wore the same tie once worn by Gordon Brown
The answer may not be found in his speech at all, but in the borrowing tables that will reveal how much money the government expects to borrow over the coming years, published in the OBR&#x27;s forecast released - assuming no repeat of last year&#x27;s disaster - when Healey sits down in the House of Commons around 13.00.
Just after that, as Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch is delivering the Opposition&#x27;s response, the real reaction the chancellor&#x27;s team will be looking for is whether the markets move, particularly when the Debt Management Office publishes the "gilt remit" - the detail of any changes to its plan to sell UK government bonds to investors.
My sense is that every effort is being made to minimise this number, and help guide down medium and long-term borrowing rates for the government and for mortgages. (It was at this moment in 2022 that markets vomited at the prospect of how much extra they thought the government would need to borrow after Liz Truss&#x27; mini budget.)
From Healey&#x27;s first day in office, the prudence message was practically tattooed to his head. He has spent more time working in the Treasury than any recent chancellor. He spent five years from 2002 to 2007 as a junior minister when Gordon Brown was chancellor - five finance bills, five Budgets, pre-Budget reports and two Spending Reviews.
Image caption, Healey says he will take on some of the challenges Rachel Reeves found tricky to resolve
For his Labour Party conference speech, Healey sported Brown&#x27;s old conference tie. But the Treasury he returned to this year is a changed place in important ways. When his predecessor wore the tie, the Treasury was running an annual surplus, not a Â£120bn deficit adding to a Â£3 trillion total national debt.
I asked him just afterwards, and just before he was whisked off to a meeting of the government&#x27;s emergency COBR committee on RAF Fairford, if he was going to have another go at some of the things that proved so difficult for his predecessor, former Chancellor Rachel Reeves.
He replied: "100%. I was very clear to the conference today and the public beyond. There isn&#x27;t the money that there was in the 1990s. People understand that. The public aren&#x27;t daft.
"Running through the Budget I deliver must be fiscal discipline and the maintenance of control on the public finances, because otherwise we won&#x27;t get on top of the debt costs."
One intriguing element of what we may see on Wednesday is that this theoretically more left-leaning Labour government appears to believe they have the political capital to save money on welfare payments.
The aim is to get some of the 1.35 million people currently off work with mental health issues back into jobs.
Some reporting suggests some affected younger people may see benefits payments cut, with the government instead investing more in getting them into jobs and keeping them in them through the offer, for example, of in-work therapy.
Back in Healey&#x27;s time under Gordon Brown, there was no OBR. Back then it was not unknown for late budget maths problems to be settled with an optimistic tweak of a forecast, a date change to the economic cycle, or choosing a stronger rate of long term growth.
The OBR&#x27;s verdict is crucial now - to what extent it agrees with projected welfare savings or even the importance it places on stronger GDP figures matters now.
The bigger point here is that the chancellor must be shocked by the contrast between the sheer power of the Brown Treasury to do what it wanted, and the reality of what he currently faces: the constraints of the OBR, manifesto red lines, bond markets, significant debts and an energy shock.
There are reasons to postpone some of the toughest decisions beyond this Budget, such as how to fund desired increases in spending on defence and social care. The economic outlook could improve were the Iran war to settle at some point in the next few months, and after revised statistics the OBR may also have cause to rethink last year&#x27;s downgrade to long term economic growth.
There is an imaginable pathway to a forecast next year with higher growth, lower inflation and lower debt interest costs, plausibly improving the public finance projections by tens of billions.
This would be a much more agreeable basis upon which to decide the pace of those big ticket spending rises. It also may be a tempting option for anyone thinking about an election. Former Treasury officials lament: "There is always a reason for procrastination."
Expect the Chancellor to laud the resilience and strength of the UK economy and seek to sustain it, and spread growth geographically. His key judgement, however, will be a balance between extra financial and economic support and the peril of still elevated borrowing costs.
He will try to offer breathing space. It may be needed not just for households and families, but for the Exchequer itself.

### 32. ★★☆☆☆ What is the Budget and what could be in it?

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 1

- **时间**: 2026-10-26T02:16:37+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Chancellor John Healey will set out Labour&#x27;s tax and spending plans when he delivers his first Budget on 28 October.
He has refused to rule out tax increases after acknowledging government borrowing costs are at "historic highs".
But the government&#x27;s room for manoeuvre on tax is limited.
Before the 2024 general election, Labour promised not to increase three big revenue earners for the g

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Chancellor John Healey will set out Labour&#x27;s tax and spending plans when he delivers his first Budget on 28 October.
He has refused to rule out tax increases after acknowledging government borrowing costs are at "historic highs".
But the government&#x27;s room for manoeuvre on tax is limited.
Before the 2024 general election, Labour promised not to increase three big revenue earners for the government: income tax, National Insurance and VAT.
In his statement, delivered to MPs in the House of Commons, Healey will set out how the government plans to raise or lower taxes.
Alongside the Budget, the Treasury will publish details about the measures and their costs.
The independent Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR), which monitors government spending, will also publish an assessment of the health of the UK economy and a forecast of what it thinks will happen in the future.
The run-up to the Budget typically generates speculation about what might be in it, which the government is trying to keep to a minimum this year.
Healey and Prime Minister Andy Burnham face a difficult balancing act, trying to offer more support to households and meet commitments on defence spending, while also sticking to Labour&#x27;s manifesto commitments on tax and the government&#x27;s self-imposed fiscal rules.
The previous chancellor, Rachel Reeves, set out two main rules, which the new leadership has vowed to follow. These are:
Not to borrow to fund day-to-day public spending by the end of this parliament
To get government debt falling as a share of national income by the end of this parliament
In March, the OBR calculated that the first rule would be met with a gap - or headroom - of Â£23.6bn. However, this headroom is expected to have shrunk.
Analysts are divided on how much it may have fallen by. While a rise in government borrowing costs will increase debt interest payments, higher inflation could push up tax revenues.
KPMG believes the government&#x27;s buffer could have fallen to Â£12bn, but the Institute for Fiscal Studies estimates it could be Â£20bn.
There has been speculation over what specific measures will be announced in the Budget.
Bank bosses have told the BBC they expect Healey to raise taxes on the sector . UK banks have been reporting bumper profits, leading to calls from unions for higher taxes.
The Treasury has options: it could raise a 3% surcharge on profits which was lowered from 8% under the previous Conservative government, or it could raise the bank levy that is based on the size of their balance sheets.
Further details on the Your First Home scheme, aimed at helping first-time buyers in England to purchase a property, are expected to be announced in the Budget.
The scheme will allow people to buy a new-build home with a deposit of 2.5%. It would provide them with a loan worth 20% of their property&#x27;s value to help pay for the purchase.
There were concerns raised that the threshold for eligibility for first-time buyers could be set at Â£90,000 for a combined income outside London, and Â£110,000 in the capital, which could have excluded couples such as teachers and nurses, or police officers.
But that threshold is will now be Â£110,000 outside of London, and Â£140,000 in the capital, the BBC understands.
There had been speculation that Capital Gains Tax (CGT) - which is imposed on the profit people make when they sell an asset that has increased in value - could be changed, through either higher rates or by removing or amending exemptions. However, this has now been ruled out .
Pensioners on low-incomes are expected to be shielded from income tax through a new personal allowance. The government had already committed to not taxing people who only get an income from the state pension. But the full state pension is set to rise above the current Â£12,570 threshold, meaning about one million people would have needed to pay tax.
The Times has reported , external that online retail giants such as Amazon and eBay are expected to be made responsible for collecting tax owed by traders using their platforms.
The paper said the money raised is expected to be used by the government to cut business rates for High Street shops, by raising the threshold at which small companies start paying rates from Â£12,000 to Â£18,000.
Fuel duty was frozen under the Conservatives in March 2022 and Sir Keir Starmer&#x27;s government continued the freeze, deciding in May to push back a planned 3p increase in September until the end of this year.
Healey told the BBC in late September that the time and place for dealing with rising petrol and diesel costs was the Budget, but would not give details on whether the freeze would be extended again.
Household energy bills are set to see their biggest rise in four years in January, with a typical annual bill forecast to jump by Â£276 .
There has been speculation , external that Healey is planning energy bill help for households on certain benefits.
The High Value Council Tax Surcharge - dubbed the mansion tax - was announced in last year&#x27;s Budget and will apply to properties in England valued above Â£2m from April 2028.
There had been speculation that the government was considering extending it to properties worth more than Â£1.5m. However, reports now suggest , external Healey has ruled this out.
Healey has said the government plans to slash "form-filling and pointless paperwork" to save UK businesses up to Â£800m.
Ministers have said the proposed changes in regulations will reduce unnecessary bureaucracy for businesses.
Healey is expected to add another Â£1bn to the Â£39bn planned spend on social and affordable housing in England over the next decade.
Regional bodies and mayors in England will be given greater powers over spending, starting with Â£285m next year for things like housing, transport and High Streets. In future the regions will be able to borrow to spend more on local priorities.
Opposition parties have released their alternative budget proposals.
The Conservatives are urging the chancellor to reduce spending and borrowing, as well as to cut green taxes, red tape and benefits to bring down energy bills.
Reform UK are recommending that the chancellor adopts 60 policies which they claim will create jobs - including approving the controversial Rosebank oil field - a project Healey is understood to back.
In the first three months of the year, the UK&#x27;s economy grew by 0.6%, while in the April-to-June quarter it grew by 0.5%.
The most recent data showed the economy grew by 0.1% in August after growth of 0.4% in July, with both figures stronger than expected.
Analysts say the UK economy is proving resilient in the face of energy price shocks caused by the US-Israel war with Iran.
Energy prices jumped after the conflict led to the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz, a key waterway for oil and gas trade.
Economists expect UK growth to slow in the months ahead as those costs continue to weigh on households and businesses.
Prices for goods and services are still rising faster than wanted. Inflation hit 3.1% in the year to August, the highest rate in five months and above the Bank of England&#x27;s 2% target, driven by higher petrol and diesel prices.
The Bank of England held interest rates at 3.75% for the sixth time in a row in September, but said they were likely to rise if high energy prices persist.
What time is the Budget and what happens afterwards?
The Budget speech usually starts at about 12:30 UK time - after Prime Minister&#x27;s Questions - and lasts about an hour.
It will be broadcast live on the BBC iPlayer and on the BBC News website.
The Leader of the Opposition, Conservative MP Kemi Badenoch, will respond to the speech in the House of Commons.
MPs debate the measures for four days, before voting on them.
If approved by MPs, tax changes can come into effect immediately.
However, the government must pass a finance bill to make them permanent.
Do you have any views, comments or questions about this story?

</details>
