### 33. ★★☆☆☆ Salon owners urge Budget VAT cut as staffing pressures grow

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T06:14:11+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Marcus Perkins, who owns a hair and beauty salon in Brierley Hill, says he is scared for the future.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Marcus Perkins said rising costs were making it harder for salons to employ staff and train apprentices
After 22 years running a salon, Marcus Perkins says he is increasingly worried about the future of the hair and beauty industry.
Rising costs are making it harder for businesses to employ staff and invest in apprenticeships, he says, while more salons are turning to self-employed models to survive.
"Seeing salons close on the high street is very scary," he said. "I&#x27;m very scared myself. Luckily it hasn&#x27;t happened yet but who knows what the future holds?"
Perkins is among more than 2,000 salon owners who have signed an open letter calling on the Chancellor John Healey to cut VAT on hair and beauty services from 20% to 10% in this week&#x27;s Budget.
The letter is backed by the British Hair Consortium (BHC), which argues rising business costs are placing increasing pressure on traditional salon employers.
However, the industry has also seen growing numbers of stylists move into self-employed arrangements, with hairdressers often renting a chair within a salon rather than being directly employed.
A Treasury spokesperson said hair and beauty businesses played an important role in supporting jobs, apprenticeships and thriving high streets.
They added that the sector had benefited from reforms to business rates.
Image caption, Stylist Trearna Hart fears fewer salons will be able to train the next generation
Salon owners say they have also been affected by increases in wage, pension and National Insurance (NI) costs.
The government&#x27;s changes to employers&#x27; NI contributions and increases to the minimum wage have been defended by ministers as part of wider efforts to improve pay, support public services and drive economic growth.
The BHC said a reduction in VAT would help businesses take on more staff, invest in training and keep more salons trading on the high street.
Chancellor says he will cut &#x27;pointless paperwork&#x27; to save businesses up to Â£800m
Perkins believed it would make a significant difference.
"We can grow the business and employ more people," he said.
The salon owner runs M&M Spa in Quarry Bank, near Brierley Hill, where he says he has trained more than a dozen young people over the past two decades.
Among them is 21-year-old stylist Trearna Hart, who said many of her peers had struggled to establish careers after leaving college.
She said she was fortunate to find an employer willing to invest in her development.
Image caption, Dorothy Senior says visiting her salon is about more than having her hair and nails done
This year alone, Perkins said he had spent about Â£2,000 on courses and travel costs to support her training.
"It&#x27;s upsetting really," he said. "We help people, we train, we nurture, we mentor. We help the next generation because if we don&#x27;t help the next generation, what happens to our future?"
The BHC claimed apprenticeship numbers had fallen sharply and warned the trend could worsen if pressures on employer-run salons continued.
Perkins believes businesses that employ and train staff are increasingly competing against models centred on self-employed workers.
He argues that creates an "unlevel playing field" because traditional salons often shoulder the costs of training future hairdressers.
Hart said the number of salons disappearing from high streets was a concern.
"At the rate it&#x27;s going we&#x27;re not going to have many salons left open and there&#x27;s no-one going to be able to do hair," she said.
The challenges facing the sector are also worrying some customers.
Research from the British Beauty Council highlighted the role hair and beauty businesses play in supporting local high streets, encouraging spending and providing opportunities for social connection.
Dorothy Senior has visited M&M for hair and beauty treatments for the past 20 years.
She said the salon was about much more than appearances.
"Every time I come in I go home feeling so much better," she said. "It isn&#x27;t just having nails and hair done, it&#x27;s the whole package."

### 34. ★★☆☆☆ Birthkeeper who allegedly ‘groomed’ pregnant women to fear hospital could face criminal charges

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **假设性事件**: 是（已降权）
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T04:29:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Victorian coroner hears Emily Lal told Stacey Warnecke that her ‘condition and mindset’ needed to be ‘solid’ to avoid a hospital birthFollow our Australia politics live blog for latest updatesGet our <a href="https://www.theguardian.com/em

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Australian wellness influencer Stacey Warnecke and her husband, Nathan Warnecke. Photograph: gofundme
Australian wellness influencer Stacey Warnecke and her husband, Nathan Warnecke. Photograph: gofundme
Birthkeeper who allegedly ‘groomed’ pregnant women to fear hospital could face criminal charges
Victorian coroner hears Emily Lal told Stacey Warnecke that her ‘condition and mindset’ needed to be ‘solid’ to avoid a hospital birth
Follow our Australia politics live blog for latest updates
Get our breaking news email , free app or daily news podcast
A birthkeeper who allegedly “groomed” pregnant women to fear hospital could be referred to prosecutors to face potential criminal charges, a Victorian coroner has heard.
Stacey Warnecke died in September 2025 at Frankston hospital in Melbourne after giving birth at home and suffering a postpartum haemorrhage with birthkeeper Emily Lal by her side, having paid her $6,000 to support her throughout pregnancy.
A US champion of ‘freebirthing’ always claimed there had been no maternal deaths linked to the movement. Is Stacey Warnecke the first?
Birthkeepers have no clinical training and operate outside the medical system.
The inquest into Warnecke’s death began in June, but closing submissions to coroner Therese McCarthy were delayed after a forensic analysis of Warnecke’s phone .
Hearings resumed on Monday, when Rachel Ellyard, in her role as counsel assisting the coroner, said that the completed analysis had revealed numerous messages, including in a group chat on the platform WhatsApp coordinated by Lal for women who were choosing freebirth. A phone call between Warnecke, a 30-year-old nutritionist and wellness influencer, and Lal was played to the court.
Ellyard said the phone evidence directly contradicted evidence from Lal, given in June, that she was hired by Warnecke primarily to play the role of a supportive friend, and that the money paid to her was primarily for her time, but not for medical advice.
“Ms Lal saw her role as to groom her clients into being strong enough to resist the temptation to seek medical help during labor, to seek pain relief during labor, to go to hospital during labor,” Ellyard said.
“It’s clear that Ms Lal thought that in most cases, going to hospital would represent a failure of someone who hadn’t done the work, who hadn’t understood enough what the intensity of labor was going to be.”
Emily Lal leaves the inquest into the death of Stacey Warnecke in June of this year. Photograph: Con Chronis/AAP During the call, Warnecke told Lal that she envisioned having a “wise woman” next to her during her first childbirth, to guide her when asking: “Am I dying, or am I alright?”
Lal responded: “You should be scared of the hospital.”
“It’s a terrifying place. For me personally, I think the biggest, the key … to actually staying home and having your baby … is that your mindset and your condition needs to be so solid.”
Lal also told Warnecke that midwives who attend home births sometimes inadvertently sabotage them, because if the birth goes on too long, they tell the woman to go to hospital. This is sometimes just because the midwife is exhausted, Lal said.
Stacey Warnecke died after choosing a birth free of all medical help. An inquest wants to know why
She told Warnecke: “I manage my own energy as much as I can in that way, so that I don’t subconsciously sabotage a person.”
Lal previously told the court that she was not clinically trained, and that it was not her responsibility to make Warnecke’s birth safer, or to call an ambulance unless specifically asked. Lal said she never gave Warnecke medical advice, and only shared her own experiences of freebirthing her own children.
Ellyard said on Monday that given the evidence from the call and many messages and documents obtained from Warnecke’s phone, “the role that Stacey thought Ms Lal would play during the freebirth … was to help her by keeping her safe”.
“Stacey was placing considerable reliance on Ms Lal, in whom she had placed considerable trust.
“Ms Lal was an expert. She was providing advice. She was providing referrals. She was selling supplements. She was offering commentary.
“This is all quite inconsistent with the idea of just being a friend who pulls your hair back and wipes your forehead. It’s something much more structured, much more deliberate.”
Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email Ellyard said Warnecke’s husband, Nathan, had also been “reassured or indoctrinated into a sense that his role was just to just sit back and support, and that whatever Stacey experienced was alright”.
Lal was invited to return to court to give further evidence after the phone analysis, but had declined to, the court heard.
Lal only gave evidence previously on the condition that the coroner grant her a certificate of protection so that her evidence could not be used against her in any future proceedings.
Ellyard put to the coroner on Monday that it would be open to her to find that Lal had committed perjury “in relation to the falsity of the evidence” and to refer her to the Office of Public Prosecutions.
The OPP prepares and conducts criminal matters on behalf of the director.
“Having regard to the materials that have now become available … it would be open to your honour to form the view that Ms Lal wasn’t truthful in the evidence that she gave to you, and that a referral could be made in relation to the offence of perjury,” Ellyard said.
She said there was also evidence Lal had been “holding herself out as someone capable of giving such expert advice and capable of meeting the needs of pregnant women in a range of circumstances” despite having no authority.
The court previously heard that Lal had asked Warnecke three times whether she wanted an ambulance called after she gave birth to the placenta, lost up to 1.5 litres of blood, then began struggling to breathe.
Lal previously told the court that it was not her role to call an ambulance against her client’s wishes, even if the woman was unconscious and dying.
Inquest into Melbourne influencer’s death following freebirth halted after new phone evidence discovered
Warnecke said yes to an ambulance the third time, but by the time paramedics arrived she was seriously unwell, having suffered a massive postpartum haemorrhage.
Warnecke died in hospital several hours later from related complications, with the hospital exhausting its blood supply trying to save her.
Ellyard said on Monday: “Stacey would have survived if she was in hospital.”
Midwives and doctors know that postpartum haemorrhage is the most common complication of childbirth and are so well trained to identify it and treat it that “women do not die in hospital” from it in well-resourced countries, Ellyard said.
Warnecke also “would have survived if she was giving birth at home with a registered midwife,” Ellyard said, drawing from evidence given by medical professionals earlier in the inquest.
“She suffered a known complication of childbirth, which was eminently treatable if identified and responded to immediately.”
The coroner will deliver her findings at a later date.

Neocloud stocks fall as Elon Musk says more than 200 will collapse
Nebius, CoreWeave and Iren all dropped more than 4% in premarket trading on Monday.
SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk said more than 200 neoclouds are "going to see guppy-level death and consolidation."
Investors are already questioning the debt-fueled expansion of neoclouds and whether the large AI infrastructure build-out will generate long-term returns.
SpaceX CEO Elon Musk attends a press briefing held by President Donald Trump with AI executives following a meeting on artificial intelligence, outside the West Wing of the White House in Washington, Sept. 29, 2026.
Shares of major neocloud stocks fell on Monday after Elon Musk said that more than 200 companies in the sector could collapse.
Nebius , CoreWeave and Iren all dropped more than 4% in early premarket trading, adding pressure to a sector already facing investor concerns about debt-fueled expansion and whether the large AI infrastructure buildout will generate returns. However, these stocks have since pared some of the losses and are now trading between 2% and 3% lower.
"As of the past few weeks, debt markets have almost entirely shut off access to money for any company that doesn&#x27;t have substantial revenue independent of AI data centers," Musk, CEO of SpaceX and Tesla , said on a post on X on Sunday.
"That means the 200+ neoclouds are going to see guppy-level death and consolidation. Some will make it through, but not many," he added.
Musk&#x27;s SpaceX operates its own data centers through which it sells computing capacity. SpaceX is a direct competitor to the neoclouds which also rent compute.
CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren shares over the past five days.
Musk was replying to a post on X by Chamath Palihapitiya, the founder of Social Capital, who laid out his views on the state of the data center buildout. He suggested that there will be "a lot of partially stranded compute and compute sitting in inventory in 2027 than is currently forecasted," which will be "bought and paid for but effectively underearning (below total cost of capital) or collecting dust."
"2027 will be a turbulent year because it will clear out a bunch of wrong/bad bets. It will also be a consolidation year for a few," Palihapitiya said.
Neocloud stocks have taken a hammering over the past month, with CoreWeave and Nebius down more than 30% while Iren has tanked 40%. The sell-off started earlier this month after the spread on CoreWeave&#x27;s five-year credit default swaps hit levels generally viewed as signs of distress. The yields on CoreWeave&#x27;s bonds also climbed.
Investors have grown jittery over companies that are borrowing heavily to fund the purchase of expensive Nvidia chips, build data centers, and rent out computing power.
Nvidia&#x27;s Jensen Huang says demand for neoclouds is &#x27;off the charts&#x27;
Nvidia , an investor in several neoclouds, looked to shore up the sector last week at its GTC event in Berlin, Germany.
In an interview with CNBC , Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said the demand for neoclouds is "off the charts."
"What is missing in the middle is the working capital to fuel that growth… because there&#x27;s just so much demand across so many sectors of AI, there&#x27;s a scarcity of capital," Huang said.
"The demand of the capital is much greater than the availability of the capital. That&#x27;s going to get solved," he added.
Marc Boroditsky, chief revenue officer at Nebius, distanced his company from concerns surrounding CoreWeave, saying "we&#x27;re not carrying the kind of debt that they are," in an interview with CNBC. Boroditsky said that if the AI infrastructure company brought its 2027 capacity online, it would sell out. And there could be "the same type of dynamic" in 2028.
Trump&#x27;s data center dividend proposal &#x27;not crazy&#x27;, says Nebius chief revenue officer
"We understand the increased focus from investors, but when we&#x27;re talking from customers, nothing has changed. The demand is still there," Roberts told CNBC last week.
"Next year, we are having conversations with customers covering multiples of all the capacity we possibly bring online, and in the last month or so, 2028 is now coming into focus. But the demand is there," he added.

### 36. ★★☆☆☆ Should you travel together? Ask these questions before booking: 'It'll save you time, money and disappointment'

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T13:38:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Consider budget, accommodations, personal habits, alone time and more, say Anna Goldfarb and Nell McShane Wulfhart, experts in friendship, travel and decision-making.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Should you travel together? Ask these questions before planning a trip
Psychology and Relationships Emotionally intelligent couples ask 1   question every morning: Psychologist
Psychology and Relationships Social isolation can inhibit success:   ‘You need to make time for people&#x27;
Psychology and Relationships I&#x27;m a psychologist who studies couples—to avoid feeling   &#x27;quietly resentful of each other,&#x27; make 1 simple change
Life Celebrity divorce attorney: Ask 5 &#x27;essential&#x27; money questions by the   third date—&#x27;spot red flags before your heart is in too deep&#x27;
Spend Travel costs rose this summer, but   Americans vacationed anyway
Blow-up fights at the Colosseum, tears atop Mount Kilimanjaro and clashes over the window seat: Incompatible travel buddies can ruin a vacation.
As experts in friendship , travel and decision-making , we know that the stresses and complications of travel, not to mention the near-infinite number of decisions you have to make, can put a lot of pressure on a relationship that was perfectly delightful within a 10-mile radius of your homes. There&#x27;s a reason that people tell you to travel with someone before you marry them!
Thirty-five percent of travelers found that they weren&#x27;t compatible with a travel buddy, according to a Booking.com survey conducted in July of 1,253 U.S. adults who&#x27;d gone away overnight within the last 12 months. Twenty-one percent of respondents had even left a trip part-way through because of their travel partner, and a bad vacation made 28% think about ending the relationship or friendship with their ill-suited travel companion.
The problem is that we can have different, unsaid expectations of what a getaway might entail: Does one person want every day booked dawn-to-dusk with activities, while the other wants to lounge in the hotel spa until cocktail hour? Does someone want to go all out with seafood towers and Champagne, while the rest of the group prefers to nosh on convenience store hot dogs and guzzle local beer?
Not every friend, romantic partner or family member is the right travel partner for you. And it&#x27;ll save you time, money and disappointment to figure that out before you plan your itinerary.
Ask these travel compatibility questions before booking
We&#x27;ve created a quiz to help you quickly determine compatibility with your prospective travel partner so you can sidestep potential headaches down the line.
1. What kind of accommodations are you imagining? Are you comfortable sharing?
Start with the basics. Some of us can conk out in the middle of the nightclub; others need a white noise machine, blackout curtains and a room to ourselves. The hotels, hostels or apartments you visit on your trip are key to compatibility on the road — it&#x27;s hard to have a blissful holiday when you&#x27;re exhausted from someone else&#x27;s snoring.
The carry-on vs. checked luggage debate will never die, and this is one difference it&#x27;s wise to hash out in advance. No one wants to spend days carefully choosing their most stain-resistant T-shirts to tuck into a backpack only to see the other person arrive at the check-in desk with two maxed-out suitcases. This doesn&#x27;t have to be a holiday dealbreaker, but discuss it so everyone knows what to expect.
Is your dream vacation full of leisurely days, wandering around, popping into shops and cafes that look interesting and taking afternoon naps? Or is your dream vacation full of museums, restaurants, activities and movement?
For some people, a "vacation" means downtime, with few planned activities and lots of white space to do whatever feels appealing in the moment. For others, there&#x27;s no point in leaving home unless you&#x27;re going to maximize your time, and leaving a destination without visiting every dot on your dense Google Map is a wasted holiday.
4. How long does it take you to get ready in the morning?
This might seem insignificant, but more than one trip has been strained by a long morning routine. One person is straightening their hair and gluing on eyelashes; the other is wearing the same outfit they had on yesterday and tapping their foot, impatiently waiting to get going.
If you&#x27;re incompatible on this point but still want to travel together, we suggest deciding on a strategy in advance. Maybe it&#x27;s okay for one person to go out and explore, get coffee or eat the hotel breakfast alone, or the traveler who takes longer to get ready commits to setting an alarm for 45 minutes before their companion.
Do you prefer to travel on the cheap? Or do you not get out of bed for fewer than five stars? In the Booking.com survey , 35% of respondents had argued over money with their travel buddy.
Budget considerations might be the most important thing to discuss before committing to anything. Ideally, you&#x27;d each set a budget for the trip, a number you&#x27;d be entirely comfortable spending, and then compare to see how far apart you are.
If one person is dreaming of an airport limousine pickup and the other person is planning to squirrel away bread rolls from the hotel breakfast to eat for lunch, you might be better off on different trips.
6. Will you be offended if your travel companion listens to music using headphones or opens a book?
Ever been stuck in an airplane seat next to someone who just won&#x27;t shut up? Compatible travel isn&#x27;t just about similar taste in food and accommodations. You&#x27;re going to be spending a lot of time with your travel buddy.
So before you get on that airport shuttle, have a quick chat: Are we both okay with doing our own thing during the tedious parts of the journey? Or does one of you expect that you&#x27;ll be "on" 24 hours a day and get their feelings hurt if the other prefers to zone out to a podcast?
Once each potential traveler has answered the questions, review them together, discuss and make a decision about traveling together — or not.
If too many differences come up, shake hands and agree to keep your hangouts local.
If you&#x27;re mostly on the same page, see if you can plan a trip that works for both of you. For example, if your ideal activities or costs don&#x27;t match up, maybe one day you split up and just meet for dinner, or you set a restaurant budget together in advance rather than figuring it out on the fly and coming home to huge credit card bills.
If you&#x27;re giving the same answer for every question, then you have our congratulations. Go forth and book those tickets!
Anna Goldfarb is a friendship expert and author of " Modern Friendship: How to Nurture Our Most Valued Connections ," a Next Big Idea Club Must-Read. She writes the Substack Friendship Explained . She&#x27;s been featured in the New York Times, The Atlantic, The Washington Post, WIRED, Oprah Daily, Goop and more. Follow her on Instagram .
Nell Wulfhart  has been helping people make big (and small!) decisions since 2013. Her clients have included politicians, hedge fund managers, Netflix executives, poets, parents, med students and many more. You can listen in on her coaching sessions on her podcast,  The Decision Coach . Find her at  decisioncoach.com .

- **发生了什么**: Audi's CEO defended the company's plans for a $4 billion plant in Georgia, calling it a business decision to grow sales and reduce exposure to U.S. tariffs.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Audi: Tariffs, growth ambitions led to new $4 billion plant in U.S.
Audi plans to invest about $4 billion in its first U.S. manufacturing plant in Georgia, with production expected to start in the early 2030s.
Audi executives said the project aims to expand the brand in the U.S. while reducing its exposure to import tariffs and other trade risks.
Audi expects the factory to have an annual capacity of up to 200,000 vehicles.
The Audi logo can be seen on a car dealership in Manhattan.
DETROIT — Audi&#x27;s CEO defended the carmaker&#x27;s plans for a new $4 billion plant in Georgia on Sunday, calling it a business decision aimed at growing sales and reducing exposure to U.S. tariffs.
The plant, set to be Audi&#x27;s first in the U.S., was announced Saturday by President Donald Trump during a rally in Georgia, near where the Volkswagen -owned luxury brand plans to build the new SUV-producing facility.
"We have significantly greater ambitions for Audi in America," Audi CEO Gernot Dollner said during a media roundtable Sunday. "The United States is the second largest premium market in the world, and today we are not fully exploiting this potential. We are changing that."
The announcement comes amid an intense restructuring of its VW parent company, including plans to cut 50,000 jobs and close four German plants , including an Audi facility, between 2031 and ​2034.
Dollner, who joined Trump at the rally, said the decision for a plant in the U.S. was years in the making, with more than 70 locations evaluated over "many months" and "intense" negotiations with public officials.
"This is not a decision against Germany. Germany remains the backbone of Audi," Dollner said. "We develop our cars here. Our technologies are created here, and Georgia does not take any task away from us in Germany."
Dollner said localized production is the best decision for the brand, which has struggled in sales this decade amid rising operational costs and Trump&#x27;s tariffs on vehicles from Germany and other countries into the U.S. Current tariffs on vehicles from Germany are 15%, down from 25%.
Last year, U.S. tariffs cost Audi 1.2 billion euros ($1.4 billion), as it imports all its vehicles for the U.S. market from Europe or Mexico.
Dollner said the Trump administration asked Audi to announce the new plant at the Georgia rally promoting Republicans ahead of Nov. 3 midterm elections .
"It was … a three-year project that, at the end, by coincidence, led to that moment yesterday," he said Sunday. "With respect of the office and country, we joined the invitation."
Dollner noted Audi is the last major German luxury brand to establish U.S. production after Mercedes-Benz and BMW.
The company said the Georgia plant is expected to begin production of Audi&#x27;s Q7, Q8 and Q9 in the early 2030s. The plant would have the capacity to produce up to 200,000 vehicles annually, roughly half of which the automaker said will be exported outside the U.S.
"This is to make Audi obviously a significant player here in the U.S. market," said Audi of America President Vito Paladino during the roundtable. He also said additional details on U.S. investments and production are forthcoming.
Audi&#x27;s U.S. deliveries were roughly flat through the third quarter of this year, following a 16% decline from 2024 to 2025.
Choose CNBC as your preferred source on Google and never miss a moment from the most trusted name in business news.

</details>

Serbia&#x27;s long-standing leader Aleksandar VuÄiÄ has admitted defeat to a student-led protest movement in snap parliamentary elections.
Celebrations erupted as crowds descended on the centre of Belgrade, Novi Sad and other cities.
The student movement was formed less than two years ago in response to a railway station disaster blamed on corruption and negligence, but fought the election with candidates from a cross-section of Serbian society.
"It is clear we have lost these elections," right-wing populist leader VuÄiÄ said as he conceded late on Sunday, citing preliminary figures that gave the students 2.2 million votes and his party 1.72 million.
The students were on course to win 52.9% of the vote, according to a later projection by independent pollsters CeSID, with the highest turnout for years of 69.34%.
VuÄiÄ had spent two terms as president and could not seek a third term, so was leading his Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) with the aim of returning to the post of prime minister, which he left in 2017.
He called the snap elections after two years of protests triggered by the collapse of a canopy in front of Novi Sad station which killed 16 people.
Image caption, VuÄiÄ said early results made it "clear" that his party had lost
The Student List&#x27;s lead candidate Ilija SrdanoviÄ told BBC Serbian: "This will be a free state."
He said the anti-corruption movement, which had attracted anti-government voters across Serbia, hoped to "free the country from tyranny".
There were celebrations on the streets of the city, with people dancing and chanting "victory", cars honking, and fireworks set off. "Serbia is free," one student told the BBC. "It&#x27;s a miracle."
How a movement of students brought down Serbia&#x27;s VuÄiÄ
During a celebration speech, Student List candidate Radomir Saicic said Serbians had shown "no obstacle was greater than their will", Reuters reported. "No force can forever overpower a people who have decided to take their destiny into their own hands," he added.
Amid scenes of people dancing in the streets Belgrade, Selena Lebovic said the result was "really fabulous".

Image caption, An aerial photo shows the size of the celebrations by Students&#x27; List supporters at Slavija square in Belgrade
VuÄiÄ made his announcement at the headquarters of his Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) in the capital Belgrade.
The centre of Novi Sad - the northern city that triggered the student-led protests - was crowded. "Everything is going to get better now," David VasiÄ, one of those celebrating, told BBC Serbian.
The Student List - which has no obvious leaders and whose members are not used to politics - had attracted anti-government voters across Serbia.
It decided to field candidates to the 250-member parliament from across Serbian society including doctors, actors, academics and sports personalities.
The anti-corruption movement campaigned on a manifesto that included purging officials who broke rules cracking down on protesters, stopping party loyalists from leading public enterprises, and freeing the public broadcaster of political control.
VuÄiÄ and the SNS party have repeatedly denied corruption.
VuÄiÄ, who backed Serbia&#x27;s candidacy for the European Union while refusing to impose sanctions on Russia over its full-scale invasion of Ukraine, had pledged to increase pensions and public sector salaries, and investment in state healthcare.
His defeat was welcomed by Hungary&#x27;s prime minister, PÃ©ter Magyar, who said he was delighted to hear from the Student List&#x27;s leaders "how much strength our victory in April had given them". Magyar&#x27;s party ended 16 years of Viktor OrbÃ¡n rule, while VuÄiÄ&#x27;s SNS party had been in power since 2012.
Image caption, Ilija SrdanoviÄ, Student List&#x27;s leading candidate, pictured during celebrations late on Sunday
European Commissioner for Enlargement Marta Kos said "a new chapter can begin for Serbia. The door to the EU is open, and we are ready to move forward together".
Earlier in the day, long queues were seen outside polling stations in cities and towns across Serbia.
This was also the case outside booths in a number of countries with sizeable Serbian communities - including Slovenia&#x27;s capital Ljubljana, Germany&#x27;s Munich, and Los Angeles and Chicago in the US.
In France, Serbian voters waited in line in heavy rain in Paris.
The election was held amid high tensions across Serbia, with police sealing off streets around the Republic Electoral Commission (RIK) in Belgrade.
Attention will now turn to who will lead the Student List as prime minister. Presidential elections are due to take place in December, with parliament speaker Ana BrnabiÄ currently filling the role in a caretaker capacity.

### 39. ★★☆☆☆ Eric Bauza, voice of Bugs Bunny and Daffy Duck, dies aged 46

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治

- **多源确认**: 1

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Eric Bauza, the voice artist behind Looney Tunes characters including Bugs Bunny, Daffy Duck and Porky Pig, has died aged 46.
A statement from the Emmy award-winning star&#x27;s family posted on Instagram , external said he had died "suddenly".
Bauza had voiced a number of characters in this summer&#x27;s animated hit Coyote vs. Acme, and appeared in the recent release Forgotten Island.
Variety reports that the cause of death was hypertensive cardiovascular disease and that Bauza died at home on Sunday.
In a 2021 interview with NBC News, Bauza, who was born in Ontario and was of Filipino descent, said he grew up a huge fan of the Looney Tunes characters and used to copy the voices as a child.
"Looney Tunes transcends all languages and all cultures," he said , external . "It doesn&#x27;t matter what part of the planet you&#x27;re from. You get the comedy."
He voiced Daffy Duck, Porky Pig, Elmer Fudd and Foghorn Leghorn in 2021&#x27;s Space Jam: A New Legacy before reprising those roles and that of Road Runner in Coyote vs. Acme.
He also played the part of Toad General in 2023&#x27;s The Super Mario Bros. Movie and its sequel The Super Mario Galaxy Movie, the fifth highest-grossing film internationally this year.
Between 2022 and 2025, he won three Emmy awards for outstanding voice performance in a number of TV series.
In a statement reported by the Hollywood Reporter , external , Channing Dungey, the chairman of Warner Bros. Television Group and US Networks, said: "Eric Bauza was a unique voice talent who brought joy and laughter to millions around the world.
"He followed in the footsteps of giants like Mel Blanc. Eric said that carrying on the legendary legacy of the Looney Tunes was among the greatest honors of his life."

</details>

### 40. ★★☆☆☆ Uganda imposes 21-day quarantine on citizens returning from DR Congo

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T08:18:22+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Uganda will also not allow most travellers who have been in DR Congo to transit through the country.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Health Minister Chris Baryomunsi said the measures were meant to keep the country free of Ebola
Ugandans returning from neighbouring Democratic Republic of Congo will be quarantined for 21 days, the health ministry has said, as the country tightens restrictions to contain the spread of Ebola virus.
More than 4,800 people have died in DR Congo since May - the country&#x27;s deadliest outbreak of the disease - and has since spread to several other countries.
Uganda&#x27;s restrictions follow questions after two people who had travelled through the country tested positive for Ebola, in Kenya and Nigeria.
The health ministry on Sunday said the 60 people who were being monitored after coming into contact with the Kenyan Ebola patient had all been discharged.
Announcing the new measures on Sunday, the health ministry said travellers who had been to DR Congo would not be allowed to transit through Uganda.
It said returning Ugandans would "undergo 21 days of quarantine at their own cost".
Uganda has also "intensified traveller screening at Entebbe International Airport and all land border crossings, supported by screening at designated road checkpoints", it added.
Uganda&#x27;s Health Minister Chris Baryomunsi told the BBC&#x27;s Newsday programme that only essential travellers from DR Congo would be allowed in but would be subjected to "intense public health measures".
"All these are measures to ensure that we keep Uganda free of Ebola," he said.
Since the two cases were reported in Kenya and Nigeria this month, Uganda has repeatedly stressed that no cases have been reported within its borders. Uganda recorded 20 Ebola cases and two people died shortly after the outbreak started in DR Congo, but the World Health Organization declared the country free of the disease in August.
Concerns have however persisted after the two passengers tested positive after passing through Uganda&#x27;s Entebbe airport on the same day.
The patient in Nigeria is the Ugandan-born wife of a Swiss diplomat who had travelled to the Ugandan capital for a holiday, according to the head of Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention Jean Kaseya, while the Kenyan had travelled from the DR Congo where he had business interests.
Diana Atwine, the health ministry&#x27;s permanent secretary, said CCTV camera footage showed no physical interaction between the two patients.
The Kenyan businessman died two days after arriving in Nairobi, and the country has since confirmed eight other cases of Ebola. A second woman, related to the businessman died over the weekend.
Questions remain over Ebola patient who travelled across three nations undetected
Ugandan officials had previously said the patient in Nigeria had travelled to eastern DR Congo, only transiting through Uganda. On Sunday, however, the health ministry said Uganda and Nigeria were both working to establish her movements, complete travel history and possible routes of exposure.
The Ugandan health minister said the recent cases meant the risk had not ended and further underscored the need to "remain vigilant".
"We share a border with [DR Congo], where transmission continues, and our communities remain closely connected through trade, travel and family ties," Baryomunsi said.
The disease spreads from one person to another by contact with infected bodily fluids such as blood or vomit.
The Bundibugyo species of Ebola behind this outbreak is relatively rare and there is still no vaccine or specific treatment - though trials are under way.

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治

- **时间**: 2026-10-26T09:59:27+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Rapid Support Forces strike five power stations in three states controlled by Sudanese armySudan’s paramilitary Rapid Support Forces have launched a coordinated drone attack against five power stations, causing mass power outages across the country’s east, a government official said.The drones hit two power stations each in the states of Al-Jazira and Gedaref, and one in White Nile state, “resulting in power outages across large parts of these three states”, the official said on

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Displaced people in Gedaref city. The UN has said attacks on power stations threaten the health of millions of people in Sudan, who are already facing extreme hunger. Photograph: AFP/Getty Images
Displaced people in Gedaref city. The UN has said attacks on power stations threaten the health of millions of people in Sudan, who are already facing extreme hunger. Photograph: AFP/Getty Images
Sudan paramilitary drone strikes cause mass power cuts in eastern states
Rapid Support Forces strike five power stations in three states controlled by Sudanese army
Sudan’s paramilitary Rapid Support Forces have launched a coordinated drone attack against five power stations, causing mass power outages across the country’s east, a government official said.
The drones hit two power stations each in the states of Al-Jazira and Gedaref, and one in White Nile state, “resulting in power outages across large parts of these three states”, the official said on condition of anonymity.
All three states are controlled by the Sudanese army, which has been at war with the RSF, its former ally, since April 2023.
The United Nations has repeatedly warned that regular attacks on power stations threaten health and services for millions of people who are already facing the world’s largest hunger crisis.
Babiker Youssef, a resident of Al-Fao, in Gedaref, said the power had been out there since early morning.
“My son is diabetic, his insulin has to be stored in the fridge,” he said over the phone, adding that he was scared he would not be able to replace the medication if it spoiled.
“We have no idea when the power will come back,” he said.
Electricity has been cut for days at a time after past paramilitary drone strikes on power stations and hydroelectric dams.
Sudan’s war is currently being fought primarily via drones, with both sides launching sophisticated attacks across the country, killing dozens of civilians at a time.
In the first three weeks of October, drone strikes killed more than 300 people and injured hundreds more, according to a UN tally.
Africa’s third-largest country, home to an estimated 50 million people, has been ravaged by the war, which aid workers estimate has killed more than 200,000 people.
The majority of Sudan’s hospitals have been forced to close, at least 8 million children are out of school and swathes of the country cannot access basic services.
Abdelhafez Daafallah, a 54-year-old farmer, said over the phone from Al-Jazira’s Al-Junaid town: “The electricity had just started to improve three months ago, the power station the militia had destroyed was just repaired and we had hope our irrigation pumps would finally start running again.
“We hoped we would be able to finally get back to farming, but now the station has been hit,” he said, frustrated after nearly four years unable to work his land.
Sudan’s RSF committed crimes against humanity in El Fasher, Amnesty says
Farmers in Al-Jazira have struggled to rehabilitate and replant their land since the army recaptured the state from the RSF last year.
Further east, in Gedaref, Ibrahim Mohammed, 37, a grocer, said he was “already facing power cuts for hours before the strike”.
“Now I have chicken in the fridge, we either lose our entire stock, or will be forced to sell it off at any price, which is still a loss.”
Sudan is also grappling with unprecedented inflation and a currency that lost more than 25% of its value in recent weeks.

</details>

### 44. ★★☆☆☆ UK poised to miss reduced aid spending target of 0.3% of national income

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T06:59:36+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Calculations from the Center for Global Development say spending next year is likely to be £410m less than anticipatedThe UK is on course to miss its target of spending 0.3% of national income on international aid by an estimated £400m by the end of next year.Calculations from the Center for Global Development (CGD) found the UK was on course for a large shortfall in promised official development assistance at the end of 2027. <a href="https://www.theguardian.com/politics/20

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

People queue for food aid in February during severe drought conditions in Kenya. Photograph: Patrick Ngugi/AP
People queue for food aid in February during severe drought conditions in Kenya. Photograph: Patrick Ngugi/AP
UK poised to miss reduced aid spending target of 0.3% of national income
Calculations from the Center for Global Development say spending next year is likely to be £410m less than anticipated
The UK is on course to miss its target of spending 0.3% of national income on international aid by an estimated £400m by the end of next year.
Calculations from the Center for Global Development (CGD) found the UK was on course for a large shortfall in promised official development assistance at the end of 2027.
The researchers said the central forecast from the Treasury’s comparison of independent experts implied UK aid would be £410m less than anticipated – at only 0.29% of national income.
However, they said it was “very plausible” that the shortfall would be £580m, at only 0.28% of national income. Even the most conservative forecast implies a £250m shortfall, according to the estimates.
Ian Mitchell, a senior fellow at the CGD and co-director of its Europe programme, described these as “material shortfalls in the spending power of UK aid”.
Making up a shortfall of even £400m would be enough to halve cuts to Africa that are likely to cost thousands of lives and mean the UK’s de facto exit from the south and east of the continent, he said.
Conservative plans to slash aid budget would ‘irreparably damage’ UK’s global credibility, charities say
The CGD attributed the shortfall to higher than expected inflation, which means national income will be higher than forecast at the spending review, and a 2025 policy change around the Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office (FCDO) budget.
The researchers called on ministers to consider allocating additional funds to international aid in this week’s budget, to avoid the embarrassment and criticism from missing a target.
The 0.3% spending target for 2027 is far below the 0.7% promised in Labour’s election manifesto, having been downgraded by Keir Starmer to boost spending on defence.
Fleur Anderson, a former minister who sits on the foreign affairs select committee, said she would like to see higher spending on development aid in the budget.
She said: “I’d like to see this government going further and reversing these cuts altogether in time, but at the very least we should be hitting the reduced target that was set.
“Falling short isn’t an abstract accounting issue. It means communities not having clean water, girls pulled out of school, vaccines that don’t get delivered, peace programmes in war zones cut.
“The upcoming budget presents real opportunity for the Treasury to address this and for the government to show that its words on Britain’s place in the world are matched by action.”
Charities have repeatedly said that the government’s deep aid cuts will hit children’s education and increase the risk of disease and death in some African countries.
The latest figures released by the FCDO in September showed that British aid cuts under Starmer’s government hit the world’s poorest countries the hardest , while higher-income countries’ share of aid increased. The data showed a 6.7% decrease in funding to the least-developed and low-income countries, amounting to cuts of £118m.
The UK’s overall official development assistance (ODA) budget in 2025 decreased to 0.44% of gross national income, compared with 0.5% in 2024, a drop of £987m.
The government has prioritised spending through multilateral agencies such as the World Bank’s development arm, the International Development Association, and the global vaccines initiative Gavi . It has also continued to fund humanitarian support.
The Conservative party and Reform UK have pledged to slash aid even further , from about £9bn a year to £1bn – a level that would leave the UK unable to cover existing contributions to the UN and World Bank.
An FCDO spokesperson said: “The publication of the FCDO multi-year ODA allocations in March 2026 set out our spending plans and priorities for the next three financial years based on GNI [gross national income] at the time of the 2025 spending review.
“Moving to a fixed budget reduces volatility and provides the necessary stability to deliver high-impact aid programmes.”

Brazil election 2026: Full second round results as Lula wins comeback against Flávio Bolsonaro
After a shock defeat in the first round, the incumbent Brazilian president, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, has turned the election around to win the runoff.
Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva 51.52% Workers' party (PT) | 56,506,197 votes
Flávio Bolsonaro 48.48% Liberal party (PL) | 53,164,037 votes
Brazil’s president is elected purely by the popular vote. A winning candidate needs one more vote than 50% of votes cast; there is no role for the parliament and no electoral college.
Because no candidate achieved 50% in the first round on 4 October, the two leading candidates go into a runoff. One of them is Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva , the incumbent leftwing president, commonly known as Lula. The other, and the leading candidate in the first round, is Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of the far-right former president Jair Bolsonaro.
Bolsonaro senior is serving a 27-year prison sentence for plotting a failed coup attempt after he lost to Lula in the 2022 election, but has endorsed his son and hopes to be released if he wins. Bolsonaro junior is a lawyer and former senator from Rio.
In 2022, Lula beat Jair Bolsonaro in the second round, with an electoral base concentrated in the north-east of the country, while Bolsonaro led in the south. But on that occasion Lula entered the second round as the leading candidate from the first round, and won the bellwether state of Minas Gerais, north of Rio de Janeiro. In the first round of the 2026 election, Bolsonaro beat Lula in Minas Gerais by 48.43% to 43.33%.
Never, since the return of democracy to Brazil in 1985, has the first round runner-up managed to turn the game around and win the second vote. But Lula’s campaign has been buoyed by massive, student-led anti-Bolsonaro protests after the first round and hopes to stage a historic fightback on Sunday.
Lula is already the longest-serving president in Brazil’s modern history, serving two terms from 2003 to 2010, before returning to office in 2023. He will turn 81 on Tuesday. Bolsonaro is 45.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

A social media post advocating raising a brick, including saying: ‘Those seemingly meaningless little things actually make you realise that, besides spending money, you can have a lively, ever-changing corner for free.’ Photograph: xiaohongshu.com
A social media post advocating raising a brick, including saying: ‘Those seemingly meaningless little things actually make you realise that, besides spending money, you can have a lively, ever-changing corner for free.’ Photograph: xiaohongshu.com
‘Zero-energy companionship’: bizarre hobby takes seed among China’s stressed young adults
Faced with a stagnant jobs market, many are spurning children or pets in favour of ‘raising bricks’
Chinese young adults looking for a cheaper, low-maintenance alternative to having pets or children have found an answer: raising bricks.
Bricks may not be as cuddly as cats or as life-affirming as human offspring, but they also cost next to nothing. “No cats, no dating, I started raising a brick,” wrote one brick parent on social media.
Raising “brick babies” is easier than house plants, involving spraying them with water a few times a day and waiting for moss spores already present in the atmosphere to attach themselves and grow. One Chinese media article praised the bricks as being “practically immortal”: “If you forget to water them, the moss goes dormant. If you remember to water them again, the greenery returns.”
A hashtag related to the trend has attracted more than 5m views on Weibo, a Chinese social media platform.
Social media user documents their brick’s changes over a year, with hashtags including #healingand #stress relief. Photograph: @South / xiaohongshu.com Chinese commentators have linked the trend to young people looking for low-effort routes to finding meaning in life. One brick parent told Chinese media that the hobby created “a sense of accomplishment no less than completing a long-term project”.
One user on Xiaohongshu, an Instagram-like platform, also known as Rednote, posted a picture of their rusty-coloured brick baby, saying “no output required, no KPIs to hit, no need to beg it to bloom [and] no comparing its growth with others”.
Another user wrote: “My friends asked if I’d lost my mind. I said no, I’m just starting to raise a brick.”
“A brick gives me a sense of certainty that feels way more grounded than a lot of relationships. Psychologists call this ‘zero-energy emotional companionship.’ It fulfils your need for company without demanding any obligation to give love in return.”
Chinese young people are increasingly shunning the traditional expectations of society such as having children and grinding out long hours in strenuous jobs, assuming they can find one in the first place. Nearly one in five 16- 24-year-olds, excluding students, are unemployed , according to official statistics. Some estimates put the employment rate much higher.
China’s birthrate is in stubborn decline and fell to a record low last year. Faced with a sluggish job market after years of studying in an intensely competitive educational environment, many young people are choosing to drop out of the rat race, and opting instead for “ lying flat ” – pursuing a low effort, low stress lifestyle, well-suited to raising bricks.
The trend of raising bricks has become so popular that Chinese media has started to warn about the health risks.
Cultivating moss could lead to the spread of mould, with some “brick parents” reportedly complaining of suffering from itchy eyes and a runny nose. Xuedong Li, a retired moss professor from Beijing’s Capital Normal University, urged brick parents to raise their mossy babies in an enclosed environment. “It is best to wear disposable gloves and a mask during handling to reduce spore inhalation and skin contact,” Li told Chinese media.

A car engulfed by flood waters in Morayfield, north of Brisbane on 22 October, 2026. Photograph: Darren England/AAP
Man found dead in Queensland flood waters as state hit by multiple storms and days-long deluge
Many rivers and roads left flooded after torrential downpours across Queensland
Get our breaking news email , free app or daily news podcast
A man’s body has been found in flood waters following an unusual weather system that delivered multiple storms and a days-long deluge to parts of Australia.
Emergency services were called to a creek at Boompa on Queensland’s Fraser Coast shortly after 11am on Monday after reports a man had been found unresponsive.
The man was declared dead after being found in Smothering Creek on Boompa Road, Queensland police said.
An investigation into his death has begun, with police to prepare a report for the coroner.
Further south, six campers were rescued after becoming stranded by flood waters at a public campground in Leyburn in Queensland’s Southern Downs.
Queensland Fire and Rescue Swiftwater crews responded to the call for help at 7.45am on Monday.
They rescued three men aged 71, 47 and 26 and two women aged 68 and 26 without incident at about 11.15am.
Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email There were no reports of physical injuries, Queensland police said in a statement.
Torrential downpours across Queensland between Thursday and Monday left many rivers and roads flooded across the state’s south and east.
There are warnings of more rain and flooding to come.
Flash flooding hit Laidley Creek and Glenore Grove in the Lockyer Valley, west of Brisbane, closing some roads, Queensland police said on Monday.
The highest recorded four-day totals were spread across the state, with Blackdown Tableland, near Rockhampton, recording 439.4mm to early Monday.
Lower Springbrook, near the Gold Coast, recorded 325.2mm, while Nambour, near the Sunshine Coast, copped 314.2mm.
Meteorologist Angus Hines said the system behind the downpours was unusual.
“The widespread rain bands with heaps of cloud cover, much cooler temperatures, and then the storms in that situation are really driven by what’s going on in the higher parts of the atmosphere,” he said.
On Sunday evening, thunderstorms were briefly connected from southern Queensland to Bass Strait, as a swift-moving system passed towards Australia’s east coast.
The storm band raced over most states, propelled by strong winds in the upper atmosphere.
“But that steering wind sort of just tapered off through the night last night over [Queensland’s] Darling Downs,” Hines said.
Flood warnings are current for Warrill Creek and the Bremer River in the Lockyer Valley, the Macintyre and Moonie rivers in the south, and the Mary River near the Sunshine Coast.
Western Queensland also remains on alert, with warnings current for the Warrego River at Charleville, the Bulloo River in Quilpie, and the Barcoo River at Blackall.
The Bureau of Meteorology said Queensland’s weather was set to return to more typical spring conditions as the week continued.
Hot and humid days are expected to give way to more subdued afternoon showers and storms.

Independent member for Kooyong Monique Ryan consoles Independent member for Warringah Zali Steggall during tributes for Helen Haines. Photograph: Mick Tsikas/AAP
Warringah MP Zali Steggall says she’s personally disappointed by Angus Taylor’s speech paying tribute to Helen Haines this morning, where the opposition leader denied any connection between robust parliamentary debate and political violence .
The independent MP, who sits next to Haines in the lower house, said “there is a link between our words and actions”.
At a press conference shortly before question time, Steggall said:
I did not feel that Angus Taylor read the room when it comes to the lesson that Helen would like us all to learn. To make what’s happened to her, the utter tragedy of it, make sense to all of us, makes sense for Australia, then we have to be able to take responsibility for the impact our words and our debate has beyond the walls of Parliament. And the same goes to the words and the images and the editorials used in media, social media ... what ads we authorise at election time for a party.
These were all part of Phil [Haines]’s speech to us this morning, his words to us, asking us to raise the bar so that when Helen does come back to this place, there’s been a meaningful shift in how we can have a respectful debate.
We can disagree respectfully, but words matter and have an impact .

That’s where we’ll leave today’s live coverage. Before we go, here are today’s top headlines:
Helen Haines’s first words after an alleged attack last week were to ask about the safety of her staff and express gratitude to bystanders and doctors.
Federal politicians made speeches paying tribute to Haines, including the prime minister, Anthony Albanese , who offered his support and well wishes for Haines.
The opposition leader, Angus Taylor , also gave words of support for Haines, but disagreed with a proposed link between parliamentary debate and violence .
Taylor’s speech received backlash, with the Warringah MP, Zali Steggall , saying he didn’t “read the room” and the Kooyong MP, Monique Ryan , calling his comments “tone deaf” .
Western Sydney airport welcomed its first international passengers this morning when Air New Zealand flight NZ165 touched down.
The NSW government announced what it calls the biggest gun buyback “in a generation” , with eligible firearms licence holders able to book beginning midday today to surrender firearms and ammunition.
Luke Gosling no longer facing breach of bail charge
A charge of breaching bail conditions has been dropped against the federal Labor MP Luke Gosling who still faces two charges of assaulting the Northern Territory administrator, AAP reports .
In the Darwin local court on Monday, the charge was withdrawn by the prosecutor Jo Poole .
Gosling, who holds the federal seat of Solomon, taking in Darwin, was not required to appear.
The 54-year-old was charged in July with assaulting the NT administrator, David Connolly , the jurisdiction’s equivalent to a state governor.
After Gosling was charged with breaching bail on 14 August, he stood aside as special envoy for defence, veterans’ affairs and northern Australia.
Luke Gosling speaks in parliament. Photograph: Mick Tsikas/AAP The assaults are alleged to have occurred at the NT Thailand Grand festival in Darwin on 18 July.
Connolly, a former cattle station manager nominated to the position by the territory’s Country Liberal party government, was sworn in in February.
Gosling’s lawyer, Marty Aust , told the court in September he would be writing to the prosecutors on the basis there was insufficient evidence to support the assault charges.
The assault matters will return to court on 25 November.
The prominent Sydney-based barrister Sophie Callan SC has taken on the case against Gosling for the NT Director of Public Prosecutions after the Commonwealth Director of Public Prosecutions declined to take it.
As part of his bail conditions, Gosling is banned from going within 50 metres of the NT’s Government House building.
He must give the administrator’s office 48 hours’ notice before attending any event where the two men would be present.
If they are at an event together, the Labor politician must not be within 15 metres of Connolly.
Man found dead in flood waters on Queensland’s Fraser coast
A man has died after being found unresponsive in flood waters in the Fraser coast region on Monday morning.
Emergency crews were called to Smothering Creek along Boompa Road, south-west of Maryborough in Queensland, shortly after 11am following reports of an unresponsive person in the water.
Queensland police confirmed that emergency responders declared the man deceased upon arrival at the scene.
Investigations into the circumstances of the man’s death remain ongoing. Police will prepare a report for the coroner.
Budget estimates hears ‘contracted’ costs of pre-Cop event more than $30m
Pre-Cop climate summit events held in Fiji earlier this month cost the Albanese government close to $35m.
The federal government told a budget estimates hearing on Monday that “contracted commitments” for the preparatory meeting held in the lead-up to the Cop31 climate summit came to $34.6m. But the final tally was expected to be lower, partly because some costs were expected to be reimbursed by other delegates.
The bulk of the figure comprised $20m for event management and $8.4m for accommodation and venues.
The climate change, energy, environment and water department and the environment minister, Murray Watt , told the hearing that some costs, including a portion of the accommodation and venue fees, were expected to be reimbursed by other countries.
Murray Watt at Senate estimates today. Photograph: Mick Tsikas/AAP Canavan rejects idea that Haines attack marks ‘turning point’ in political discourse
The Nationals leader, Matt Canavan , has pushed back against suggestions that the attack on the independent MP Helen Haines marks a “turning point” in Australian politics .
Speaking on ABC’s Afternoon Briefing, Canavan dismissed the suggestion that rhetoric in Canberra has deteriorated, describing parliament as a “release valve” for society.
“I don’t particularly think our political rhetoric is overly different from eras gone past,” he said.
After Andrew Hastie’s call to leave “the nasty personal politics of Maga” behind in the wake of the attack on Haines, Canavan rejected any direct link between political commentary and community violence. He also pushed back against calls for politicians to receive increased security.
Police officers, emergency officers, nurses face probably more constant threats of assault and real physical challenges than we do …
I’m not asking for any kind of special protection here.
Canavan added that he was “a little bit wary” of a proposed parliamentary civility pledge, saying:
If we go too far the other way, that’ll actually increase tensions and reduce social harmony.
Matt Canavan speaks at a press conference earlier today. Photograph: Mick Tsikas/AAP Following parliament’s tributes to Helen Haines , the independent MP Monique Ryan has said that politicians must change their conduct in the wake of the “unthinkable” attack on her crossbench colleague.
Appearing on the ABC’s Afternoon Briefing, Ryan said political leaders should listen to security experts who correlate the tone of political language with rising community aggression.
She criticised the opposition leader Angus Taylor’s speech paying tribute to Haines this morning as “tone deaf” and “hugely disappointing”, particularly in light of remarks made by Haines’s husband earlier in the day.
If Angus Taylor doesn’t think that the words that we use matter, I don’t understand how he aspires to lead a political party …
If we can’t rise to the occasion now after something as unthinkable as what happened to Helen and think about how we behave in the house and in the public space … we’re never going to do that.
Monique Ryan in parliament with other crossbenchers. Photograph: Lukas Coch/AAP
Helen Haines’s first words after alleged attack were about safety of staff and gratitude to bystanders, MP reveals
First international flight touches down at Western Sydney airport
Western Sydney airport welcomed its first international passengers this morning when Air New Zealand flight NZ165 touched down.
The arrival marked the start of commercial international services at the new airport – Australia’s first major new airport in more than 50 years.
Following a brief turnaround, the return flight (NZ166) departed fully booked. Air New Zealand will operate three return flights weekly on Mondays, Wednesdays and Fridays.
The prime minister, Anthony Albanese , welcomed the arrival as a milestone for the area:
Western Sydney is now connected directly to the world, bringing more jobs, more visitors and more opportunities for local businesses.
And importantly, it means people in Western Sydney can fly overseas without having to travel across the city first.
Passengers disembark Air New Zealand flight NZ165 at Western Sydney International. Photograph: Hollie Adams/Reuters View image in fullscreen
Return flight NZ166 takes off. Photograph: Izhar Khan/Getty Images
Western Sydney airport is open from today. Here’s everything you need to know
Big Build royal commission to hold public hearings through much of the Victorian election campaign
The royal commission into allegations of corruption on Victorian government construction sites is set to hold two weeks of public hearings in the middle of the state election campaign.
The inquiry updated its website on Monday afternoon confirming the first block of public hearings from 9 November to 20 November.
This falls during the caretaker period, when the election campaign is expected to be in full swing, and includes a few days of early voting, which opens on 18 November.
Labor MPs are bracing for potentially damaging allegations.
Here’s what the royal commisison heard on the first public hearing last month:
Bikies, ‘moral compass malfunctions’ and corruption cost: Victoria’s Big Build royal commission holds first public hearing
That’s all from me today, thank you for following along.
My colleague Ima Caldwell will take you through the rest of today’s updates. See you back here tomorrow morning.
Government accused of ignoring lessons from robodebt
An algorithm used by the federal government to assess the level of care older Australians are entitled to is unreasonable, unfair and lacks human oversight, according to a damning investigation by the commonwealth ombudsman.
The investigation also found transparency recommendations from the robodebt royal commission have been ignored.
The investigation followed reporting by Guardian Australia that older people were being left distressed and without adequate care after their home support needs were assessed by an “inhumane” algorithm as part of the government’s mandatory Integrated Assessment Tool (IAT).
Aged care assessors are barred from overriding the IAT’s funding package recommendation, even when the decision is wrong.
Documents released under freedom of information reveal departmental panic, severe clinical distress among frontline assessors, and secret IT workarounds after officials realised in the hours before the rollout of the algorithm that legislation legally barred human override, and that this was causing distress within days of implementation.
The shadow health minister, Anne Ruston , said the report shows: “The algorithm must be scrapped.”
Labor defended this system for months while the Coalition and the crossbench said it was broken. Today the independent umpire has agreed with us.
Unreasonable and unfair: damning investigation criticises Labor’s algorithm-based tool for aged care
NSW police minister says gun buyback reflective of ‘very sensible approach’ by government
The NSW police minister, Yasmin Catley , and police commissioner, Mal Lanyon , held a press conference earlier today to detail the state’s gun buyback program. As we reported earlier, eligible licence holders are now able to book an appointment to surrender any excess or reclassified firearms from next Monday, 2 November.
Catley said bookings were essential as the buyback program began, saying people cannot walk into police stations without an appointment. Those who do turn in eligible weapons will receive compensation ranging from $450 to $1,000, depending on the firearm. Small businesses affected by changes to gun laws will also be eligible for grants up to $25,000.
A second phase for high-value firearms will begin next year.
“This has been a very sensible approach by the government,” she told reporters.
A very sensitive way of responding to the worst terrorist attack that we have seen on our streets in this country.
When asked about a draft proposal to allow some to apply for more than 10 firearms in limited circumstances, Catley said people would need “a very good reason”, such as competitive sport shooting.
“If you are at those elite levels, then it may be the case that you do need more than the 10 guns,” she said.
Warringah MP Zali Steggall says she’s personally disappointed by Angus Taylor’s speech paying tribute to Helen Haines this morning, where the opposition leader denied any connection between robust parliamentary debate and political violence .
The independent MP, who sits next to Haines in the lower house, said “there is a link between our words and actions”.
At a press conference shortly before question time, Steggall said:
I did not feel that Angus Taylor read the room when it comes to the lesson that Helen would like us all to learn. To make what’s happened to her, the utter tragedy of it, make sense to all of us, makes sense for Australia, then we have to be able to take responsibility for the impact our words and our debate has beyond the walls of Parliament. And the same goes to the words and the images and the editorials used in media, social media ... what ads we authorise at election time for a party.
These were all part of Phil [Haines]’s speech to us this morning, his words to us, asking us to raise the bar so that when Helen does come back to this place, there’s been a meaningful shift in how we can have a respectful debate.
We can disagree respectfully, but words matter and have an impact .
The alleged attack on the independent MP Helen Haines loomed large over the House of Representatives, creating a civil yet sombre question time . Some politicians offered compliments to their counterparts across the aisle, others contributed by keeping their derision for dixers to themselves.
The government faced questions on the sale of Victoria Barracks in Sydney.
The impact of social media on violence against women, workers and politicians came under the spotlight.
It is yet to be seen whether this question time atmosphere will last more than one day.
A rose on Helen Haines’s desk during question time. Photograph: Lukas Coch/AAP The independent MP for Indi, Helen Haines , has been given leave of absence until 26 November after she was injured in an alleged attack in her electorate.
The leader of the House of Representatives, Tony Burke, clarified he had simply chosen the last sitting date of the year:
Obviously, we will continue to move resolutions for as long as Helen needs us to.
No leave of absence motion stops the fact that, if at any moment, Helen feels she’s ready to come, then obviously she immediately will.
Continuing from the previous post, social services minister, Tanya Plibersek , says the tenor of public debate has changed significantly during her three-decade long political career.
It is more violent in its tone, and I think the industrial scale of the abuse that all of us receive – but particularly the women here receive – that has changed.
But I’d say this about social media … Social media companies profit by the division they drive in our society.
And that’s why all of us have a responsibility to resist that too. To resist that division, to resist the violence and discourse, that the big tech giants are forcing on us.
Independent for Bradfield and the electorate’s first female MP, Nicolette Boele , directs a question to the longest-serving female member of the House of Representatives, Tanya Plibersek .
Violence against women, whether physical, spoken, or in writing, is used to silence them. And when it keeps women out of this place, it decides to represent us. That is not democracy.
What does the minister say to the young women of Australia now, wondering whether public life is safe for them?
I’m not surprised if there are young women all over Australia looking at the horrific [alleged] attack on Helen Haines , and wondering whether a life in public is worth it, whether it’s safe for them.
And what I would say to them is 100% it is worth it.
Every single day in this place, we make important decisions that change this country and change it for the better.
We can’t vacate the field to the people who would stop us from doing that.
Social services minister Tanya Plibersek, 26 October, 2026. Photograph: Lukas Coch/AAP Greens leader expects Steele-John to remain on leave until investigation complete
Greens leader David Shoebridge says he’s “glad” Jordon Steele-John made the decision to stay away from Senate estimates after a week of confusion following the release of a letter accusing him of inappropriate behaviour.
Over the weekend, Steele-John said he would not attend parliament next week after seven members wrote to Shoebridge urging an investigation into claims he had acted inappropriately towards women between 2017 and 2025.
Shortly after the letter’s release to parts of the media, Shoebridge said Steele-John would go on leave while an independent investigation took place. Steele-John publicly rejected the idea that he was on leave and has denied all allegations made against him.
In a press conference before question time this afternoon, Shoebridge said other members of the Greens will ask questions of the departments and agencies involved in the NDIS when they appear later this week. He added he would not go into a “blow-by-blow discussion about the exchanges”.
Asked whether Steele-John would remain on leave until the investigation is complete, Shoebridge responded:
Well, I’ve made it very clear to Senator Steele-John what I believe the community expectations are. If you ask me tomorrow, I’ll say the same. If you ask me the day after it, I’ll say the same. I’ve made it very clear what I believe the community expectations are.
Online radicalisation involves mixed ideologies, with violence the ‘only principle’, Burke says
Home affairs minister, Tony Burke , is asked a dixer on how the government is working with neighbours and partners to keep Australians safe.
He says all countries are now dealing with the radicalisation of young people:
Every country is reporting a new cohort, and growing fast, of young people, particularly male, where we used to worry about people becoming involved and radicalised in their teens.
The radicalisation [is] happening online and involving mixed ideologies.
Ideologies that would be completely incoherent with each other, but only having violence together as their only principle.
I want to assure the House of the cooperative work that Australia is doing with all of our agencies and all of our effort.

</details>
