Angus Taylor criticised for ‘tone deaf’ response to alleged attack on Helen Haines
Zali Steggall and Monique Ryan say Coalition leader was wrong to deny link between parliamentary debate and political violence
Angus Taylor has been criticised for his response to the alleged attack on Helen Haines , with crossbenchers Monique Ryan and Zali Steggall labelling a speech by the opposition leader “tone deaf” and saying he did not “read the room”.
Parliament returned in Canberra on Monday, a week after an alleged attack outside the Indi MP’s northern Victorian electorate office, which led to her losing both legs. A 43-year-old man has been charged and the incident continues to be investigated.
Former Liberal leader Peter Dutton told News24 he believed more violence against politicians was “inevitable” in Australia. Police have not revealed any established motive for the Haines attack.
“It came as no surprise to me, I am sorry to say. I think it has been building for a long period of time,” he said on Monday.
Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here
The episode has sparked debate about the safety of MPs and the state of the political environment, including the culture of parliamentary debates.
The opposition leader, Angus Taylor, and the One Nation MP Barnaby Joyce have rejected calls for a change in practice to cool Australia’s political debate.
“In the past week, it has been suggested by some there is a causal connection between robust parliamentary debate and political violence,” Taylor said. “I disagree with this view.
AFP boss says politicians’ heated rhetoric is fuelling threats against MPs
“The Westminster system is about settling differences through robust and rigorous debate, not violence.
“Importantly, we debate with adversarial vigour, but we never allow that vigorous parliamentary debate to spill over into violence. People who resort to intimidation and violence cross a line that must never be crossed.”
He said Labor had been correct to call out far-right extremism in Australia but called for all extremism to be rejected. He said judges should “come down harder” on people found guilty of intimidation or violence.
“Another thing we must do is celebrate Australia more readily. Too many Australians have been taught to feel guilty and shame about our country and about our history.
“Is it any wonder that some feel deep animosity towards our democracy, our institutions, and our nation?”
Joyce told News24 his thoughts and prayers remained with Haines and her family, calling the alleged attack “so unAustralian”.
“Unfortunately what we’re seeing in Australia and around the world is a change in dynamics,” Joyce said. “I think parliament has got its part to play but I don’t think it’s going to be a solution to the problem.
“The issues, such as the Palestinian march across the [Sydney Harbour] Bridge, the vindictive, arsenic type of rhetoric that we see in the public realm, these are all part of it.
“I think it’s foolish to think if question time changes, Australia changes. Not necessarily, in fact highly unlikely.”
Steggall, the independent Warringah MP, who sits next to Haines in the lower house, said she was personally disappointed by Taylor’s speech.
“There is a link between our words and actions,” she said.
“I did not feel that Angus Taylor read the room when it comes to the lesson that Helen would like us all to learn.
Helen Haines vigil sparks calls for greater safety for women in politics – video
“To make what’s happened to her, the utter tragedy of it, make sense to all of us, make sense for Australia, then we have to be able to take responsibility for the impact our words and our debate has beyond the walls of parliament.”
She said the same caution should be applied to media reporting, election advertising and social media posts.
Steggall referenced conversations with Haines’s husband, Phil Haines, in Canberra on Monday.
“His words to us, asking us to raise the bar so that when Helen does come back to this place, there’s been a meaningful shift in how we can have a respectful debate,” she said.
“We can disagree respectfully, but words matter and have an impact.”
Ryan, the Kooyong independent, called Taylor’s response “tone deaf” and “hugely disappointing”.
“If Angus Taylor doesn’t think that the words that we use matter, I don’t understand how he aspires to lead a political party,” she told ABC TV.
“If we can’t rise to the occasion now after something as unthinkable as what happened to Helen and think about how we behave in the house and in the public space … we’re never going to do that.”

Former RAAF pilot who allegedly murdered wife was ‘love rat’ and ‘master manipulator’, jury hears
Robert John Crawford on trial after wife found near ride-on lawnmower on their Toowoomba property
Get our breaking news email , free app or daily news podcast
A former RAAF pilot was a “love rat” and “master manipulator” who staged his wife’s death to make it appear like an accident, a jury has heard.
Robert John Crawford, 49, pleaded not guilty on Monday to murdering his wife, Frances Elizabeth Crawford, at Upper Lockyer on 29 July 2024.
Her body was found near a ride-on lawnmower at the bottom of a retaining wall on their sprawling rural property west of Toowoomba.
Crawford murdered his wife in the context of a long relationship breakdown, prosecutor Chris Cook told a Brisbane supreme court jury.
Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email The husband then embarked on a long campaign to make her death appear an accident, Cook alleged.
Four women with whom Crawford allegedly had affairs are due to be called as witnesses.
“He made out to be a man with good Christian values,” Cook said.
“However, his story did not match his behaviour. He was a man who lived a double life for a long time.”
Cook described Crawford as “a cheater, adulterer, love rat, philanderer and womaniser”.
The jury was told Crawford was not committed to the marriage, with the couple on the verge of divorce when the victim died.
The members were told Crawford was motivated to kill his wife because he stood to lose financially in a divorce settlement. He wanted to retain possession of the couple’s house and did not want to split his superannuation.
The jury was told Crawford wavered between divorcing her husband and giving him the chance to prove himself to her and their children. She had set her husband a detailed list of boundaries, including “deal breakers” that would lead to divorce.
The deal breakers included sexual or emotional infidelities, pornography and masturbation, the court was told.
Justice Lincoln Crowley told the jury the prosecution would allege Crawford caused his wife’s death either by strangulation or blunt-force trauma.
The jury will be taken to the site of the alleged murder on Wednesday.
Crowley told the jury the viewing would help them to understand the evidence.
The trial is expected to hear from up to 47 witnesses over the next three weeks.
Crawford’s defence barrister has not yet delivered an opening statement.

'Serbia is free': scenes of celebration in Belgrade as Vučić concedes Serbian election – video
Serbian leader Aleksandar Vučić ousted by student movement in seismic election
Vučić’s 12-year reign looks to have been ended by record turnout of voters in election provoked by student-led protests
The long-serving leader of Serbia , Aleksandar Vučić, has conceded defeat, ushering in a seismic political shift after a historic election that saw ​the increasingly authoritarian leader pitted against a student-led movement that had spent two years protesting against his government.
Vučić pointed to preliminary results that suggested voters had overwhelmingly backed the students, potentially giving them a majority in the country’s 250-seat parliament.
“We still do not have final ⁠results,” Vučić told supporters at his party headquarters in Belgrade. “On the basis of a sample … it is clear ⁠that we have lost these ​elections, the Student ‌List has ‌won, they will have between 135 ‌and 140 seats and we will have 105.”
Ilija Srdanović, first on the electoral list ‘Student List – Students Win’, celebrates after the first results of the parliamentary election in Belgrade. Photograph: Đorđe Kojadinović/Reuters A representative sample by the ​CeSID-IPSPS pollsters suggested that the ​Student List’s candidate list will have ​138 deputies, while Vučić’s rightwing Serbian Progressive party (SNS) garnered 104 seats. The remaining seats ‌were likely to be shared ​between ethnic ​national minority parties, said the pollsters.
As news broke that Vučić’s 12 years in power had come to an end, celebrations erupted on the streets of Belgrade and other cities.
In the capital, Ivana, 43, was ecstatic. “This feels like New Year’s Eve to me, like three New Year’s Eves all at once, really,” she said. “We can finally breathe again.” She added that she was hoping for swift accountability for top members of the ousted regime, a key campaign pledge from the Student List.
A person celebrating the election results hugs a police officer as the result of the Serbian election prompts street celebrations in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Ana Milosavljevic/The Guardian Others offered handshakes, high fives and hugs to police officers in the streets, while a chorus of honking horns rang out from passing cars.
At the headquarters of the Student List, supporters chanted “Victory, victory!” as they applauded, cried and traded hugs. “Dear Serbia, tonight the voice of the people is being heard,” said Radomir Saičić, a professor at Belgrade University, speaking on behalf of the students at their headquarters. “Tonight we celebrate victory, tomorrow we build the future.”
The scenes of joy came hours after a record number of voters turned up to cast their ballot on Sunday. Across the country, long lines snaked out of polling stations, with some reporting wait times of up to two hours. By 7pm local time pollsters and monitors CeSID and Ipsos put the turnout at 67.6%, the highest since the breakup of Yugoslavia in 1992.
The high turnout, along with the estimated 150,000 people who flocked to the students’ final pre-election rally last week in Belgrade, fuelled early speculation that the students may have rallied enough support to launch a new political era in the Balkan country.
Serbian voters around the world queue to cast ballots in snap parliamentary election
In the lead-up to the vote, opinion polls had varied wildly, with analysts pointing to the high degree of uncertainty at play as the candidates representing the grassroots student movement went up against Vučić’s SNS.
The results were watched across the world as part of 10 pivotal days of elections across the globe in which the authoritarian right is facing off against pro-democracy forces. The victory for the student-led movement in Serbia is also likely to reverberate across Europe, adding momentum to the youth and student protests that have rocked cities across the continent.
A man, carrying his dog, leaves a polling station after casting his vote as people queue to vote during the parliamentary election in Belgrade. Photograph: Marko Đurica/Reuters As people waited for the election results to come in, speculation had begun to swirl online about whether Vučić would seek to hold on to power regardless. His swift admission of defeat, however, was reminiscent of his ally, Viktor Orbán , in neighbouring Hungary six months ago, fuelling hopes of a democratic resurgence to central Europe.
The high-stakes election in the EU-candidate country traces back to November 2024, when a concrete canopy collapsed at the Novi Sad railway station, killing 16 people. The tragedy set off protests among students, snowballing into a broad anti-government movement as evidence suggested that slipshod construction, fraud and corruption were to blame for the roof collapse.
As Vučić, the increasingly authoritarian leader who has dominated Serbian politics for more than a decade, grappled with mounting pressure from the students, he resigned the presidency last month to gamble on snap elections.
People in Belgrade celebrate Aleksandar Vučić’s defeat at the polls. Photograph: Armin Durgut/AP The students seized on the chance to transform the movement into political power. They announced a slate of 250 candidates for the 250-seat parliament, ranging from academics to athletes and farmers, as they called on voters to back their push to rebuild Serbia’s economy and restore its democracy.
The campaign pitted them against the political monolith of Vučić’s SNS party, leaving them in an uphill battle against a ruling party that had taken over almost all of the broadcast channels and which wielded ministries and other state institutions as political tools.
Vučić had repeatedly sought to undermine the students by accusing them of being stooges of foreign powers, a charge that the students have dismissed. As he pledged to increase pensions and public sector salaries, Vučić sought to present himself as a leader capable of walking the diplomatic tightrope between the country’s ties with the west, and Russia and China to steer Serbia through global uncertainties.
Undaunted, the students took to the streets, staging rallies, turning up in the rural areas that had long been Vučić strongholds and going door-to-door as they sought to pitch themselves as the anti-establishment candidates and to rally voters behind their slogan of “Serbia or Mafia?”
Former Serbian basketball player Dejan Bodiroga, a candidate for the Student List, celebrates after Aleksandar Vučić conceded defeat in Belgrade, Serbia. Photograph: Petr David Josek/AP The message was reiterated on Sunday as Dejan Bodiroga, a candidate on the Student List and the Euroleague Basketball president, cast his ballot in Belgrade.
“Today, above all, Serbia is fighting for freedom and for the return of a country that has been hijacked,” Bodiroga told reporters. “Serbia is voting today to decide which direction it will take – towards the future or the past.”
In the lead-up to Sunday’s vote, both international and local election monitors expressed concerns about detentions and reports of surveillance and cyber-attacks against activists.
On Sunday, independent monitoring groups warned of recorded cases of photographing of ballots that suggested pressure on voters, attempted vote-buying and carousel voting.
Among them was Raša Nedeljkov of the election monitory CRTA, who said that “a large number of irregularities continue to be recorded, while violent incidents outside polling stations are also becoming increasingly frequent”.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

An Israeli checkpoint in the flattened area of Farsh al-Hawa, an entrance to Hebron, where two Palestinian gunmen killed two Israeli soldiers in a shooting last Friday. Photograph: Quique Kierszenbaum/The Guardian
An Israeli checkpoint in the flattened area of Farsh al-Hawa, an entrance to Hebron, where two Palestinian gunmen killed two Israeli soldiers in a shooting last Friday. Photograph: Quique Kierszenbaum/The Guardian
‘It’s collective punishment’: nearly 2,000 Palestinians in Hebron displaced as Israeli forces take over homes
Families forced to flee as Netanyahu tries to prove security credentials after two Israeli soldiers killed days before election
H ebron, the biggest city in the West Bank, had paid little attention to Israel’s looming elections until it found itself suddenly in the midst of them, a proving ground for Benjamin Netanyahu’s security credentials in the run-up to Tuesday’s vote.
A gun attack on an Israeli military checkpoint on Friday in which two soldiers were killed brought immediate televised visits from Netanyahu and his finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich, whose ruling coalition is narrowly trailing with two days of campaigning left.
They vowed abrupt and sweeping retribution, and the prime minister warned of imminent military operations on a scale the West Bank has not seen in nearly 60 years of occupation.
Within hours, the whole city of 250,000 people had been sealed off and the neighbourhood of Farsh al-Hawa, closest to the site of Friday’s attack, had been flattened. By Sunday morning, there was nothing left but rubble of an industrial area that had been full of car workshops, a big ceramics plant and other enterprises.
Map of the West Bank, marking the Palestinian communities As the bulldozers were at work there, another district a few kilometres away was being systematically emptied. Ein Deir Baha was allegedly home to at least one of the two men involved in the attack (both of whom were killed on the spot), and on Friday its residents paid the price.
The imam from the local mosque, Fahed Ahmaro, was woken at about midnight by the loudspeakers from military vehicles patrolling the street, telling homeowners to vacate their homes immediately for their own safety.
Fahed Ahmaro, who was forced to flee his home in the Deir Baha area of Hebron by Israeli security forces, looks at his house from a distance. Photograph: Quique Kierszenbaum/The Guardian Ahmaro had 30 members of his family living in his house. He rushed his frail mother and some of his grandchildren to his daughter’s house in another part of Hebron, and was on his way back in the hope of picking up some of his possessions when his neighbour phoned him.
“He told me not to come back, and the army had blown up the gates to the house. I don’t know what they have done to it by now. I heard they destroyed the furniture,” Ahmaro said. “You don’t know what you have until you lose it. Now I realise how devastating it is to lose your home.”
Fahed Ahmaro’s house in Hebron, now a military position, with netting over its upper floors Photograph: Quique Kierszenbaum/The Guardian At midday on Sunday, he was in a Hebron market shopping for a change of clothes for his grandchildren, uncertain how long the family’s exile would last.
He had heard that the house was being used as a base by Israeli soldiers and looking up at the house, perched on the side of a steep hill, he could see some lights on but no sign of life.
The Hebron municipality said that 370 families, nearly 2,000 people, had been displaced by the army sweep through Ein Deir Baha. In its official comments, the Israeli army had said that the displaced population had left voluntarily, that “civilians who wished to evacuate were permitted to temporarily leave the area”.
Ahmaro was outraged at the suggestion that he had any choice about fleeing.
“Never, never, never. Why should I leave my house?” he asked.
Abdul Halim al-Tamimi tried to stay put when the military came at 1.30am. The 77-year-old was told his family had only 10 minutes to vacate their home.
“An intelligence officer told me if we didn’t go, they would displace us the same way they have done it in Gaza,” he said. “We stood there in front of the house, and we refused to leave until we were faced by three military vehicles and they forced us out.”
By the time the Tamimi family left, it was 3am on Saturday morning. On Sunday morning their home was festooned with large Israeli flags and ringed with military vehicles. The army had turned the substantial villa into its headquarters.
In its statement, the Israeli military claimed that 2,000 local people were from the Jaba’a clan, from which the gunmen were allegedly drawn, and which the army claimed was “associated with Hamas”.
The claim drew consternation and derision from people in Ein Deir Baha. They numbered the extent of the Jaba’a clan at 150 at most, with no known reputation for radicalism.
“We are from a completely different family. We are innocent people. We have nothing to do with the whole thing,” Tamimi said. “I don’t know what they are using it for, other than maybe election publicity.”
Palestinians in the occupied West Bank have no vote in Tuesday’s elections, but their lives could be deeply affected by the outcome.
Wasam Sadi, a Palestinian driver, has difficulties with his vision after he was attacked in his truck by Israeli settlers near the entrance of Hebron. Photograph: Quique Kierszenbaum/The Guardian By Sunday evening, all roads in and out of the city remained closed to cars, with heavy steel barriers swung across them. The only traffic was on foot, with taxi drivers squabbling for fares. The driver of a delivery truck, Wasam Sadi, pulled in at one checkpoint with the cab windshield smashed and glass fragments in his eyes. He had been attacked by settlers at a nearby junction, he said.
“It’s collective punishment,” said Hebron’s mayor, Yousef al-Jabari. “They have dragged people from their houses, and they have prevented the media from getting into the district in order to cover up what is happening.
“Netanyahu and his gang are using Palestinian land to displace Palestinian people, closing the city, closing the mosque, in order to achieve their political goals.”

Lana Nusseibeh has Palestine’s endorsement but faces criticism over UAE’s record on human rights and Sudan
An Emirati diplomat who as UAE ambassador to the UN helped secure the delivery of humanitarian aid to Gaza has entered the race as a late contender to succeed António Guterres as the world body’s secretary general.
Lana Nusseibeh, who is of Palestinian heritage, is widely noted for her advocacy of the Palestinian cause and the two-state solution and has won the endorsement of the state of Palestine .
She is however facing criticism over the UAE’s contested role in arming the Rapid Support Forces in Sudan, a group that has been accused of genocide by two successive US administrations.
Nusseibeh, 48, has been one of the leading UAE diplomats denying reports that the UAE supplied weapons to RSF leader Mohamed Hamdan “Hemedti” Dagalo in defiance of a UN arms sales ban.
A grouping of Latin American and Caribbean states, CELAC, has also sent a letter of objection to the security council, claiming her late entry came just as a consensus was emerging behind their region holding the UN top job.
Fears mount that next UN chief will downgrade climate action
Nusseibeh, who was educated at Cambridge University, was born in 1978 in Abu Dhabi, where her father, Zaki, had emigrated after the Arab-Israeli 1967 war made it difficult for him to return to his birthplace, Jerusalem.
She is the niece of Sari Nusseibeh, a distinguished Palestinian intellectual and author of a widely admired book on Palestine, Once Upon a Country.
She has justified her late – but widely predicted – entry into the race on the grounds that the UN security council appears to be close to deadlock on the six remaining candidates in the race, with no one as yet able to establish a clear lead after six rounds of voting, the latest on 16 October.
The latest straw poll – the first in which it was revealed whether any of the five permanent members of the 15-strong UN security council objected to a candidate – ended with Rebeca Grynspan of Costa Rica holding the lead spot.
But Grynspan faced objections from two unidentified permanent security council members, making it harder for her to claim she is a breakaway consensus candidate. She has support from nine security council members and objections from five. Guterres is due to stand down at the end of the year
Other candidates include: the Argentine diplomat Rafael Grossi; the UN envoy from Guyana, Carolyn Rodrigues-Birkett; Olara Otunnu, a distinguished veteran Ugandan diplomat; and Ecuador’s former foreign minister María Fernanda Espinosa. Two previously declared candidates have pulled out.
In her vision statement, Nusseibeh said: “At the heart of the UN sits a wager: that states will give up a little today to gain a great deal together over time.
“States have not stopped needing to partner with one another, but they no longer trust that cooperation will yield sufficient returns. Rebuilding that trust, with faster, more certain, and more realistic results, is the work of the UN.”
Brett McGurk, who served in the Biden administration as a top national security adviser, backed Nusseibeh for her work on Gaza .
“How did UAE become the largest global provider of humanitarian aid to Gaza, according to UN data? Because of Lana Nussaibeh – a diplomat who knows everyone and works with them … she would be an inspired choice for this impossible job,” he said.
Cuts force World Food Programme to end support for half a million Palestinians in Gaza
Nickolay Mladenov, the Bulgarian diplomat who heads Donald Trump’s Gaza Board of Peace, said Nusseibeh “has always understood that the UN works when someone is willing to systematically build the bridges nobody else wants to build”.
He added: “In the Security Council [as UAE representative] she did that quietly and consistently and with great professionalism. She now has the chance to take that same spirit and ethos to the UN secretariat.”
But there is a fierce backlash from human rights activists who point to the UAE’s domestic human rights record and its alleged funding of the RSF in Sudan, the group responsible for killing thousands, including in the capture of El Fasher in October last year.
A January 2024 report by the UN panel of experts described allegations from sources in Chad and Darfur – including those of arms being smuggled into the RSF from the UAE – as credible.
One of the strongest attacks on Nusseibeh’s candidacy has been mounted by Kenneth Roth, the former executive director of Human Rights Watch, over her denials of UAE provision of any “military, logistical, financial or political support to any faction in Sudan”. The UAE says it supports the formation of a civilian-led government in Sudan.

### 54. ★★☆☆☆ Higher training costs for older workers risks plan to tackle youth jobs crisis, employers say

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T06:00:24+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Exclusive: Ministers planning overhaul of apprenticeship funding to increase cost of over-25s training, Guardian understandsBritain’s largest employers have told the government that forcing businesses to pay more to train older workers risks damaging its goal to tackle the youth jobs crisis.The Guardian understands ministers plan to overhaul Labour’s appre

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The chancellor is expected to announce measures to bolster opportunities for young people in this week’s budget. Photograph: Izusek/Getty Images
The chancellor is expected to announce measures to bolster opportunities for young people in this week’s budget. Photograph: Izusek/Getty Images
Higher training costs for older workers risks plan to tackle youth jobs crisis, employers say
Exclusive: Ministers planning overhaul of apprenticeship funding to increase cost of over-25s training, Guardian understands
Britain’s largest employers have told the government that forcing businesses to pay more to train older workers risks damaging its goal to tackle the youth jobs crisis.
The Guardian understands ministers plan to overhaul Labour’s apprenticeship funding scheme to increase the cost of training a worker over the age of 25 in England by 25%, to encourage firms to train young people instead.
Drawn up before Wednesday’s budget, the proposal, which was first reported last week, is expected to form part of the government response to Alan Milburn’s review into why so many young people are unemployed.
The chancellor, John Healey , is widely tipped to announce measures to bolster opportunities for young people to help the 1 million 16- to 24-year-olds not in education, employment or training (Neet) across the country.
However, business leaders urged the government to reconsider the plan to make training older workers more expensive.
“It will just mean opportunities are shifted around instead of creating new ones,” said Robert Halfon, a former Conservative skills minister who is now the executive director of policy at the manufacturing lobby group Make UK.
“We agree that getting young people back into employment and training is a moral and economic imperative. But making it more expensive for employers to train older workers isn’t the answer,” he added.
Calling on Healey to “break the cycle” of cutting and reallocating the apprenticeship budget in England, Halfon said the system was already struggling to deliver what businesses and young people need.
“If the government is serious about getting young people into good jobs and giving industry the skills it needs, it should use that opportunity to grow the number of apprenticeships available rather than restrict training elsewhere.”
Andy Burnham has placed youth jobs at the heart of his agenda, including sweeping changes to retool technical education and plans to reverse a 40% drop in youth apprenticeship starts over the past decade.
With over-25s accounting for more than half of new apprenticeship enrolments, ministers have been clear they want to rebalance the system towards supporting more younger people.
Under the latest proposal, which is yet to be finalised, employers would be able to fund the full cost of an apprentice under the age of 25 using the money they contribute to the growth and skills levy – the £4bn-a-year tax used to fund the national apprenticeship system.
Andy Burnham has placed youth jobs at the heart of his agenda, including plans to reverse the drop in apprenticeships. Photograph: Simon Dawson/No 10 Downing Street However, for an older worker, they would face a “co-investment rule” whereby levy funds would cover 75% of the costs. The rest would need to be covered separately.
Employers have for years been told they were free to spend their levy funds on training as they saw fit.
John Foster, the chief policy and campaigns officer at the CBI lobby group, said: “We can’t make skills a zero-sum game.
“While it is right to focus on supporting young people who are Neet into work, adult training also has a vital role to play in opening up entry-level opportunities and helping people adapt and update their skills mid-career, particularly as AI reshapes the workplace.”
Ben Rowland, the chief executive of the Association of Employment and Learning Providers, said: “The key to any further changes to the apprenticeship system is to bring employers on the journey. Anything that is too abrupt or too quick significantly endangers that.”
Business leaders have been pressing Healey to use Wednesday’s budget to provide support with soaring costs amid the war in the Middle East, and have urged the chancellor not to add to the pressure on firms after Labour’s increase in employer national insurance contributions .
Kate Shoesmith, the director of policy and insights at the British Chambers of Commerce, said: “At a time when businesses are under pressure to improve productivity and drive growth, employers need greater flexibility and support to invest in skills at every stage of a person’s career.
“The best way to encourage more youth apprenticeships is to make it easier and more affordable for firms to take on young people by reducing the wider costs of doing business, rather than creating new barriers to training.”
Labour has been told its youth skills strategy risks failure without reform, after just 160 young people were enrolled into its foundation apprenticeship scheme in the first eight months. Ministers have an ambition for 30,000 starts over the current parliament.
Joe Dromey, the general secretary of the Fabian Society, said that while flexibility was important for business, the current system was skewed too heavily towards funding older, often already highly qualified workers.
“These will not be easy decisions, and some will be unpopular with businesses. But tackling youth unemployment means making difficult choices about where public funding is spent.”
A government spokesperson said: “It’s right that we focus on reversing the 40% decline in the number of young people starting an apprenticeship and give them the skills they need to succeed.
“That’s why we have reformed the system backed by £3.3bn to deliver 50,000 additional apprenticeships for young people and support employers with a new hiring payment of £2,000 for SMEs who take on a 16- to 24-year-old apprentice.
“The Milburn Review is due in the autumn. We will not pre-empt its findings.”

### 55. ★★☆☆☆ Can GOP Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick, a moderate who's bucked Trump, keep his Pennsylvania swing seat?

- **分类**: 🇺🇸 美国
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T10:00:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: GOP Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick has consistently won his Pennsylvania swing seat, but Democrats say this election is an opportunity to oust the veteran lawmaker.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Trump frustration threatens Fitzpatrick&#x27;s once-safe Pennsylvania seat
GOP Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick has consistently outperformed his party in Pennsylvania&#x27;s swing 1st congressional district, but amid frustration with President Donald Trump, Democrats see an opportunity to win the seat that has long eluded them.
The southeastern Pennsylvania district was seen as a safe Republican seat in 2026, but sentiment has shifted toward Democrats in recent weeks and is attracting spending from both parties in the waning weeks before the Nov. 3 election.
Fitzpatrick is among the most moderate House Republicans, but Bob Harvie, his Democratic challenger, is attempting to cast him as a rubber stamp for Trump.
Bob Harvie, Democratic congressional candidate (L), and Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick R-Pa.
Over nearly 10 years in Congress , Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick has cultivated an image as a middle-of-the-road GOP lawmaker who operates above the partisan fray and is willing to buck President Donald Trump .
It&#x27;s a formula that has allowed Fitzpatrick, who is among the most moderate lawmakers on Capitol Hill according to his hometown newspaper, to win swing voters and consistently outperform his party in a highly competitive district that opted for Joe Biden in 2020 and Kamala Harris in 2024. But in a year when Republican candidates across the country are being weighed down by Trump, Fitzpatrick is facing a stiff challenge from Democrat Bob Harvie in Pennsylvania&#x27;s 1st Congressional District, and testing whether his personal brand can carry him in a difficult national political environment.
"Brian Fitzpatrick is Brian Fitzpatrick. I&#x27;m not Donald Trump. I&#x27;m not anybody else. And that&#x27;s the way voters should approach this," Fitzpatrick told CNBC. "I&#x27;m not happy with [Trump]. I&#x27;m not happy with his tone. I don&#x27;t think he&#x27;s done a sufficient job. I think leaders are supposed to unite our country, unify people. He has not done that."
The battle between Fitzpatrick and Harvie comes as Trump&#x27;s approval rating craters , the Iran war drags on and consumer prices rise . The contest is one of the approximately four dozen House seats considered in play in the Nov. 3 election that will decide control of Congress for the last two years of Trump&#x27;s term. Pennsylvania voters are often an indicator of how the country will swing overall, and because the state is on the East Coast, Fitzpatrick&#x27;s district is one to watch as returns come in.
Trump must sit for deposition in BBC lawsuit by Dec. 11, judge orders
Virginia state Sen. Lucas, a Trump critic, charged in marijuana racketeering case
California&#x27;s Proposition 40: What voters need to know about billionaire wealth tax battle
Democrats are on offense and spending money in districts previously thought out of reach — including Fitzpatrick&#x27;s, in a race that could signal Democratic success outside of just the most vulnerable seats.
But Harvie, a former high school social studies teacher and county commissioner, is still a long shot to win. The Cook Political Report with Amy Walter shifted the race in Democrats&#x27; direction, from "Likely R" to "Lean R," earlier this month. Erin Covey , who leads coverage of House races at the Cook Report, called Fitzpatrick Democrats&#x27; "white whale."
As part of Democrats&#x27; final push to unseat Fitzpatrick, Harvie was joined earlier this month by House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries , D-N.Y., and Democratic Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro for a rally in Bucks County .
"This is an incredibly important state," Jeffries told reporters after the rally. "The Commonwealth is a big swing state, and Bucks County is a big swing county. And so this is a critically important race."
Fitzpatrick grew up in Bucks County, which makes up much of the district, and is a former FBI agent who succeeded his brother, Michael, in 2017. He&#x27;s a co-chair of the bipartisan Problem Solvers Caucus and, according to a recent Philadelphia Inquirer analysis of his voting record, has voted against the White House and his party this Congress more than any other House Republican. The paper endorsed Harvie earlier this month.
Fitzpatrick is also part of a bipartisan group that&#x27;s been outspoken as the White House has taken a more conciliatory tone toward Russia in recent weeks. He led a bill last week that would undo Trump&#x27;s recent authorization of the U.S. purchase of Russian diesel . And he signed a bipartisan letter urging the Department of Defense to restore Ukraine&#x27;s access to satellite communications that are critical to the country&#x27;s defenses, after President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said the services were downgraded. Fitzpatrick threatened to pursue the firing of U.S. officials responsible for the slowdown, the New York Times reported .
Fitzpatrick is seeking his sixth House term and for much of his career has easily warded off Democratic challenges. Christopher Nicholas, a Republican consultant based in Pennsylvania, said Fitzpatrick "has been the most moderate member of the House."
"If they are able to beat him this cycle, it is definitely going to be a great night for Democrats," Covey said.
Harvie casts Fitzpatrick as a rubber stamp for Trump and questions the claims of bipartisanship. While Fitzpatrick was one of only two Republicans to vote against the final version of Republicans&#x27; 2025 signature tax and spending package, he voted in favor of an earlier iteration in the House that sent the measure to the Senate and set it on a path to passage. Fitzpatrick has said he supported the initial draft of the bill, but opposed cuts to Medicaid that were added in the Senate.
Harvie also called out Fitzpatrick&#x27;s mixed record on tariffs. Fitzpatrick voted to overturn Trump&#x27;s tariffs on Canada , but has also sided with most Republicans on procedural votes to limit Congress&#x27; ability to challenge some of the president&#x27;s tariffs. Fitzpatrick voted in favor of a controversial, Trump-backed election bill, dubbed the SAVE America Act , which would require identification to cast a ballot and proof of citizenship to register to vote. And he backed a controversial GOP immigration proposal and Trump&#x27;s bid to rename the Gulf of Mexico the "Gulf of America."
"When it comes to our economics in this country, people are suffering," Harvie, a Bucks County native, told CNBC. "The government is one that is totally detached from the people it&#x27;s supposed to serve. Internationally, we&#x27;ve never been weaker and more isolated … and all of that is because of Donald Trump and because of people like Brian Fitzpatrick, who are just letting him run amok."
Fitzpatrick is still favored to win. But Brit Crampsie, a Democratic strategist based in Pennsylvania, said Democrats have a stronger candidate in Harvie than they have in past cycles.
"Bob Harvie, from his time as a school teacher to a local official, has deep ties to this community, and he&#x27;s very representative of this community," Crampsie said. "He understands Bucks County and has high name ID."
In addition to Trump&#x27;s low favorability and voters&#x27; economic concerns, Fitzpatrick and other Pennsylvania Republicans must also contend with one of the most popular governors in the country at the top of the ticket. Shapiro is expected to win reelection easily, according to recent polling . In Fitzpatrick&#x27;s district, Shapiro is predicted to coast to victory by double digits, Covey said.
Pennsylvania also has three other neck-and-neck races that together could decide control of the House. Two Republican incumbents, Reps. Ryan Mackenzie in the 7th District and Scott Perry in the 10th, are seeking reelection in races labeled "Lean D." Republican Rep. Rob Bresnahan, in Pennsylvania&#x27;s 8th District, is in a "toss up" race, according to the Cook Report.
Fitzpatrick, however, noted he won nearly 55% of voters in his district in 2022, even as Shapiro won 59% of the vote in the district, evidence to him that his constituents are more than willing to split their tickets.
Independent public polling on the race for the district is sparse, though a recent internal survey conducted by Global Strategy Group for the Harvie campaign showed him and Fitzpatrick tied. A September poll conducted by Public Opinion Strategies for the National Republican Congressional Committee and Fitzpatrick&#x27;s campaign had the incumbent up by nine percentage points. A poll released Sunday by the New York Times, Philadelphia Inquirer and Siena University found Harvie with a narrow 48% to 46% lead over Fitzpatrick — within the poll&#x27;s margin of error.
Recent outside spending paints a competitive picture.
The Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee has committed more than $1.5 million to TV ads in the district since the start of October, while House Democrats&#x27; main super PAC has pitched in more than $7.5 million, according to AdImpact data. Prior to this election, neither group had bought TV ads in the district since 2020 .
Republicans are also shelling out to shore up Fitzpatrick. The House GOP&#x27;s main super PAC, the Congressional Leadership Fund, and the NRCC have committed just under $5 million and just over $1 million, respectively, to ads in the district, according to AdImpact. And Trump&#x27;s super PAC, MAGA Inc., recently purchased $100,000 in ads in the district opposing Harvie, according to an Oct. 12 FEC filing .
"Here&#x27;s how you know where his loyalties lie," Pennsylvania Democratic Party Chair Eugene DePasquale told CNBC. "Donald Trump and MAGA Inc. coming in to spend $100,000 to bail him out because they know when push comes to shove, he&#x27;ll be loyal to them."
Fitzpatrick has a significant cash advantage, with more than $709,000 in his war chest as of Oct. 14 according to FEC filings , compared with Harvie&#x27;s just over $409,000 in cash on hand .
Reilly Richardson, a spokesperson for the NRCC, pointed to this fundraising disparity, saying Harvie had "a stagnant campaign" and "a significant cash disadvantage."

### 56. ★★☆☆☆ What Democrats and Republicans fear most about each other: CNBC survey has the answers

- **分类**: 🇺🇸 美国
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T12:00:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The economy is still a key issue in the 2026 election, but voters are concerned about Trump testing the bounds of the office and Democratic Socialist polices.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Voters fear DSA policies in Congress after midterm election
Voters are most concerned about the implementation of policies associated with the Democratic Socialists of America if Democrats control Congress and about President Donald Trump testing the bounds of his office if Republicans retain the House and Senate, according to a CNBC survey.
The economy is still a key issue, but 1,000 voters who participated expressed concerns about corruption, progressive policies and Trump&#x27;s behavior.
The results come with less than two weeks to go before the Nov. 3 midterm election, with Democrats largely favored to win control of the House and with a narrow edge in the Senate.
Top 5 takeaways from CNBC&#x27;s All America Economic Survey
If Democrats take over Congress , Republican voters are worried they&#x27;ll move too far to the left and enact socialist policies. If Republicans keep control, Democrats fret about President Donald Trump having unchecked power.
Those are the findings from a new CNBC poll that shows what each side of the political divide fears about the other as the midterm election looms. Republicans in the survey also say they worry about weakened border security and higher taxes under a Democratic Congress. Democrats say they&#x27;re concerned about continued Immigration and Customs Enforcement deportations and ongoing military efforts in Iran under a Republican one.
"That totally shows the polarization … and the fear that one side is going to completely undo the agenda of the other," Ron Bonjean , a Republican strategist and co-founder of ROKK Solutions, said of the results. "What Republicans are worried about is that Democrats are going to go back to the way it was during the Biden administration."
The results of the latest CNBC All-America Economic Survey — conducted between Oct. 14 and 18 and including 1,000 registered voters — could serve as a warning for both parties after the election. Both Democrats and Republicans have struggled with low favorability amid widespread distrust of the government .
If Democrats lurch too far to the left and adopt Democratic Socialists of America policies, they could squander the gains some forecasts project they could make in many heavily Republican parts of the country, said Lucas Holtz, a senior political advisor at Third Way , a center-left think tank.
"They should not misread their mandate if they hope to retain the inroads that they&#x27;re making this cycle with Latino voters, with working-class voters, who they have been hemorrhaging cycle after cycle," Holtz said.
And Republicans should understand that voters in this moment want to change course, Bonjean said.
"Voters absolutely want change from the way things are going now, but they also don&#x27;t want to go back to the way things were when Democrats ran Washington," Bonjean said. "The branding is so bad on the Democratic side, and it&#x27;s not great on the Republican side."
Respondents were able to select up to two of their biggest concerns in the event that Democrats or Republicans control Congress, which don&#x27;t necessarily indicate their top electoral priorities. The CNBC survey by Hart Research Associates and Public Opinion Strategies separately asked people to name the most important issues facing the country. In answering that question, affordability issues ranked highly across the political spectrum, while Democrats and independents also listed government corruption as a top priority , and Republicans listed immigration and border security.
Still, the concerns expressed by voters about what the opposing party might do in office reflect the messages in the political advertising that is blanketing much of the country in the final days before the midterm election, according to Alex Jacquez , senior vice president of policy, advocacy, and research at Groundwork Collaborative, a progressive think tank. Democrats on the campaign trail have hammered Trump for his alleged corruption and Republicans have attempted to paint Democrats as overly progressive.
"For Democrats, the main attack ads [targeting them] have ... been about DSA and they&#x27;ve been about the cultural issues that Republicans are trying to hit to tie them to a kind of a far-left or radical movement," Jacquez said. "But the overall vibe of the election is still first and foremost about the economy, people tend to blame the president for prices and Congress for enabling him."
Trump’s approval rating on economy hits all-time low as midterm looms
Corruption is voters’ top issue ahead of midterm election
Trump is pushing a massive AI buildout. His own voters are wary of what comes with it
America by the numbers: How voters feel about the economy, inflation and Trump
If Democrats gain control of Congress, 22% of all voters said they are concerned about them failing to bring down prices, including 31% of Democrats, 22% of independents and 15% of Republicans. In the event the GOP retains the gavels in the House and Senate, 24% of voters said they worry whether prices will come down, including 32% of Republicans, 22% of independents and 17% of Democrats.
But those worries trail fears about checking Trump and policies associated with the DSA, a political organization that has notched recent wins in places like New York City , Washington and Denver and advocates progressive social and populist economic policies.
Of all voters polled, 44% said they fear Trump would be allowed to operate beyond his constitutional power, including 74% of Democrats and 45% of independents. Twenty-eight percent of respondents said they are concerned with the implementation of DSA policies, including 54% of Republicans and 22% of independents.

SoftBank reportedly plans $50 billion tech investment in Malaysia
Malaysian Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim said Son had identified locations in Sarawak and peninsular Malaysia for the planned investments.
SoftBank signed a framework agreement earlier this month to explore AI and digital infrastructure development in Sarawak.
Malaysia is seeking to become an "AI nation" by 2030 as it attracts major data center investments from global technology companies.
Masayoshi Son, chairman and chief executive officer of SoftBank Group Corp., speaks during the Future Investment Initiative (FII) Institute Priority Asia conference in Tokyo, Japan, on Monday, Dec. 1, 2025.
SoftBank Group plans to make $50 billion of investments in Malaysia, Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim said, according to a Bloomberg report.
Anwar said Saturday that Son had identified locations in Kuching, Sarawak, and peninsular Malaysia for the planned investments following a video conference between the two earlier in the week, the report said .
The country&#x27;s prime minister said the planned investments would focus on artificial intelligence, new technologies and data centers, according to the report.
The planned investment comes as Malaysia seeks to become an "AI nation " by 2030, with the government launching a national AI action plan earlier this year to strengthen the country&#x27;s AI capabilities and infrastructure.
Earlier this month, SoftBank signed a framework agreement with Singapore-based Grab and Malaysia&#x27;s Petroleum Sarawak Berhad to explore AI and digital infrastructure development in Sarawak, including AI computing services and advanced technologies.
SoftBank is not alone in pursuing technology investments in Malaysia. Google announced a $2 billion investment in 2024 to build its first data center and cloud region in the country, while Amazon Web Services said it planned to invest $6.2 billion through 2038. Microsoft also announced a $2.2 billion investment in cloud and AI infrastructure and other digital initiatives.
The country recorded 385.7 billion ringgit ($94 billion) in data center-related investments between 2021 and the first half of 2026, according to the Malaysian Investment Development Authority.
SoftBank did not immediately respond to CNBC&#x27;s request for comment.

- **多源确认**: 1

- **发生了什么**: Protests over inequality and living costs drew an estimated 410,000 people across France, with clashes in several cities for a second straight weekend.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

In pictures: Protests over inequality, rising living costs turn violent across France
France’s Interior Ministry said 410,000 people protested nationwide Saturday, including about 110,000 in Paris; unions estimated nearly twice as many.
Most marches were peaceful, but clashes broke out in Paris, Marseille and several other cities; unrest was less severe than the previous weekend.
The movement began with a protest over conditions at one high school in suburban Paris and grew into nationwide demonstrations.
Protests across France over a worsening cost-of-living crisis and growing inequality descended into violence for a second straight weekend.
France&#x27;s Interior Ministry said 410,000 people took part in protests in several major cities, including Paris.
After rioters set fire to the historic city hall in Limoges and looters smashed supermarkets and luxury boutiques in Lyon last weekend, French authorities greatly stepped up security ahead of Saturday&#x27;s protests.
In Paris on Saturday, riot police fired tear gas and water cannon to disperse a group of around 500 hooded youths who hurled stones at officers, Reuters quoted a security official as saying.
In France&#x27;s second city, Marseille, youths dragged large metal sheets to block a street, set fire to rubbish bins, and hurled rocks and bottles at riot police. The confrontations continued after sunset, and police said looters raided two jewelry shops, according to a Reuters report.
Clashes also flared in Lyon, Bordeaux and Grenoble, while police fired volleys of tear gas at demonstrators in Clermont-Ferrand, Lille, Nantes and Nice, according to the agency.
However, the violence was still less intense than a week ago, when looters and vandals went on the rampage in a number of French cities.
The demonstrations began last month as a group of high school students in suburban Paris lamented poor school conditions, including teacher shortages and unsatisfactory facilities. The demonstrations have since grown and evolved into nationwide protests.
France&#x27;s economic and political woes have sharply raised the cost of government borrowing. The yield on its 10-year bond rose above 5% for the first time since 2002 this week, settling around 5.03% on Friday.
Some investors fear the country&#x27;s debt problems could spread to the rest of the euro zone .

### 59. ★★☆☆☆ I paid off $11,000 of credit card debt in 18 months while still traveling, shopping and living my life

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T14:00:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: I racked up over $11,000 in credit card debt traveling, shopping and dining out. Here's how I paid it all off in under two years.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

How I paid off $11,000 in credit card debt in 18 months
Millennial Money 32-year-old doctor works in the hospital   where she was a teenage janitor
Become Debt-Free This is ‘one of the worst’ credit card   habits—41% of cardholders do it
Save and Invest Why everyday Americans may   choose to work until they die
Life I retired early at 40 and spent the last year following BTS   around the world—&#x27;I feel a sincere sense of gratitude&#x27;
On The Job 38-year-old runs a mobile pizzeria out of his Smart car in   New York City: It&#x27;s &#x27;like a circus or a magic act&#x27;
I made the financially irresponsible decision to go to Coachella in 2024.
Yes, I am a personal finance reporter with over seven years of experience telling readers how to wisely manage their money. Yes, I&#x27;ve also been in credit card debt for basically all of that time.
I have carried a balance on one or more credit cards for nearly all of the 10 years I have had credit cards. Sometimes, especially in the early years when I was still a college student, I did not have the cash on hand to pay it all off. Most of the time, however, I just didn&#x27;t have the discipline.
I was making more than the minimum payment every month, but I also felt like credit card debt was normal so I didn&#x27;t make a concerted effort to fully clear my balance. I assumed when my peers were putting things on credit , they too, weren&#x27;t paying it off right away, though many of them actually were.
I did get my cards paid off for a brief period in 2020 when I put my Covid-19 stimulus checks toward my debt. But then I got a dog, moved into my own apartment and continued traveling and spending at levels I felt were manageable. Soon I was carrying a balance again and that balance gradually grew to a peak of $11,310 in March 2025.
But as of Sept. 18, I am credit card debt-free. Here&#x27;s how I racked up five figures of debt and paid it all off in under two years.
My debt came from a mix of genuine necessities and trivial pursuits. I charged vet bills and supplies for my dog but also flights and hotels for trips whenever I felt like I could afford them, and treated myself to dinner or a new outfit admittedly too often. Instead of intentionally making payments to bring down the balance, I&#x27;d often use so-called " girl math " to justify my continued card usage. I&#x27;d make a mental note of whatever I spent when I swiped my card, then pay back what I thought was more than the total I&#x27;d spent — without actually keeping track or paying much attention to the interest adding up.
For example, in October 2024, I spent $764 on one of my cards on dog-sitting services, a hotel and dining out, and I made payments totaling $838, which seemed like a net positive. But when you factor in the $60 I was charged in interest during that period, it was as if I&#x27;d hardly made a payment at all.
Despite the months I paid the credit card company more than I spent, an interest rate of at least 21% made my balance continue to grow. My strategy was purely vibes-based, just spending when I wanted to then throwing a chunk of money from my paycheck at my balance every other week. I didn&#x27;t really sit down and look at the numbers to see how much I was moving my balance each month — or in which direction it was headed.
The end of 2024 and beginning of 2025 got particularly spend-heavy as I went on several trips and attended my best friend&#x27;s wedding. I got a bit carried away, and my balance surpassed $10,000. Though I checked my account regularly, it didn&#x27;t really occur to me how dangerous it was to carry that much debt . If I&#x27;d lost my job for a period or had some expensive emergency at that time, I could have struggled to keep up with even a minimum payment or meet my other financial obligations.
Thankfully, that didn&#x27;t happen. I could still manage all my regular monthly bills including my rent and student loan payment. I was still contributing to my 401(k) and some emergency savings . Chipping away at my credit card debt felt like just another one of those obligations, not something that was actively holding me back.
I started dating my now-fiancé in early 2025, and he kindly helped me realize how significant this level of debt was, even if it&#x27;s not all that uncommon. Around 45% of credit cardholders have carried a balance at least one month in the last year, according to LendingTree&#x27;s analysis of Federal Reserve data. Americans with any kind of debt have an average credit card balance of $7,756 as of the first quarter of 2026, per LendingTree&#x27;s analysis of over 400,000 of the company&#x27;s users.
I sat down with my budgeting spreadsheet and crunched the numbers. Pulling up my monthly credit card statements, I looked at the net payments I was making on the debt each month and was fairly surprised to see how many months I was actually adding to the balance instead of bringing it down, even slowly.
There are plenty of tactics people use to address credit card debt, from putting their cards in the freezer to committing to " no-spend" periods . I am fortunate enough that I didn&#x27;t have to take any kind of drastic measure when I got serious about paying off my debt.
I did cut back on some spending areas, mainly dining out and shopping for clothes. Living in New York, it&#x27;s easy to drop $100 on dinner or drinks with friends. I said yes to fewer nights out, and when I went out to dinner, I would skip the $15 cocktail, for example. I stopped Ubering home as frequently as I used to, saving another $40 or so when I did go out.
I can be a very impulsive shopper when it comes to clothes, so I got more intentional about pre-selecting items and waiting before buying to make sure I really needed or wanted them. Instead of going to Target and mindlessly adding things to my cart, I&#x27;d make online orders so I only got what I needed. I made a great effort to save for trips ahead of time and paid those charges off right away instead of over several months.
And instead of just throwing big chunks of money at my debt hoping it would exceed whatever I spent in a month, I actually started putting my monthly statement balance in a spreadsheet. This way, I couldn&#x27;t deny the months I added to my balance, despite still making payments. I moved in May 2025, so that month, for example, my balance went up slightly as I paid movers instead of putting extra cash toward my debt.
For the most part, I would use my debit card for purchases, but I didn&#x27;t cut myself off from my credit card altogether.
I took the classic budgeting advice of "paying myself first" to work down my credit card balance while also saving some for emergencies. On my biweekly pay days, I made a payment on my credit card balance and had an automatic contribution to my emergency savings fund. Then I&#x27;d use whatever was left in my checking account for groceries and other necessities. I&#x27;d often make an extra payment the weeks I didn&#x27;t get paid, too, if I had extra cash after those essentials. From October 2025 through September 2026, I&#x27;ve managed to throw nearly $600 a month, on average, at my debt.
That emergency fund proved especially handy in May 2026 when my dog had a very expensive vet visit .
It&#x27;s something I write about often — how it&#x27;s smart to keep emergency savings even while you&#x27;re paying down debt — but actually going through it was a good learning experience. It kind of hurt to have to tap into the cash savings I&#x27;d been really proud of building. But in the end, I was glad I didn&#x27;t just have to add the $6,000 vet bill to my credit card debt. It slowed down my progress a bit that month, but ultimately didn&#x27;t derail me. And now that I&#x27;m not putting $500 toward credit card debt every month, I can focus on rebuilding my savings and other financial goals.
Personal finance columnist Michelle Singletary recently told me "Debt limits your possibilities." I didn&#x27;t really feel that way when I was on a vacation that I charged to my card. But as I recently turned 30, started planning my wedding and thinking about my future, I started feeling like my debt payment is a bill I don&#x27;t need or want to have to pay every month. And I feel lighter without it.

### 60. ★★☆☆☆ Healey to shield low-income pensioners from income tax in the Budget

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-26T10:28:12+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Changes are needed to prevent the full, flat-rate state pension becoming liable for income tax after it rises in April.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

By Joe Pike ,Â  Political correspondent Â  and Â  Richard Wheeler ,Â  Political reporter
Pensioners on low incomes will be shielded from income tax by Chancellor John Healey in his first Budget on Wednesday.
The full, flat-rate state pension is expected to increase by 3.9% to Â£13,036.40 a year from April. This would take the payment above the Â£12,570 personal tax allowance and therefore be liable for income tax if no changes are made.
The government had already committed to not taxing people who only get an income from the state pension. Healey will explain this pledge will be met by plans for a new tax allowance for low-income pensioners.
Healey&#x27;s plans come as the Conservatives and Reform UK unveil their alternative Budget proposals, including deep cuts to the welfare bill.
An insider&#x27;s guide to Budget Day: &#x27;Don&#x27;t fall off the kerb&#x27;
The chancellor&#x27;s allies argue his plans for pensioners will mean they do not need to face an administrative burden just to pay a small amount of tax.
More specific details, including how many pensioners this will affect, have yet to be confirmed by the Treasury.
The Budget is due to be printed this evening, with Healey&#x27;s speech likely to be fine-tuned right up until it is delivered on Wednesday lunchtime.
Treasury sources concede it will not involve the scale of tax and spending announcements seen from Labour and Conservative governments in recent years.
The Tories are calling on the chancellor to cut green taxes, red tape and benefits to bring down energy bills.
Reform is recommending the chancellor adopts 60 policies which they claim will create jobs - including approving the controversial Rosebank oil field - a project Healey is understood to back.
Liberal Democrat Treasury spokeswoman Daisy Cooper has called on the government to agree a new growth and defence partnership with the EU, including joining the single market. She argued this would help "ease the huge tax burden weighing people down".
The Green Party has urged Healey to use his first Budget to "make the UK affordable", including by funding energy bill support by ending a tax relief for data centres.
Senior Green politicians, including leader Zack Polanski, MPs and peers, made clear their suggestions in a letter to Healey.
